ANONYMOUS GIFT

CABARET 

An exciting love story. Behind the scenes of gaiety and splendor in the most fashionable cabaret in Paris there is intrigue, mystery and murder. 

A young girl, in search of her father, is directed there and is caught in the meshes of this hotbed of crime. Unknown to her, this night spot is used as the headquarters for a clever and unscrupulous gang who prey on the women who frequent the club. 

A young Englishman, whose sister is one of the blackmail victims of the gang, attempts to rescue her from their power. 

They meet under circumstances which for a time lead to misunderstandings as to each other's character and motives, but their love is consummated when the complicated background of this notorious spot is fully revealed and the gang exterminated. 

Packed with thrills, the story moves to a stirring, emotional climax.

CABARET 

BY 

L. H. BRENNING

CABARET

Chapter I 

WHEN Marie Delorme bought her ticket for Paris, the man who, unknown to her, had been watching her for several weeks, stood directly behind her at the Victoria Station booking office. 

Something like a half hour had yet to pass before the 

Folkstone-Boulogne Continental Express pulled out. Having secured his ticket, the man walked to the telegraph office and sent a cable to an address in the Champs Elysees. "Girl traveling Paris this morning. I am on same train. Suggest advise Pierre as precaution." 

He signed the telegram "Legrand." Then he lounged on to the platform and found a seat, taking pains to remove himself some distance from where Marie was seated. It was his care, his skill at self-effacement, which had rendered so successful and secret his observation of the girl who traveled alone. So sure was he in this matter of espionage that when the train started he did not trouble to do more than verify the fact that Marie was on board, before settling down to a study of an English daily newspaper-a calm, self-possessed fellow dressed in a black suit with a thin white line in it. When he removed his hat he disclosed very kinky black hair that gleamed with grease above a thin and sallow face lit by large and insolent dark eyes. A straggling black moustache emphasized the dirty pallor of his skin. 

Marie's porter had found her a compartment to herself,

CABARET 

and she had settled down to a comfortable journey when, at the last moment, and just before the train began to move, a young man came bustling in, directed a perspiring porter to leave a heavy suitcase in the corridor, and, thrusting a shilling into the man's hand, gave him just time to jump the train as the whistle went. 

The young man turned, smiled, and mopped his brow with a handkerchief. "Close work," he smiled. "Had a taxi and got jammed in a narrow street near Piccadilly. That was taking a short cut. My own fault. Ought to get up earlier." He sat down with an air of relief, and added: "I say. Am I intruding? 

I'll find another seat somewhere if I am." "Not at all," said Marie. "Anyhow, I think the train is fairly full."

He looked a nice sort of man, she thought, healthy, openairish, but his resolute grey eyes and square-cut jaw suggested that, in a tight corner, he might be a tough customer. "Are you catching the Paris train on the other side?" he asked. "Yes. Are you?" "Train and boat permitting." He glanced at the rack and the seats whereon were two suitcases and a couple of hatboxes. "I thought you might be going farther-Rome or somewhere." "Because I've got lots of luggage? No. I'm going. home...." Marie hesitated as she said the word. "French? You speak English perfectly." ... “I've lived in London nearly all my life-as long as I can remember, in fact."

CABARET 

3 "Have you, though? Like it? Or prefer Paris?" "I don't know Paris. I don't know France at all," laughed Marie. 

He looked at her with some amazement. "Really? You're presenting me with a great opportunity, you know." 

Marie regarded him with mock solemnity. “I had no intention of doing so," she said. "What is it?" "Why-of displaying to a native of France that exhaustive knowledge of Paris at which my English friends scoff."

Marie laughed. “I think I could buy a guide book at the 

Gare du Nord." 

He laughed with her. “Oh, I wasn't going to offer my services. I was going to tell you all about it while we traveled." "I feel crushed," admitted Marie. "But before you start, let me recite what I've already learned. I don't want you to describe the Louvre, or Napoleon's Tomb, or Notre Dame or the Eiffel Tower, or well, the rest of the sights. I've read about them. And the book in which I read it all-and which, I believe, is called 'Seeing Paris Without Tears'also told me there are night dancings in Montmartre and

Montparnasse and on the Champs Elysees, and that of these the best and the finest is the Cabaret Vibart, also on the 

Champs Elysees. Now . . . right away!" ... 

He took out a cigarette case. "Smoke? Those are Virginian under my thumb. The others are Turkish. I wonder why they publish guide books. Completely queered my pitch. 

You know it all. By the way,"-holding a lighted match toward her—“you ought to go to the Cabaret Vibart. It's a marvelous place." "Are you going there?" Marie's eyes were studiously in-

CABARET 

nocent. She expected assent and a hinted invitation; but, instead, she saw his eyes change, harden, and then, very swiftly, smile. "I shall be there," he said softly. "Yes. Most certainly. 

Get father to take you." "I shall, if I can find him," agreed Marie. "Find...? I'm sorry. Have I said anything hurtful?” "No. Only I'm going to Paris to look for my father. 

That's all." "I see." He leaned back and added nothing further, plainly feeling that he should not probe into her private affairs. 

She considered him for a while. Should she tell him that her errand was not exactly the simple one she had intended? 

She would like to tell somebody-to ease her mind of some part of the burden of uneasy speculation which had lain heavily upon it for so long. He had an air of reliabilityas of one used to receiving confidences and respecting them --that encouraged her. She need not confess she was afraid. 

She went on: "As I have said, I have lived in London ever since I could remember anything. Although my father and mother were 

French, I was born in England while they were on holiday --but I shall probably bore you with these petty reminis cences." "You won't-if you find it helpful to tell me." "You guessed that, eh? All right. Father has always lived in Paris. I don't remember my mother at all. Father's a sculptor, a very minor and modest one making a fairish living only. I've lived with a Madame Latiere at a boardinghouse in Bayswater. She's really brought me up. Father used to send a weekly remittance to me. It was quite a good

CABARET 

5 one, and I've managed to save out of it. Hence this firstclass compartment and the hats.” "But I don't understand." "I'm telling you. Father was always popping over—once a month, say. The last time he was across was eight weeks ago. I shouldn't have worried much, but the weekly remittances ceased then; and that was frightfully serious, you know." "Cut off without a shilling! Have you written him?” "Yes. Screeds and screeds and had no reply. You have to know father to appreciate the position properly. If he were an ordinary human being I should have been very worried long ago and probably have asked the police to make inquiries; but he's not ordinary. He calls it being artistic. I call it being careless. He just strolls through lifea little old gentleman with a benign smile and no memory for yesterday and no outlook for tomorrow. He really wants a capable woman about him." "Meaning you." "I think so. I decided that yesterday, in a talk with Madame Latiere. I thought I'd go over and look him up. By the way, out of your vast knowledge of Paris, can you tell me where this road is?"

She showed him the torn-off top of a letter bearing an address. He inspected it and thought for a moment. "Yes. It's off the rue des Petits Champs. That's near the 

Place de l'Opera right in the middle of Paris." 

She put the paper away. "So you have never seen your father's home?" "No. That seems queer, doesn't it?” "Well, it does a little."

CABARET 

Queer! Marie considered bitterly that it was something more than that. How often had she begged her father to allow her to return with him to Paris! Her life in England had been pleasant enough—she had her circle of friends, girls who had been with her at the boarding-school in Surrey, where she was educated; boarders at Madame Latiere's; young men and women she met at tennis and golf. She read, painted, danced. Her existence had been full of engaging occupations. But surely, as she had so often said to her father, a daughter's place was by her father's side. 

Constantin Delorme had always put her off with excuses and objections. He had no convenience for her in his home; his friends, irresponsible bohemian characters like himself, were not the right sort of friends for a girl like Marie; there was no femininity in the atmosphere in which he lived. 

It was much better that Marie should remain-perhaps for only a little while yet-with the capable and motherly Madame Latiere. Some day in the near future, if possible, he would make arrangements so that Marie could join him. 

But not yet. There were other reasons-this was when Marie had pushed the matter too vigorously for his feeble contentions-reasons which he could not divulge, why she should never visit Paris, even for a day or two, before the arrangements he continually promised to make were completed. 

What these reasons were Marie found herself trying to guess-trying, at the same time, to convince herself that they were not terrible ones.

It was the fear that they might be terrible, that her father might be in some dangerous position, that had compelled her, after the long weeks of waiting, to disobey his injunctions and to undertake this journey.

CABARET 

7 "I expect father will be annoyed when he sees me," she said. “He's made Paris a forbidden city for me, but eight weeks is a long time to wait for news of him. Goodness knows what he's done. He's probably got himself ill or into some ridiculous mess and is trying to hammer along by himself without letting me know for fear of worrying me." “I see.” He hesitated a moment. "You might—I hope you won't need help. It's just possible. If I can do anything 

My name's Madden. Peter Madden. And I'll be staying for an indefinite time at the Hotel Chatham in the rue 

Daunou. You could find me there whenever you wished." "Thanks very much. If you receive a message from Marie 

Delorme you'll know it's from me." "Marie Delorme . . ." He repeated it quietly, and swiftly scanned her, admiring the intrepidity of her small alert head with its close crown of blonde hair, its level grey eyes. "I'll remember that. I hope everything will be all right." "I expect it will be," said Marie. “And I'm not going to allow father to live alone any longer. It's absurd, isn't it? It isn't as though he were a determined old roué who had no intention of allowing his young daughter to observe his follies. And even if he were he needs one of the world's good women about him sometimes." "As an antidote?" smiled Peter Madden. "By way of contrast. And now, why are you going to Paris?"

It was some time before he replied, and again Marie saw that slight hardening of the eyes. His reply was curt and final. “Oh—just on business. It's not worth discussing."

CABARET 

Marie was silent. Peter Madden broke the silence, and when he did it was to discuss something other than the object of his visit to Paris. They talked until the train reached 

Folkestone. 

Marie admitted to herself that Peter was useful. He saw her luggage safely aboard, found her a deck-chair and rugs, steered her through the clamorous customs house at Boulogne. He appeared to know the ropes, and had obviously journeyed to Paris many times. His knowledge of the language, she found, was quite wide, though his accent was atrociously English. She took the first luncheon with him on the French train and derived considerable interest from watching the unfamiliar landscape slide past the express as it bored its thundering way toward Paris. "This is a thrill for me," she observed. "Home again," smiled Peter. "I don't know . . . I feel a stranger in a strange land.

You people have anglicized me to that extent. I'm beginning to wonder if I shall prefer Paris to London." "I should think so. Only don't stay down in that street near the Opera. Go and live at St. Cloud, or somewhere. 

Perhaps you could persuade your father to take an atelier there." "I don't know. He's not easily persuaded. He's an extraordinary creature. Like a little child in lots of ways. One can never really get at what he's about. He's the original conception of the word vagueness. He's rather a dear, though." She paused. "How often does this train jump the points?" They had just roared through a station, the long wide coach lurching madly as the locomotive crashed a network of metals.

CABARET 

9 "I don't know. Why?" "I was only wondering if, on the law of averages, a jump was due now." "I think this is the driver's lucky day," said Peter reassuringly. "Have some brandy with that coffee. It helps smother the taste. The Americans are the only people who can make coffee properly." "Please don't start fostering racial antagonisms," pleaded 

Marie. "Have you been to America?" "A great many times." "Do tell me about it."

She listened while he told her of New York, of Chicago, of Salt Lake and Detroit, Washington and Boston; and he was still telling her when they ran into the Gare du Nord.

Peter Madden secured a taxi for her beyond the barrier, and it was not at all difficult, in that place of crowds and hurrying people and of idling standing sightseers, for Legrand to lounge inconspicuously close to them, lighting a cigarette, when Marie climbed into the taxi. Peter gave the man the address Marie had shown him on the torn top of the letter, and the taxi started off. 

Legrand ran to the nearest telephone box and put a call through. "Is that you, Pierre? Have they advised you of my wire this morning? Good. The girl's on the way now. Taxi."

Legrand came back to the turmoil of the great station with the relieved look of one who has at last accomplished a task. 

Meanwhile Marie was racketing away down the rue 

Lafayette with Peter Madden's last words in her ears. “Au

'voir. I'm not going to say good-by. And don't forget-if you're in any difficulty, ring me at the Hotel Chatham.”

She felt restless now and behind her assumption of assurance was a certain fret. She acknowledged that she would be glad to see her father and reassure herself regarding him, though, at the same time, knowing him, she was annoyed at his silence and inclined to debit it to carelessness. 

The taxi reached the Place de l'Opera, circled the Metro station at giddy speed, slammed down the Avenue de l'Opera and into the rue des Petits Champs as though its driver's life hung on a split second. It pulled up with a shriek of brakes before a small café.

Marie regarded the establishment with some bewilderment. A sign bearing the word Tabac hung outside it, and across its window was the inscription Degustation. Within, some tables and chairs were scattered about that part of the floor which was not enclosed by the semi-circular zinctopped counter. Behind the counter a large lady attended to the wants of several men of the laboring class. 

Marie consulted her slip of paper again. The address was right enough. She went inside and inquired rather hesitantly for Monsieur Constantin Delorme. On this, the large lady, giving her an appraising look, shouted: “Pierre! 

Pierre!" and from a door behind the zinc came the fattest, grossest, whitest-faced man Marie had ever seen. She felt herself go cold at his approach. It was as if some loathsome slug came crawling out of its native darkness toward her. 

Pierre leaned on the zinc, surveying her with sunken, piglike eyes. "Ah, yes, ma'm'selle. You come to inquire for Monsieur

CABARET 

II Constantin Delorme. That is right. He lived there.” A very fat finger stabbed vaguely upward, and Marie imagined the existence of a flat above the zinc. "But he has removed himself. During some weeks. Are you by chance Ma'm'selle Marie Delorme?” "That is my name." "So. There is a message left for you by him. It is that you go tonight, or any night you may arrive in Paris, to the

Cabaret Vibart in the Champs Elysees. Let me see. Midnight. That was the time. The Cabaret Vibart at midnight, tonight. Ask the porter outside for Monsieur Delorme. 

There you will find him. You will remember, ma'm'selle?

The Cabaret Vibart at midnight." 

That was all Pierre could tell her and Marie left in the taxi, directing the driver to take her to any modest hotel he could recommend, for she had not an unlimited supply of money. He put her down at a hotel in the rue d'Antin a few hundred yards away, where she secured a very comfortable room with bath for thirty-five francs a night. 

She was not definitely disturbed. Pierre's appearance had shocked her, though she had no reason to suspect him of being other than an honest café-proprietor. She half wondered if she should telephone Peter Madden, but decided she would not do so until she had been to the Cabaret Vibart. That, she recalled, was the place Peter Madden had described to her-the biggest, most flaring, luxurious night joint in all Paris. 

And her father was there. . . . She wondered what he was up to, what strange whim among his many strange whims had led him to a place like that.

CABARET 

And wondering all this, she did not stop to speculate on the extraordinary fact that her father appeared to have anticipated her visit to Paris. 

She dined, put on her best evening frock, and prepared to go to the Cabaret Vibart at midnight.

Chapter II 

THE lights climbed the white façade, flared brilliantly for a few seconds, went out to utter darkness; climbed again, flared again; went out again.

BART RET 

Beneath, in red and mauve neon, writhed and glowed: 

On danse, on chant, on rit.

On either corner of the building, mauve and blue streaks rocketed away to nothingness, coming from nothingness, appearing and vanishing fantastically-a madness of color made magical against the semi-darkness of the Champs Elysees. 

Beneath the projecting canopy of gold-colored glasswhich glowed richly from the myriad lights beneath it and flung upward a yellow radiance that, when the other lights were out, lit the white concrete face of the building gloriously, and when the other lights showed, was lost-the porter stood, a war veteran with medals across the gorgeous uniform provided for him by the Cabaret Vibart. 13

CABARET 

He opened the doors of the sleek and silent cars which slid to the curb-side under the sedate trees of the great thoroughfare. He stood, cap in hand, while monsieur and madame strolled through the open doors into the wide foyer beyond. Through those doors came the sound of tumultuous music, the quick, heathen throb of jazz, thumping as the death drums thump in an African forest . . insistent, evil, alluring. 

The foyer was all stainless steel and scarlet, slashed angles and futuristic lights, a place of many grotesqueries, imbued with a feverish quality of unreal but terrible life. At its far end were two doors, swinging silently on invisible hinges.

The doors were encased in stainless steel. They shone like the steel doors of a prison. 

Monsieur and madame would go through that doorway, an eager chasseur pulling one of the doors aside to allow their entry. The hot air leaped out each time the door was opened, charged with perfumes, the aroma of cigar smoke, carrying with it the chatter of many tongues, the increased clamor of the band. Beyond was the dance-floor, with a dais about it, and with many tables at its edge. A strange fantasy had gone to the decoration of this room. It presented to the shocked imagination an impression of ruthless mechanization, of the age of the machine. The furnishings were of steel, as were the chairs, the tables. The rail and its supports along the edge of the dais were of steel. But where, in the foyer, scarlet had been the background of this menacing, gleaming metal, here was employed a rich and deep blue, which in the cunning lights looked like silk, soft to the gaze, apparently soft to the touch.

The place was full. An army of waiters fled from table

CABARET 

15 to table. The courtesans of the establishment ogled and danced. The plop of champagne corks sounded below the unceasing cacophony of the band. White shoulders gleamed close to black evening suitings; fair heads leaned toward dark sleek heads. There was talk and laughter, smoke lazily wreathing under the lights, money flowing as swiftly as the champagne bubbled from the gold-foiled bottles.

The Cabaret Vibart, the most famous of its kind in all Paris, was in full swing, and the sound of its revelry reached out in diminishing cadence to the lamplit broadness of the 

Champs Elysees beyond its doors.

It had been the longest evening Marie had ever known. 

She had dined at her hotel and, the meal finished at halfpast eight, faced the problem of passing the time until midnight. With a cloak over her evening frock, she had walked to the Opera and taken a soft drink outside the Café de la Paix, and thus wasted some of the intervening and endless minutes. She wished she had gone to a theatre, but her ignorance of Paris was absolute and into the bargain she had gradually felt more and more strained as midnight crept nearer on lethargic feet. 

In fact, this feeling of strain had overcome her previous decision not to communicate with Peter Madden, and somewhere round about nine o'clock she telephoned his hotel. 

He was out. He had dined and left about twenty minutes before she put her call through. They had no idea when he would return. She now wished she had 'phoned him immediately she received Pierre's message.

Normally self-reliant and fearless, she was conscious of a vague fear. She kept telling herself she was being foolish and absurd, but the fear persisted. She had felt it in her

CABARET 

bones ever since the slug-like Pierre had disgorged himself from his den to peer at her across the zinc. But she was not going to be intimidated by her own foolish weakness.

Her father had asked her to go to the Cabaret Vibart at midnight. What an idiot she would look if she arrived with a policeman! She laughed at herself-but the laugh did not ring altogether true. At ten minutes to twelve, after spending most of her time chatting to the hall-porter of the quiet little hotel in which she was staying, she sent for a taxi and drove to the Cabaret. From the porter she had learned of the Cabaret Vibart's richness and luxury, and this had largely reassured her. She had nothing to fear in a place crowded with people. 

The taxi put her down on the edge of the aura of golden light from the glass canopy and an eager commissionaire, cap doffed, opened its door for her. She paid the cab off and saw it slide away, and stood for a moment staring at the place to which she had been summoned. 

Within its entrance-hall people lounged, talking, smoking-well-dressed men and elegant women, with watchful chasseurs standing by to minister to their wants. Constantly the big stainless steel-covered doors swung open, permitting her narrowing, widening views of the tumultuous interior, flinging to her ears the throb of jazz, to her nostrils heated perfumes. Above, the façade lights glowed and dimmed, lighting her face, darkening it. 

She said to the porter: "I've come here to meet Monsieur Constantin Delorme. I understand you would know about it." "Yes, ma'm'selle. This way, please."

He took her into the foyer and to a chair, then called a

CABARET 

17 boy. He spoke some soft instructions to the boy, who hurried away. Within a few moments Legrand presented himself before Marie. The thing had gone like clockwork, and now Marie no longer worried. She was expected. All was well. She looked up without suspicion into the face of the man who had secretly observed her for so many weeks in London. "Ma'm'selle Delorme? You want to see my friend Constantin? Permit me to introduce myself. Georges Legrand. 

Will you come with me, please?”

He escorted her through a small door opening off the foyer, and she found herself in a long and narrow, dimlylit passage which ended in a wide square space, insufficiently lighted by a single electric bulb. This space proved to be the well of a stairway, which lifted to dim landings, and zigzagged its way to the enormous height of the building. Legrand paused at a door near the foot of the stairs, tapped on it, and waited. Here the sound of the band could no longer be heard. The silence that succeeded Legrand's tap on the door was so absolute and crushing that Marie found herself again filled with vague fears. She began to hear the beating of her heart. 

On the first landing a door crashed open, loosing a flood of yellow light and a torrent of enraged words. Marie and Legrand looked up. There was a sharp and whiplike crack, a crack which brought a startled and terrified cry to 

Legrand's lips.

A reeling man hit the balustrade above, broke it with the weight of his impact and crashed to the floor at Marie's feet, where he lay on his back, his face upturned to the dimmed light of the stairwell.

CABARET 

She saw that face, pallid now and distorted, eyes staring, a smear of blood on the bald head-saw it and knew it. 

Lurching forward, she came to her knees at the side of the man who lay on the floor, and hearing again the commotion above, looked up.

Peter Madden was leaning over the broken balustrade. 

In his hand was a gun. 

She cried: "You've shot my father! You've shot my father!" 

Her cry was so full of the wild and piercing agony that filled her heart that she was startled at the sound of it. It seemed to echo and hang amid a sudden and awful silence which swooped down on that dim-lit place. It was as though all action were suspended momentarily-Peter Madden leaning over the balustrade with the gun, the men behind him on the landing, the dead thing at Marie's feet,

Legrand back against the wall, suddenly pale, with yellow teeth showing. all still, like a cinematic picture checked in its projection. ... 

Then there was movement. Madden vanished from the balustrade above. Marie heard a short scuffle, a cry. She was no longer looking upward but was bending over the prostrate body of Constantin Delorme and crying to him. to speak. He did not speak. Perhaps he was even then hearing the last great words of Judgment.

Feet rattled on the stairs and two men came running down, one crowding behind the other with frantic haste. 

Legrand slid away from the wall, pulling at his lower lip with a shaking hand, his eyes staring. No sound had come from the room beyond the closed door. 

The first of the two men who had rushed down the stairs

CABARET 

19 flicked his hand toward Legrand. Legrand nodded, took a long sideways look at Constantin Delorme, and scurried out of sight. 

Marie, sobbing, heard a quiet voice. "Henri. Fetch Therese. Quick." The voice held a note of urgency, but was perfectly calm and smooth. Footsteps sounded again, soft and padding and swift on the carpet of the corridor.

Marie was aware of these things dimly, as though they took place at a distance. The horror of the swift and irrevocable tragedy had stunned her. She found it incredible. 

But Delorme's white face, looking up into hers, slowly conveyed its message to her numbed brain. 

Her search was ended. She had found her father, and in the same instant, had lost him for ever.

Involuntarily she put her fingers to her eyes, and wondered to find no tears. She could not weep. Beyond a sense of dreadful, irretrievable loss, she could not feel. She was shocked to the core of her being. Long minutes went by.

She became conscious that the man who had spoken, who had flicked her conductor out of sight as he might have flicked a pawn off a chess board, who had sent Henri scurrying to find Therese, was standing over her. As she looked up at him he was taking a cigarette from a golden case, and he lit it while his eyes met hers. "You saw it happen?" she cried. "He's dead. Oh, can nothing be done?" "Everything," he replied in a calm voice. "Everything will be done that is necessary."

The woman who had padded along the corridor drew 

Marie gently to her feet.

CABARET 

"Lean on me, chérie," Therese said kindly. "Just steady yourself a little."

As she spoke, Henri returned with another man, and Marie saw them, while dull stupefaction still held her helpless, lift the dead body and begin to carry it past the foot of the stairs. She made a movement to follow them, but 

Therese restrained her. "Not that way," she said in her gentle voice. "It would be bad for you. Come with me until you feel a little better," and she led Marie through the doorway at which Legrand had waited and into a small ante-room. "Take a sniff at this," she said.

Marie sniffed the smelling-bottle and felt the woman's hand laving her forehead. Through the pungency of the smelling-salts crept the cold clean perfume of eau-de-cologne. 

It was like balm on her heated temples. She lay for a few seconds on the chaise longue to which Therese had drawn her. Then the shock of realization came upon her again, and she sat up, glancing about her wildly. "I must go," she panted. "My father . . . It's murder." 

Therese laid a soothing hand on her knee. "There's no hurry, chérie. None at all. You stay here till you are quite recovered. Shall I bathe your head again?” "No! No! My father has been killed. Don't you understand? My father . . ." Grief choked her words. Tears swept into her eyes. Huddling down, she sobbed, her hands covering her face. Therese patted her shoulder, saying: "There, there. You'll be better soon. You really mustn't give way like this. You'll be ill, you know. Now come

Marie moved, as though she would throw off the hand 99

CABARET 

21 with which Therese caressed her. She managed to steady herself sufficiently to stop crying. "I want to see that man," she said. "Which man?" asked Therese. "The man who spoke to me when when it happened." 

Therese's brows wrinkled. She appeared to be deep in thought for a moment. "I'll fetch Monsieur le Directeur," she said. "He will know. I was only instructed to attend on you, ma'm'selle. 

Will you rest here for a moment while I go for him?" 

Marie nodded, and Therese went to the door, looking back as she reached it. Marie was huddled on the chaise longue, crouched under a weight of agony beyond endurance. Therese went out, and in a very short time there entered the man who had smoked the cigarette above Constantin Delorme's body.

Marie, looking up at him, saw that he was tall and elegantly dressed in evening clothes. His features were pale and irregular but very attractive in their Latin masculinity, and lit by direct and fearless eyes. He was obviously a man of considerable and lithe strength, and there was an air of openness about him which, though Marie could not know it, many women had found deceptive and dangerous. 

He said: "I am Charles Marteau, madame. One of the directors of the Cabaret Vibart. I understand you wish to see me?" "Yes. About my father—” 

He checked her. "Your pardon, madame. Is your father here? I will have him brought to this room. We had no

CABARET 

idea when you were taken ill, that you were escorted. I hope, by the way, that everything has been done for you that you would wish."

Marie was now on her feet. She stood and stared at himand stared-and stared. He met her stare easily, a little polite smile in his eyes. 

She suddenly cried: "My father has been killed! You saw him killed. At the foot of those stairs. . . 99 

A look of stupefaction leaped into Marteau's eyes, and then left them kindly and a little gentle. "Madame!" The word was touched with the slightest hint of protest.

Marie felt her senses reeling; she felt frantic and desperate. 

She came across the room toward him. ... "You stand there-and look like that-and say But you saw my father shot on those dreadful stairs. You were there! And Peter Madden-with the pistol. . . ." "Madden?" His brows wrinkled. "I'm very sorry indeed, madame, but I'm afraid I don't know the name.” 

Marie drew her hand shakily across her forehead. "You don't know..." Her eyes lighted. "There was a man— 

Henri. He came down the stairs with you. You can't-you dare not-stand there and tell me I am wrong. Henri was there."

He nodded. "There are several Henris working here as waiters, madame, and most certainly one of them reported your fainting fit to me. I'll have him fetched."

He touched a bell push in the wall and a man Marie had never seen before answered the ring. "Send No. 17 to me," said Marteau. As the man went he turned to Marie and added: "All our waiters are numbered.

CABARET 

23 If I asked for Henri any one of four or five would arrive. 

Won't you please sit down? You still look very ill. And can 

I order a taxi for you or order you any refreshment? A little champagne..." "No. Please don't talk to me."

She stayed by a table in the middle of the room, leaning on it with one hand. She did not look at Marteau. She was trying to think.

This suave, velvet denial; this smiling pity; this cottonwool resistance-more nerveracking than any brass-bound antagonism. . . and her father's face as she had last seen. it, upturned toward hers in the dim light of the ghastly stairwell, with the echoes of the gunshot flung back and forth in the dark spaces above.

There was a discreet tap at the door and, in response to 

Marteau's call, a waiter entered. He had a table napkin over his arm and in his coat lapel gleamed the little white bone button displaying his number. "That's the man," said Marie quickly. 

Marteau turned slowly to Henri. "Will you tell me where you found this lady?" he asked. "Yes, monsieur. I found her in the corridor outside. I presume she had wandered by mistake through one of the private doors-perhaps seeking the cloakroom. She looked very ill, and as I approached and asked if I could assist her, she cried out and fainted. It was then I reported to you." "Was she alone?" asked Marteau. “Oh, yes, monsieur.”

Marie awakened to swift and panting talk. Henri had come down the stairs with Marteau. Henri had seen her father, had been sent for Therese. "There was Legrand. He

CABARET 

saw it. He brought me here.” Her voice was lifted hysterically.

Henri stood very politely, a little subservient, emphatically incredulous. He said: "That is all, monsieur." "All right," said Marteau. “You can go." He turned to 

Marie. "You have seen Henri, madame. He confirms what little I know of this unfortunate happening. You ask for a 

Monsieur Legrand. Did he come here with you? If he did, and he is likely to have remained, I will have him found." 

Marie spoke dully, slowly. “I must see the police.” 

Marteau's brows lifted slightly. "I hope, madame, you are quite aware of the seriousness of that remark. This establishment has a high reputation-” 99 "I must see the police about this,” repeated Marie. "Certainly, madame. I'll have the Sûreté telephoned."

Marteau paused, and his voice changed slightly. There was a little hint of impatience, even of anger, in his tone. "I think it would be better, not only from your standpoint but also from mine." • • Again he touched the bell, and again it was answered by the man who had fetched Henri. 

Marteau said: "Telephone the Sûreté and ask them to send an officer along here at once." 

They waited. During this period of waiting Marteau rang for half a bottle of champagne and some biscuits and two glasses. Marie, however, refused the champagne. Minute piled on minute. Marteau spoke only once. "I wish they would hurry. I have my affairs to attend to. The place is full. It is not . . . nice . . . to have the police summoned, madame.” 

Marie said nothing. She felt crushed and despairing. At

CABARET 

25 last the man who answered the bell announced that an officer of the Sûreté had arrived. Marteau told him to send the officer in, and got to his feet. "I will leave you alone,” he said to Marie; and as a loud knock sounded on the door: "Come in."

A man entered. He was short, thick-set, and he wore a bowler hat which he did not attempt to take off. He had a hard face lit by considerable intelligence. He said: “I am Sergeant Ganier from the Sûreté. I understand you want my help." 

Marteau indicated Marie. "This lady would like to see you, sergeant. By the way... your card of identity?"

A card was produced and he scanned it and returned it to the detective. When the situation had been explained, when he had heard Marie's accusations and Marteau's calm denial, Sergeant Ganier requested Marteau to withdraw. “I can better question ma'm'selle if we are alone, monsieur," he said; and Marteau went out. When he had gone Marie told Ganier all her story. He made various notes. The telling occupied a long time, for she started in London, related her train journey, and the happenings since her arrival in Paris. "I know Pierre's zinc," he said. "Pierre is, I believe, quite all right-respectable, you know. But this is a strange tale, ma'm'selle. The Cabaret Vibart is of the highest reputation. We have never had any trouble with it in any way. 

Would you like to wait here while I question a few people? 

Then I shall ring up Headquarters and report. You will be quite all right."

Once again Marie waited in the closed and silent room. 

She felt incapable of thinking. The enormity of Marteau's

CABARET 

suave denial, of Henri's slick and simple story, the sudden horror of her father's death, had stunned her. But she felt she liked Ganier. There was a certain crisp directness about him. He was gone for some time. 

When he returned he looked perplexed. "I've seen Marteau and"-he consulted his pocketbook-"Henri Falon, a waiter, and a woman named Therese Setard. They all tell the same tale—that you were found by Falon in the passage outside, fainting." "But that isn't true-"

Now you can do nothing here. I have the address of your hotel and I would advise that you go straight back there. I shall certainly see you in the morning, and hope to have something to report. I am having another plain-clothes man sent up." "But my father-his dead body is somewhere in this place. 

Surely that should convince you—” "Madame, I've searched every corner of the Cabaret Vibart. There is no dead body, no man wounded or hurt, in the place."

Marie began to protest, began to display signs of once more becoming frantic. He soothed her with steady commonsense phrases. At last she consented to leave. "And now," he smiled, "you will excuse me."

When he had gone Marie stood for a while, dazed and shaken. Then, with a little shrug, she walked to the door and opened it. In the corridor she met Marteau. 

She said to him: "I'm going to my hotel. I want you to

CABARET 

27 show me the way out from here." As she spoke she began to walk past him; but he smiled at her and caught her arm. "Pardon me, madame," he said. "Not yet."

Chapter III 

MARTEAU'S grip on Marie's arm was not hurtful, but it was firm enough to hint at the immense strength of the fingers that held her. She guessed that a scream for help would not carry beyond the walls of that silent corridor; and realizing the uselessness of resistance or argument she allowed Marteau to conduct her across the stairwell and into a room on its farther side. A man was sitting in the room. 

He was cleaning his nails with a paper knife he had picked off the desk at which he lounged, with an elbow resting on its surface. He looked up at Marie as she entered, but did not put down the paper knife and did not speak. Marteau closed the door. "Jake, this is the lady who calls herself Miss Delorme. 

Miss Delorme, I want you to meet my colleague, Mr. Fish.” 

Jake Fish nodded. For a fractional second his eyes swept over Marie, and she saw quick admiration in them, an appraisal which stung. She flushed hotly as Marteau urged a chair forward for her and she sat down. 

Fish, after a long stare at her, a stare which traveled from the crown of her head to the tips of her high-heeled shoes, inserted the tip of his little finger into his right ear, gave it a vigorous twist and said: "I'll say you're easy to look at, Miss Delorme.” He spoke with a pronounced American accent, and Marie then realized why Marteau was speaking in English. Probably Fish spoke little or no French. 28

CABARET 

29 As her wits began to clear she was able to examine him. 

He was short and thick-set, heavy jowled, his face deeply pitted with pock-marks, clean-shaven, with a wide and thicklipped mouth. He was dressed extravagantly in an evening suit of exaggerated cut which seemed to emphasize, rather than detract from, the brutality of his appearance. 

Marteau had taken a seat behind the desk. He looked stern, even threatening. "Now," he said, "I want to have a few words with you, young lady." 

Marie said nothing. She glanced from Marteau to Fish.

Fish was picking his teeth with the paper knife and watching her intently. He grinned as her eyes met his. "To begin with," added Marteau, "you came here tonight under circumstances which I consider mysterious, and you have levelled against this establishment a most serious accusation." He paused. "Who sent you?" The question was asked sharply.

Marie's first impulse was to tell him she refused to answer any questions; but the shock of the recently enacted tragedy had left her with little inclination for a battle of wills, and she thought that the sooner she dealt with Marteau's catechism, the sooner she would be allowed to go.

She wanted to get away to some quiet place alone, where she could endeavor to recover from the shock of her father's slaying, and where she might, in time, find a softening of her grief. Instead . . . here was Marteau questioning her, behaving quite politely, but with a threat behind the politeness.

Here was murder done and known to her, but covered by an audacious piling up of lies which left her brain reeling.

CABARET 

She knew, as she looked into the eyes of Marteau, that he had created a situation which was, of itself, a gigantic lie. 

There was a sardonic humor in those eyes. They might even, in a mocking fashion, be acknowledging that he had lied, and that those about him had lied; and challenging her to attempt to prove it.

Yet at the moment-to fight on, to refuse to answer his questions, to endeavor to prove what he could easily disprove through the lips of many witnesses, meant too great an effort. She just wanted to get away. Later she might fight. . . ...

Marteau had repeated his question. She guessed it was only the preliminary to others, an opening question to which he himself might very well know the answer. "My father asked me to come here to meet him," she said. "Your father being?" suggested Marteau. "Mr. Constantin Delorme," replied Marie. 

Marteau smiled and leaned with his forearms on the desk, his hands clasped before him. "How long have you called yourself Delorme?" "Always. It's my name. What does this mean? What are you trying to arrive at?" "I'm trying to discover why you should come to this place tonight and endeavor to ruin it," replied Marteau sharply. "I'll say we are," echoed Fish, and pitched the paper onto the desk-top. knife 

Marteau stabbed a hard glance at him, as though he would tell him to keep his lips closed and leave the matter to him. 

He pointed a finger at Marie.

CABARET 

31 "You say your name is Delorme. Where do you live?"

Rebellion was lifting within Marie, but she fought it down as she viewed the prospect of an early escape from this inquisition. "London." "With relatives?" "No. With a friend." "Why not with relatives?" “I have none there.”

Fish, who had been listening intently, leaned back as though an important point had been established. "I see," said Marteau. "So your only relative is . . . this father of yours .. this Constantin Delorme?” 

Marie cried suddenly: "I shall not answer any more of your questions. What right have you to cross-question me in this fashion?" 

Fish leaned forward, his thick lips moving, his amazingly light eyes gleaming. Marteau's hand moved almost imperceptibly, and Fish, with a slightly savage look at him, relaxed and sat back once more. "I advise you to be sensible," smiled Marteau. "You have been sensible so far, and you will so continue." His voice was a little caressing, but there was a hint of iron threat in it which checked the further protest Marie was about to voice. "Now," said Marteau. "What did Monsieur Durant think of your visit here?” 

Marie stared at him blankly. "Monsieur Durant . . . 

I've never heard of him." Her expression lent all the confirmation necessary to her words.

She was aware that Fish was watching her very closely,

CABARET 

and she realized this might be a question of tremendous importance.

Monsieur Durant think of your visit to Paris? I ask it with emphasis. Think for a moment before you reply." "I've never heard of him," said Marie, with a little impatience showing in her voice.

Never heard of Durant, eh? Like hell you haven't." He swung his gaze on Marteau. "All right. All right. I'm talking now-the way this dame wants talking to." He again addressed Marie. "Listen, sister. You've been living on that guy Durant for years; and you try and tell us you don't know him. It's his money that's put food into your mouth and clothes on your back. He's the bird who subsidized the man you call your father, and you've got the front to sit there and say you've never heard of him. Find a couple of suckers to tell it to, but don't try and string us." 

Marie's color lifted. For a moment she forgot her grief and her fears, and was solidly angry. She even forgot to wonder-for that moment-what truth there might be in

33 teau was eyeing her with a little curiosity. He broke in. "Please... Jake . . . Your methods are well-clumsy."

We have many rivals, and some of them are unscrupulous. One never knows to what depths commercial antagonism will sink at times. 35 but when it comes to handling a difficult situation you're useless. You hindered rather than assisted me. I don't know why you insisted on being present."

36
