Chapter IV 

AT THE end of three days Marie found herself in a state of desperation. She had brought with her to Paris what little money she had saved from the allowances made her by Constantin Delorme, but she was realizing that this could not last a great deal longer. She would be equally penniless in London and Paris, and with her source of income dried up, her anxiety as to the future constantly increased.

Sergeant Ganier, of the Sûreté, had called on her daily. 

He was always very kind, and she had come to rely on him, though so far he had little to report.

He had failed to locate Peter Madden, though he had established the fact that Madden had booked a room at the 

Hotel Chatham on the day Marie arrived in Paris. But very early next morning he had left the hotel. Ganier gave

Marie details. It seemed that Madden got back to the hotel at about one o'clock in the morning. He appeared to be in a hurry. He had gone straight to his room, packed his things, called for his bill, and departed. He had told the porter he did not require a taxi, and had gone off on foot, carrying his suitcase. Whether he had picked up a taxi afterwards, Ganier could not tell, though the Sûreté had sent out an appeal to any taxi driver who might have driven 

Madden that night. So far no reply had been received.

Madden's departure from the hotel was undoubtedly in 37

CABARET 

the nature of a flight. Ganier was assured that his bag was a heavy one, and it was highly probable that he refused to allow the porter to summon a taxi because he did not wish that official to hear him give the driver an address. 

It was on the morning of the fourth day that Ganier presented himself at the little hotel in the rue d'Antin and displayed some excitement. "There is a discovery," he announced. "I have made it."

Marie had already learned that he was of considerable importance in his own estimation. "It is a strange discovery, one which makes me wonder if somebody in this business is not mad." 

His alert little eyes fixed themselves on Marie's face, and she had an uncomfortable feeling that she was the person to whom he referred. Nevertheless, she asked him what he had found out. "This," he said, and produced a notebook. She had seen it a great many times before. He was always writing in it and consulting it. "You reached Paris on the seventeenth of this month, ma'm'selle. On that date you claim that you visited the Cabaret Vibart and there saw your father, Constantin Delorme, shot dead. That is right, eh?" "It is true," said Marie quietly. "Whatever anybody says, whatever denials they make." 

He gestured. "So. Now further, you say Constantin

Delorme had not visited the address he gave you-Pierre's zinc-for some time." "Yes. That's what they told me there." "That's what they told me, also." Ganier looked up from his book triumphantly. “And now I, Ganier, know why. 

He was ill. Very ill."

CABARET 

39 Marie stared at him. "But I was to meet him at the 

Cabaret Vibart," she protested. "Ah!" Ganier looked at her questioningly. "But listen to this, ma'm'selle. Again I refer to dates. You arrived on the seventeenth, and you say you saw Monsieur Delorme shot on the seventeenth. That is where the inexplicable madness of the affair comes in. For Constantin Delorme 

Idied on the fourteenth. He died of some disease of the heart. And he is buried in the cemetery of Pere Lachaise."

A moment or two elapsed before the full purport of this was appreciated by Marie. Her father .. dead on the fourteenth, three days before she reached Paris . . . buried in the Pere Lachaise... dead of heart disease. "It's impossible," she cried. "It's a trick. I implore you not to believe it, Sergeant Ganier. I saw him myself. I heard the shot. And besides, that fat man at the café-Pierrewould have known." "No, ma'm'selle. I have seen Pierre. You can see him yourself, if you wish. He has told me that the last time he saw

Constantin Delorme, and it was some time ago I have the date here... ." He began to turn the pages of his book over. "But no matter. That date is unimportant. It was some weeks before he died. On that date Delorme left the message with Pierre. Pierre understands he constantly visited the Cabaret Vibart. Pierre just passed the message on to you. 

It was all he could do." "It's impossible," repeated Marie dully. She felt that here was the end of everything. Her mind groped vaguely into the black fog of mystery that swirled in front of her. 

Sergeant Ganier was kind in a chiding fashion, as though he dealt with a difficult child.

CABARET 

"But it is not impossible," he said. "You may be sure I did not rest at that point. I inquired further. Your father died in the private nursing-home of Doctor Krause, in the Avenue Kleber. I assure you, ma'm'selle, that that is a most fashionable place in which to die, and very expensive too. 

Doctor Krause is a fashionable doctor just now. I have seen the death certificate, I have seen the undertaker. I have, in fact, seen everybody." 

Ganier concluded his speech triumphantly.

Slowly Marie's brain stirred to normal activity. Here was something wrong; something terribly wrong. It is true that for a brief and reeling moment she wondered if, indeed, she had been the victim of hallucination that night at the Cabaret Vibart; but common sense told her the absurdity of this and told her, also, that she encountered grim trickery piled on trickery, a conspiracy of death and silence which demanded thorough and relentless investigation. 

She asked: "Then why did Peter Madden run away from the hotel at which he had booked a room and in which he never slept?"

Ganier indulged in one of his voluminous gestures. "That is a question to be answered, ma'm'selle. Be sure I have not overlooked it. Oh, no. We proceed in that direction. On the other hand, you must not attach too much importance to it. He may not have been running away. He may have met somebody." Ganier smiled. The smile was altogether 

Gallic. "Perhaps more enticing quarters were offered him. ... After all, an Englishman visiting Paris. . . . But with ma'm'selle one does not pursue that point to its definite conclusion yet. As I say, I investigate." ... "Yes," said Marie. She spoke a little drily. Her tone drew

CABARET 

41 a quick look from Ganier, as though, for the first time, he realized that she might prove a little difficult, might prove that she had a will and brain of her own. 

She added: "I want to see Doctor Krause. Can I see him immediately?" "Certainly, ma'm'selle. I will come with you."

A taxi carried them to the spacious Avenue Kleber and pulled up outside one of its luxurious houses. Ganier escorted Marie inside. A fountain played in the hall, and the place looked like the private residence of a wealthy man save that, as they waited, a uniformed nurse crossed from one room to another. They were ushered into the presence of Doctor Krause.

He was a tall man, with a pointed beard and grey hair, rimless pince-nez perched on a rapacious nose. He wore a spotless white linen overall. Ganier introduced Marie. "Ah, yes, Delorme." Krause touched a bell. A uniformed nurse appeared in reply to the ring. Krause said: “Bring me Monsieur Constantin Delorme's dossier, please." In a few minutes it was brought.

Krause appeared to be a somewhat irritable and autocratic person. Marie debited this to the vogue he was enjoying. He probably lorded it over his languishing women patients. As the nurse went out he glanced at Ganier and said: “You know you've been through all this with me before." ... "I know, monsieur." Ganier was a little apologetic. "But this lady is the daughter. . . . You understand . . . ?” 

Krause looked at Marie with a little more interest. "Oh, I see. I'm sorry." He was flipping over papers in the folder before him. "Now... Delorme." He spoke to Marie. "I attended your father on and off for some years, Ma'm'selle

CABARET 

99 Delorme. He suffered from angina pectoris. I have here some data. Hm." He cleared his throat and read out a mass of technical details which were Greek to Marie; and then added: "I warned him constantly, but he was a man ofshall I say easy habits. He took nothing seriously. He neglected my warnings. At last he had a serious attack and came in here. Oh-a week or so back. Fortnight perhaps. 

I can give you the exact date. He died Krause adjusted his pince-nez and scrutinized one of the sheets. "He died at three twenty-three on the fourteenth of this month. 

The usual symptoms. I issued the certificate and, being unaware of the existence of any relatives, I arranged for the funeral. He is buried at Pere Lachaise. But this gentleman knows that. He can supply you with any further information."

Krause's manner indicated that he was a little impatient of the whole affair. He was an important man with a great 

Ideal to do. He sat and smiled at Marie. 

Marie was quite calm. "Thank you very much," she said. "It has been kind of you to devote your time to me. By the way, could I have a copy of the death certificate?" 

For one fraction of a second Krause hesitated. Then: "Yes. Certainly. I'll let you have one now."

He touched the bell again and gave more orders. They waited a few minutes. Krause chatted about Constantin

Delorme while they waited. A man of irregular habits, a foolish man, squandering life recklessly. It was plain that 

Krause's precise medical mind did not approve of Delorme. 

In due course a certificate was given to him. He scrawled his signature across it and wrote the word "Copy" in the top corner, and handed it to Marie.

CABARET 

43 She thanked him, said good-by to him, and went out with Ganier. "Now I want to go to the Pere Lachaise," she said. “Is it a long way?" "A good distance from here, ma'm'selle." "Then I can't afford a taxi. Can I travel in any other way? 

Electric railway for instance?" "Yes, ma'm'selle. We can go from l'Etoile to the Pere 

Lachaise station. L'Etoile is quite handy." 

They went down to the shrilly clamorous subway and they racketed across Paris below ground in one of the worst underground railways in the world; and as they went Ganier kept shooting inquiring little glances at the very calm and set girl at his side, the girl who had suddenly taken charge of him and the affair, and who, having ceased to weep and look frightened, now issued orders and made decisions. 

He might have been more surprised could he have seen Doctor Krause during those minutes while he traveled with Marie. Krause was on the telephone, shut in a private room. "Look here, Marteau," he said, "that girl has been here with Ganier. She was evidently not satisfied with what he told her." "Well?" Marteau spoke impatiently. "Is she satisfied now?" "I don't know," said Krause slowly. "But I'm not." "What do you mean?" "I mean that girl. She's too calm and collected. When I told her Delorme was dead and buried I expected protests and cries that it was not possible. Instead she just thanked me and asked for a copy of the death certificate... to keep." "Well? Is that so terrible?"

CABARET 

"It might be with her. I don't like her, Marteau. She might be very dangerous. And I have a reputation." "Which I can wreck with a word," said Marteau bluntly. "You'll take your orders, Krause, and obey them, and leave the rest to me. You sound as though you're afraid—of a girl...." "That's it," agreed Krause. "I am." 

Marteau rang off.

Meanwhile Marie and Sergeant Ganier had reached the great cemetery of Pere Lachaise lying away in the Menilmontant district of Paris, in the lurking-place of banditry and dark things which move at nightfall. Ganier led Marie straight to the grave.

He tried to analyze the new Marie who seemed to have been born during this tour of confirmation. She stood and looked at the new grave, and she stooped and fingered the little label on the one wreath which graced it. "In memory of Constantin Delorme.-Richard Krause." "There will be a stone, ma'm'selle?" he said, a little hesitantly. "Oh, yes, of course. I must pay for it. The bill-Dr. 

Krause's bill, I mean. I forgot to ask him for it. And I must see about my father's effects." "I will inquire into all that, ma'm'selle," said Ganier hastily. “And I will report immediately. If there is a banking account, I will ferret it out. You shall leave that to me." 

Perhaps Marie smiled slightly. Ganier did not know. She was a little bewildering this new girl who talked to him with an ice-cold voice, and who seemed suddenly to have erected about herself a barrier behind which she moved and thought invisibly. She reminded him of somebody. . .

CABARET 

45 somebody who was, if Ganier were right, tremendously capable. He could not think whom it might be. They returned to Marie's hotel, booking through to the Opera, and walking from the station to the rue d'Antin. 

There, Marie asked: "What are you going to do now, sergeant?" "I shall report," said Ganier. "But I shall not abandon the case, ma'm'selle. When I report I shall suggest that a little further investigation might be advantageous. Everything will be done. You can leave it to me." "Thank you.” Marie gave him her hand. “Oh, by the way, you say everything. There appears to me to be one thing you have overlooked." "Really, ma'm'selle? What is it?" 

Marie shook her head. "I don't think it's worth worrying about just now, sergeant. I shall see you again." 

He assured her she would, and he went off thinking deeply, a little mystified and not a little disturbed. What could he have overlooked. 

Marie, walking into the hotel, was thinking of Sergeant 

Ganier's omission. He was a policeman, investigating the case of a man who was dead and buried. The cause of death was in doubt, yet the sergeant had not mentioned the wordexhumation.

Chapter V 

LATER that day Marteau rang Marie up. He had had time to think over Doctor Krause's message, and though he was still inclined to deride Krause's fears, he felt that a few precautionary measures would certainly not be wasted. On the telephone he was very charming. "I have been wondering how you are getting on, Ma'm'selle 

Delorme," he said. "I haven't heard from you, though your

Sergeant Ganier has been running round now and again. 

Have you seen the mysterious Mr. Madden?" "No," said Marie. "As a matter of fact, I have nothing to report." 

Marteau, though he tried hard, could detect no break in her voice. "I see. Now I hope you'll pardon me. I gather, from your address, from what I have heard, that your finances are not -er-limitless. If there is anything I can do to help you ...

By that I mean, if you thought of seeking employment over here in Paris, I might be able to assist you." He paused. "For instance, there are posts in the Cabaret Vibart. . . ." "That's very kind of you," said Marie. "May I bear it in mind?" "Assuredly. And get into direct touch with me." 

She thanked him and rang off.

Another day passed. She had now completely recovered from the shock she had endured at the Cabaret Vibart on 46

CABARET 

47 the night of the shooting of Constantin Delorme. She could, in fact, trace the moment of her recovery to her interview with Doctor Krause.

There, during that interview, she had suddenly realized that giant forces were, for some inscrutable reason, arrayed against her; and the knowledge, instead of crushing her hopes and her courage, had stiffened her, had brought about in her a change at which she herself marveled. 

She felt utterly unafraid, relentlessly resolute. These people-whoever they might be and for whatever they might strive had command of power. They could induce a highly fashionable and prosperous doctor to issue a false death certificate. She could not doubt that it was false, since she had seen her father killed. That doctor was afraid of the certificate, afraid of his signature at its foot. Marie had asked for a copy deliberately in order to observe Krause's reactions to the request. They had been plain to her. 

She was discovering within herself an amazing capacity to think quickly and decide surely. She no longer needed

Ganier. She began to regard him more as a mild form of amusement than as an assistant. She knew this lack of emotion was directed solely toward the people and the problem confronting her. She knew that behind its glacial wall lived and moved the quickly passionate and warmhearted woman who was Marie Delorme. But there was the wall, protecting her, keeping her miraculously aloof, so that she could think with naked clarity. 

Marteau had tried to be friendly. She laughed as she thought of it. Marteau wanted her to be under his observation; so he offered her employment. Marteau wished to hold her... that his scheme might go forward.

CABARET 

Well, Marteau should have his chance. Marteau meant danger. He meant death. Constantin Delorme had died in the presence of Marteau. But she, too, meant danger . . . to 

Marteau. She had the sure confidence of that; sure confidence in the newly born Marie Delorme. If Marteau called for a battle of wits, she would join the battle.

Meanwhile she wondered where Peter Madden was, and what part he might take in the conspiracy. A major part. 

Of that she was sure. He had shot her father. He remained in the background while Marteau and Fish talked to her.

He had, through this, become a powerful and sinister personality in her thoughts, a dim figure moving secretly through shadows toward a hidden goal. Perhaps one day she would meet him, and then, whatever might be his power and reputation among Marteau and his kind, she would wrest from him his knowledge of the whole affair. 

But Madden was out of her sight, beyond her reach. There remained Marteau. She decided to call on Marteau and ask him for a job at the Cabaret Vibart. 

She set out just before dusk, on the evening of this decision, and she walked to the Cabaret. There were a number of employees there getting the place ready for the night. 

Monsieur Marteau, one of them said, was not there. He never arrived at this time. On hearing Marie's name, the man's manner changed. He became polite. Monsieur Marteau had left instructions that if Ma'm'selle Delorme ever called and asked for him she was to be sent to his apartment in the rue St. Honoré if he were not at the Cabaret. 

She found the apartment without difficulty. It was at the top of a large building, the lower part of which consisted of elegant shops. On this upper story there was an

CABARET 

49 air of quiet restfulness, a hint of luxury, which was sustained when the door was opened in response to her ring and she was invited inside.

Here was the home of a wealthy man, and a man of considerable taste. Lights burned low in the hallway of the apartment. The furnishings were exquisite. Marteau's man had answered the door. He, too, it appeared, had his instructions. Monsieur Marteau would see Ma'm'selle Delorme immediately. It was not necessary that he should ask. 

The man took Marie into a quiet room off the hall. It was a lovely room, with a Louis Quinze clock ticking sedately on its mantelshelf. In a few moments Marteau appeared and greeted her with a show of warmth. As he did this, his man looked in. Marteau turned to him. "Oh, Auguste, I forgot." He glanced at the clock. “You've still plenty of time for your appointment. You can go directly you're ready. And-er-any time round midnight will do, you know." 

Auguste thanked him and withdrew. Marteau smilingly explained to Marie. “Auguste has an appointment of honor.

A maid down the street, I believe. Well, ma'm'selle, why have you called?" "I want a job," said Marie frankly. "You offered me one.

I don't know to whom else I can turn in Paris. I know nothing of the place. I can't go back to London because I took a single ticket, thinking . . .” She paused. “I haven't enough for my fare, after I've settled my hotel bill."

There was the sound of a shutting door. Auguste had gone out. 

Marteau thought for a moment. "I can offer you a post," he said slowly, "at my Cabaret. I don't know whether it is

CABARET 

one you would wish to take; but it is all I can offer. With your beauty, you would make an admirable dancingpartner. You will receive a hundred and fifty francs a week. 

But, of course, the girls pick up a great deal from visitors. I assure you there is competition to become a dancing-partner at the Cabaret Vibart. Most of the girls receive no payment from us."

Marie guessed the real nature of the employment he was offering; but she had set her feet on the path and she was resolved to tread it resolutely. "That is the only kind of post you have vacant?" she asked. "The only one. Why not? You are charming.” "Thank you." She spoke drily, and looked at him. His eyes told her he could read her thoughts regarding the offer. She added, quickly: "I'll accept. When do I start?" "At once," he said, and as he spoke the doorbell rang. 

Marteau started to his feet. "Who can that be?" he muttered. "Excuse me." The bell was ringing again, insistently. 

He hurried from the room, leaving the door wide open, so that Marie could see across the hall, and could watch him unfasten the outer door. 

As he pulled it open a man stepped inside. Marie, clutching the arms of her chair, leaned forward.

The man was Peter Madden, and as he took a step into 

Marteau's flat he jammed into Marteau's ribs a short black gun. Marie heard his words distinctly. "I've waited and watched for your man to go out, Marteau. 

Now we'll talk. . . . Put your hands up-and keep 'em up." 

Marie saw Marteau lift his hands slowly. Though he had his back to her she could tell that he was staggered by Mad-

CABARET 

51 den's appearance, and for the moment was unable to do anything but obey Madden's order.

She herself, after a second of immobility, in which surprise dominated her, got to her feet and ran toward the door of the room. It was as she made this movement that Madden realized her presence. 

His eyes left Marteau's face and flicked in Marie's direction. She saw his lips move. 

And Marteau, taking instant advantage of his folly, struck powerfully with the edge of his right hand on Madden's wrist, so that Madden's fingers relaxed and the gun fell to the polished floor.

The whole of this action had been so swift that Marie was actually only at the door of the room by the time the gun was on the floor and the two men were locked in a quick and fierce embrace, the sight of which brought her to a terrified standstill. 

Marteau, having forced the gun out of Madden's hand, had dropped his other arm about the Englishman's neck, and was forcing him backwards. Madden, taken utterly by surprise, momentarily had the worst of the encounter, and it was not until Marteau's fingers were feeling for his throat that he began to fight in earnest.

Marie, clutching at the jamb of the door, found both terror and a strange high thrill in the sight; and, more strange than that, a curious leaning toward Madden, a faint, galling wish that he might not be defeated. 

Marteau's right foot, sliding across the floor under the strain of the lock, touched the gun and sent it spinning toward the wall. Madden was now punching, and Marie heard the blows thud home heavily on Marteau's ribs—half-

CABARET 

arm blows, delivered from a short distance, but carrying weight and hurt; for Marteau was jolted badly as each one connected, and his cheeks paled. 

Marteau was a big fine fellow, but Marie realized that in this hand-to-hand struggle, though the initial advantage had been to him, Madden must be the victor. Concealed somewhere in his lithe and easy frame was a ferocious and swift strength which Marteau was now appreciating terribly.

The double, insistent beat of the fists at his sides at last had their effect. He reeled back, his hold loosed, and Madden, hooking cleanly, got him on the side of the face, skittling him across the room, so that he hit the wall and slid in a heap. 

Crouching there, his right hand groped sideways. Marie screamed-for that right hand had dropped on Madden's gun.

Madden, who had been about to follow up his advantage, saw the danger just in time. His speed of movement was revealing and amazing. He leaped sideways as Marteau, his face convulsed, lifted the gun, and he was out through the open doorway, the door slammed after him, in a split second.

It was then that Marie stirred herself from the immobility into which the struggle between the two men had frozen her. Madden was the man she wanted to see most of all. 

She began to run toward the door, but Marteau, with a movement of the gun, checked her. "Stay there," he breathed, and got to his feet.

His movements were uncertain, and his breathing was

CABARET 

53 very heavy and uneven. He pressed his left hand to his side and turned slowly, so that Marie saw a dreadful, white contorted mask on which evil passion was deeply stamped. 

He said, quietly: "I'll kill him for this."

There was no declamation in it. In fact, there was hardly any tone of threat. It was as though he uttered a cold and awful and inevitable condemnation.

He turned the gun over, looking at it, his hand still pressed to his side. Marie stood and watched him, and found her heart cold within her. He was more terrible than she had thought, more terrible in these minutes of defeat and static rage than she had imagined any man could be. 

For the first time she began to doubt the wisdom of her intention to work in the Cabaret Vibart. For the first time she began to appreciate in full the immensity of the risk she was taking and the kind of people with whom she would have to deal. 

Marteau's breath was easier, and some color was coming back to his cheeks. He had had what was literally a bout of in-fighting with a man who, though he had not previously known it, was a clever and dangerous middleweight boxer, and the blows he had received had carried more weight and strength than the distance the fists traveled had seemed to make possible.

He straightened himself and ceased to press his hand to his side and looked at Marie. "You saw him attempt to murder me," he said. 

Marie awoke to concentration on the statement. "Did he?" 

She paused. "What does it mean-that man coming here like that? And who is he?"

CABARET 

Marteau was now rapidly recovering himself. He dropped the pistol into his pocket and a flash of his usual easy smile showed on his face. "A man who is trying to do me a great wrong," he said. "It is my own affair, and I can handle it-and him." 

Marie was silent. Plainly, Marteau did not intend to offer her any explanation of Madden's intrusion. “I saw him . . . that night . . . at the Cabaret Vibart," she said. "He held the pistol when my father . . ." 

She stopped. Marteau was looking straight into her eyes. "What night?" he asked gently. He was now master of himself.

Marie met his look, thought for a moment, and then smiled. "Perhaps it was the night I made a mistake," she replied.

For a second she saw something in his eyes she had not seen there before-a little amazement, perhaps admiration, certainly wariness. He considered her. "Ah! You have decided that, eh?" "What else?" she asked coolly.

She knew that, unacknowledged by either of them in so many words, they fenced and understood completely. 

Marteau laughed. "And you will work in my Cabaret?" "I thought that was arranged."

He stepped across to her and looked down into her face. "You are a very self-possessed and calm young lady, aren't you?" "Does one necessarily go into hysterics because two men fight?" she asked. 

He shook his head slowly. "You know exactly what I

CABARET 

55 mean, so why say that? Well... you work for me. It is arranged. Permit me to compliment you. The reason for the compliment you may well understand without explanation from me. And now I'm afraid I must reluctantly cease to enjoy, for the time being, your most charming company."

She left him with a memory of one last long look he gave her. It was charged with appraisement, a little uncertainty.

Chapter VI 

THE Cabaret Vibart had its grip on Marie. That was how she felt about it a week later, after she had worked there as a dance-partner for six nights, from about ten in the evening until the small hours of the morning. For she owed the 

Cabaret money-the price of the fashionable dresses she must wear but could not afford to buy, and money for fines inflicted upon her for infractions of rules which she was only beginning to learn. "You'll never be able to leave until the debt is paid," the dark-haired Julie Lebrun informed her as they painted their eyebrows at adjoining mirrors in the dressing-room. "Monsieur Vibart will see to that. Oh, he's terrible, that Vibart." "Why should she want to leave?" asked Annette Bouchard, her blue eyes child-like beneath her blonde curls. "The pickings are good, aren't they?” "She could pay her debt in one night," said graceful 

Zanine Ponsot, with a meaning wink, "if she had more sense. How much did your monsieur give you last night, Annette?"

Marie began to view the Cabaret not so much as a building to which many people came, and through whose doors. passed in and out a multitude whose faces were forgotten, but as a living entity, a creature terrible for evil, something 56

CABARET 

57 which had secured her, body and soul, to hold to its will for ever. 

She would wake in the mornings with the strident crash of its band in her ears. Through her sleep would run the insistent mutter of the trap-drum, the drone of the saxophones, the high and shrill laughter, the ceaseless shuffle of feet on the dance-floor.

In her dreams the lighted façade of the Cabaret Vibart would leer at her like the grinning countenance of an ogre with yawning jaws through which pigmies passed laughing.

She met men there . . . and more men, and more men. 

Foreigners mostly: Americans, English, Argentines, Germans, Japanese, Spaniards, Scandinavians, Russians. .. men from all the nations of the earth coming in through the open door, dancing, drinking, loving, spending, going on leaving her behind . . . but with more coming at their heels-for ever and ever, it seemed. ... ...

The lighted dance-room flamed before her vision. It throbbed like a great gleaming heart set in a dreadful body.

Even in that first week she began to dread the evil spirit of the Cabaret far more than she dreaded the men who controlled it. The Cabaret Vibart . . . flaunting itself on the 

Champs Elysees like an overpainted, shameless woman standing boldly against the brightest of the street-lamps, snatching at the men as they strolled the pavements. 

It was a great building; she learned that slowly, without having a chance to go over it. There were many rooms in it, apart from the dance-room, the cocktail bar, and such withdrawing rooms as the public were permitted to use. Upstairs was a labyrinth of passageways and chambers. Upstairs was 

Vibart's Room.

CABARET 

Marie heard of Vibart's Room quite soon after she joined the Cabaret, but it was not until many days had passed that she began to learn something of Vibart, of the reason why Vibart's Room was regarded as something secret and sinister.

Among all the girls about the place, all so well chosen for their beauty and their ability to wear pretty clothes charmingly that often the rich and virtuous women who excitedly came with their husbands to see so famous, or infamous, a place, felt drab by comparison, Marie was most friendly with Julie Lebrun. It was from Julie that she learned the story of the Cabaret Vibart and of the Secret Room. 

Julie liked Marie from the moment they first met in the dressing-room. Julie was a brunette, petite, vivacious, with a curved and scarlet mouth and eyes like a frightened fawn's. 

Her name, thought Marie, was not Lebrun; but that did not matter in a place where names and past records were of no consequence, and only beauty and a willingness to sell it mattered.

Julie talked and talked. She was a great chatterer, gay and careless and laughing. "You . . . you are clever with the men—and yet foolish.” 

She imitated Marie's voice very skillfully, for she had a pretty gift for mimicry. "But no, monsieur. I must stay here. 

I cannot come for a ride with you. Dinner tomorrow? Perhaps. If not—I shall be here and dance with you. No, monsieur. Really. I must stay ." Julie laughed. “Very clever. 

And they give you francs for your pretty refusals! But not so many as if you had said yes." 

Marie laughed with her, but Marie's laughter was not so real as Julie's.

CABARET 

59 "Vibart likes girls who say yes," Julie added. "I've never seen him,” Marie said. "None of us has seen him," said Julie. "That's why he seems so terrible, I think-because we know he's here all the while, and yet he never shows himself. We're all afraid of him-as you will be. He hates us, you know-hates everybody, and makes himself a hermit. A hermit, here in Paris!" 

And Julie told Marie the story-as much as she knew-of 

Vibart and his cabaret.

Emile Vibart was an ex-soldier who, returning to Paris in 1919, had conceived the idea-an audacious one in those days—of running a "dancing" on the Champs Elysees, near the richest quarter of Paris, and where it was easy of access, instead of in Montmartre or Montparnasse. 

Julie thought that two other men, Jake Fish, an American, and Victor Durant, who had been in the army with Vibart, had arrived in Paris with the man who now employed them. 

But perhaps they had come along afterward, when the Cabaret was established. She did not know. She knew that 

Charles Marteau had come along afterward-to join Bivart,

Durant and Fish in the business of running the Cabaret. 

Almost from the first Vibart's venture had been a success. 

Vibart was an excellent musician, and a man of taste. 

Durant had a flair for business. Marteau knew all that need be known about women, wine and food. Fish understood how to attract the English and the Americans. The band, the floor, the cuisine, the cellar were, as now, near perfection. 

The modest establishment in which Vibart conducted his operations was crowded nightly. Within a few years Vibart had been able to build the huge Cabaret which now bore his name.

CABARET 

But in those few years, said Julie, a change had taken place in Vibart. For some reason which had never been made public, he began to dislike his fellows. Perhaps success, which would have sweetened most men, had soured him. More and more rare, so people who knew the Cabaret at that time had told her, became the occasions when he would appear with his violin to lead the dance-band.

When the new building was completed, when the greater Cabaret Vibart flaunted itself in lighted magnificence on the Champs Elysees, Vibart had become a recluse. He had had constructed, right in the top of the great building, a room, or perhaps if one could penetrate the forbidden doorway one might find that it was really a suite of rooms, which was called by everybody Vibart's Room. There he worked and controlled the Cabaret, sleeping there, living there alone, by night and by day, seen seldom-and less and less as time went by until he, at last, was not seen at all. 

He remained in the secret room, closely impregnable, wielding his power over all of them, from Victor Durant downward. People passing his door could sometimes hear him playing the violin which had, many years earlier, attracted folk to the old, small Cabaret Vibart which had mothered its brilliant daughter. In Vibart's room he sat and played haunting music and ruled . . . almost a legendary figure, a figure round which the imagination of Julie played; so that she saw a horror unimaginable, a thing of evil-for the commands that came from Vibart's Room were often harsh and terrible. ... 

Marteau, Fish and Durant personally controlled the Cabaret, with Durant at the head, Vibart's chief lieutenant. But 

Vibart signed the cheques and the letters, dealt with the bills

CABARET 

61 and the requisitions, issued orders which none dare disobey. 

The invisible hand of Vibart was on everything; and everything moved and existed by his will. "He's dreadful," said Julie in a little whisper, after she had communicated all this information to Marie in a series. of rather breathless sentences. “More dreadful than Durant.” "This Durant," said Marie. "What is he like, and where is he now?" 

Julie glanced round. Her voice was a little lower when she replied. "You want to be careful of him, my dear. Marteau is . . . oh not so nice, eh? And that Fish is a porc horrid. 

But Durant. . . ." Julie paused. "If I thought Victor Durant hated me I would not go to the police, I would not waste time doing anything at all like that. I should just run—and run-until I was so far away and so safely hidden that he could not catch me.' 99 "And where is he?" persisted Marie. "He went to America-on business." Julie flicked painted eyelid. "There is much business here of one kind and another. People are foolish, you know-and pay. pay. He went some weeks ago. And he got back to Cherbourg—oh, a week or two since. He started from the coast in a car and just on the outskirts of the town had a smash, which laid him up. I believe he is coming back any time now. You will see that things will change slightly then. Tighten up, if you understand. He permits no nonsense-that one. I am glad he is away. To see him standing up there on the dais, sometimes, watching us, is like seeing death standing there." 

Julie shivered, and Marie felt like shivering with her. In advance, she dreaded Victor Durant.

CABARET 

It was the same with the other girls, with the waiters, with them all. Durant's return was viewed by most of them with dread, by the bravest with uneasiness. Durant's hand was steel-closed on the Cabaret Vibart. Yet he took his orders from Vibart's Room.

Marie wondered what manner of man this Emile Vibart could be who ruled these terrible people, at whose word they fled to instant obedience.

She became more and more intimate with Julie. Julie told her some of her private affairs. "You must meet my ami," she said to Marie. "Boris 

Kolotsky is his name. He is❞—she sought for a word—"more than wonderful. So handsome. You like dark men, very artistic ?"

Marie smiled, and found herself unaccountably thinking of Peter Madden. "I like all men who are nice," she said. ... 

Julie laughed. "Ah-that is so. But just one . . . among all those, eh? Just one to whom you give that something 

You shall meet Boris. I've told him about you, and how you are teaching me to speak English properly; and he wants to meet you."

Marie was amused. In the old days, in London, more than one young man had sent her a similar message. But when, a day or so later, Julie repeated her invitation, Marie felt strangely that she did not wish to meet Boris. She knew it was absurd to feel like this, but the feeling persisted. 

Before she could meet Julie's friend, Victor Durant came home.

It was known in the Cabaret on that particular night. The word went round among all the people employed there.

CABARET 

63 Durant was back-that afternoon on the Atlantic Boat Express. He looked well, and bore no traces of the injury he had received in the car smash. He was, in fact . . . himself. ...

Marie felt slightly athrob with anticipation as she entered the dance-room. She would see the terrible Durant, whose name Marteau had flung at her as an accusation, and of whom everybody was afraid.

It was nearly eleven. As yet the Cabaret was sparsely patronized. Marie sat alone at a table, for Julie, with whom she usually sat, had been engaged by a stout German from Hanover, and was being dragged round the floor. 

A man walked onto the dais from one of the private entrances. Marie sensed a slight tensing of the atmosphere, unrecognized by the clients but plain to the employees. She stared at the man as he stood and gazed round him.

He was tall and slim, straight and elegant, in his Londonmade dinner suit. As he occasionally lifted a cigarette to his lips, Marie caught a glimpse of a diamond flashing on a finger of his left hand. She could distinguish his face quite clearly-narrow, long, cheeks quite pale but healthy, black hair coming down to a widow's peak in the middle of the forehead and brushed straight back like a sleek, shining, skull-cap-a distinctly handsome and dangerous man, with a cold reserve about him.

It was as she noted these things that she realized Durant had seen her. She thought she detected a slight stiffening in his stance-just for a flashing second. Then he walked to the edge of the dais and looked steadily her way. After a moment or two, he lifted his hand. 

He was beckoning her. She hesitated; then remembered he was Victor Durant, lord, under Vibart, of all this place.

CABARET 

She got up and walked toward him, conscious that Julie, hugged to the massive chest of her big German, was watching her with an expression that might have been one of alarm. She mounted a short flight of steps and came face to face with Durant. He was, she thought, as handsome at arm's length as at a distance-save that, close, one could see the cold rapacity in his eyes. 

He looked her over. "You're new, aren't you? By the way, in case you don't know it, I am Monsieur Durant. You may have heard of me. I am the chief director." "Yes, monsieur." Marie strove to keep quite calm under a scrutiny like a dagger-stab. “I've been here just a little over a week." "I see. What is your name?" "Marie Delorme, monsieur."

Durant took a long draw at his cigarette. There was no change in his face or eyes. His lips continued to smile slightly. "Do you live in Paris?"

Marie reflected that here was a catechism which followed rather closely that of Marteau; only in this case it was justified. Durant examined a new employee. "I do now, monsieur. But I came from London." "Really? You like Paris better, eh?" Durant smiled. "Who engaged you? Jules?" Jules was the dance-band leader. "No, Monsieur Marteau." "That's all right then. And you like it here?” "Quite well, monsieur." "Good." Durant waved his hand. The interview was ended. Marie went back to her place. Julie, passing her, gave her a quick and frightened little smile. Durant stood for a

CABARET 

65 moment or two smoking thoughtfully. Then he turned toward the private door and vanished through it—walking slowly, deliberately. 

Marie, though she didn't know why, felt afraid.

Chapter VII 

EMILE VIBART sat in his private room-in the secret room high up above the dance-floor and the clamor of the Cabaret, high up in a silence so intense that it seemed filled with the stealthy pattering of spirit feet. He sat crouched at the big desk, and Jake Fish stood over him. Down below, Victor

Durant was talking to Marie; but neither Fish nor Vibart was aware of it.

Fish hit Vibart again, brutally, horribly, so that again Vibart's tortured face struck the desk-top. "You'll say nothing to Durant, you swine. D'you understand? Nothing. . . . Those cheques were for business purposes only. Get that?"

Vibart sobbed and clawed with emaciated hands at the screwed-up papers that littered the desk. There were ever so many of those papers. They all had writing on them- 

Vibart's writing. All the writing began in the same way. "My darling Louise, "It is so long since I saw you, so long since I heard from you. 

Is it possible that you no longer love me?"

Only that was written on the papers-as though Vibart asked and asked a question to which he could find no answer.

He moved and tried to get up, and Jake thrust him down 66

CABARET 

67 again into the chair. "Do you want to smell the iron again?" he said. Vibart started, and huddled back from him. "I'll sign," he promised. "I'll sign. Anything. Oh, yes, Jake; I'll sign. Cheques Yes. Documents 

Give me a pen." "9 Yes. "You're not asked to sign now," snarled Fish. "You're asked to keep your mouth shut. Understand? Just keep your damned mouth shut-tight." He leaned over and spoke quietly. “Vibart, if you talk . . . if you spill anything . 

I'll have the hot iron on you once more." ... "Oh, God, no." Vibart's trembling speech seemed wrung from his heart. “God ... no no Jake, you wouldn't. · • • • • • I've had enough. After all these years. Not the iron-any more No. You wouldn't. You remember we ... friends once; you remember that estaminet near · · • 99 were "I ain't remembering anything except that you've got to keep quiet," snarled Fish. “Now let's have it. Promise . . or the iron." Vibart stood up weakly. He was a tall man, and his hair was snow white, though he was no older than Fish. A hand which had once been strong enough to wield a bayonet for France now trembled like an old man's as it fingered the stubble-covered chin. His eyes met the eyes of Fish. The unfinished letters on his desk might have seemed the work of a man insane. But his eyes showed no insanity now. They showed a horrible understanding and something else. They were the eyes of a man crawling under a load of terror which increased by day and by night; of a man fighting desperately to cling to the last shreds of a reason he felt to be tottering under strain that was unendurable. Only Emile

Vibart could have stood up under the imprisonment and tor-

CABARET 

ture inflicted on him through the long years by Durant, Marteau and Fish; but now even Emile Vibart was breaking. There were hours when he did not know what he didand when he grew conscious again he was startled to find the scrawled sheets he had littered across the splendid carven desk. "My dear Louise" . . . And a heart-cry for a love that was lost for ever. 

Fish drew close to him. "Have I got to tell you again I'll put the hot iron on you?" he demanded. "Listen, Vibart. 

I'll have your eyes one day, damn you. That's what I'll do.”

Vibart shuddered. The hot iron meant horror beyond imagining. At first they had used it often-way back at the beginning of this black gulf of years into which he had dropped. From time to time since they had used it when his imperious spirit had rebelled and taken no heed of consequences. But now . . . he could fight no more. He had fought wonderfully through incredible time. Now he was their slave, the thing which signed their cheques, which wrote their orders, which trembled at their words ... the effigy of power in the Cabaret Vibart, a pitiful mockery enwrapped in a mystery of their making. "I promise,” he said.

As he spoke, the door opened and Marteau walked in. 

Marteau looked a little agitated. At sight of him Vibart drew back, shrinking, his hands clutched to his breast. "It's O.K.," said Fish. "He's got it clear. Haven't you, Vibart?" “Why—yes. Anything. Of course." Vibart spoke with pitiful eagerness. 

But Marteau did not seem anxious to hear the result of 

Fish's talk with Vibart.

CABARET 

69 

He said shortly: "Jake, Durant wants you at once. He sent me for you. 

You're to come down to the office." "Eh . . . ?" Their eyes met. Vibart was watching both of them. "Oh, all right. I'll be with you." Fish turned to Vibart. "Remember?" 

Vibart nodded. 

Fish, passing him, struck him a blow which skittled him into his chair, and, laughing, went out at the heels of 

Marteau. He bolted the door after him.

When they had gone Vibart picked himself up and lurched into his chair, sitting there at the desk, his hands working before him, his face drawn with an agony which was not physical.

His tortured brain groped vaguely through a maze of thoughts, a maze it trod hour after hour, day after day, during the years of his captivity. 

They-Durant, Marteau, Fish-had seemed all right in the beginning; his good friends; Marteau and Durant fellow soldiers with him, fighting for France, and Fish an Ally, an

American soldier they had met near St. Mihiel. When the three-first Durant, then the other two-had descended upon him in Paris, after he had started his little cabaret show on the Champs Elysees, and when his violin was drawing the crowds, it had seemed good to welcome them, to try and help them by employing them; particularly as his war experiences, and especially the occasion in March, 1918, when he was one among a fleeing terrified rabble bombed by German planes, had cursed him with a morbid horror of crowded places.

The Cabaret had been successfully launched. His good friends could now direct it under his supervision. He could

CABARET 

sit in an office and tell them what to do—and play his beloved violin.

He remembered how they had all planned this great new place; and how from those plans it had grown to reality. 

It was Durant who had suggested the private suite of rooms -the quiet retiring-place where Vibart could find the solitude he loved. 

And then the day when he found that they had cheated him; the day they had locked him in, tortured him, broken him to their will.

For more than a year after that fierce and sudden attack upon him by the men who had robbed him, Vibart had been kept in close confinement like a caged beast. 

Then on the completion of the building he had helped to plan, he had been moved to the suite of rooms he had intended for his home, but which now became his prison. 

They were almost as he had designed them—the big room of business, his chambre d'affaires; the small salon opening off this; the bedroom; the lavishly appointed bathroom which was the only luxury he permitted himself. But he had not planned that the doors which formed the only exits from the suite should be lined with bronze; he had not planned that heavy bars should guard the windows, that their panes should be filled with opaque and toughened glass, moulded upon a mesh of thin but tempered steel. He had not planned that pointed fangs of steel should fringe the high walls surrounding the lead flat on the roof where he was permitted to take exercise. These things had been planned by the men he had trusted, the men who had despoiled and imprisoned him. He could walk onto the lead flat from his bedroom; it was spacious enough to allow the sunlight to

CABARET 

71 come pouring in a flood of glory between the walls; and there, on fine days, he would pace restlessly, pondering his unhappy lot, or sit on heaped cushions, drawing sad melodies from the strings of his violin. But there was no exit from it except back into his prison; nothing to be seen from it but the sky and a distant glimpse of a corner of the Arc de Triomphe, for no roof in the Champs Elysees overlooked the tall roof of the Cabaret Vibart. 

He knew, from the talk of Durant, Fish and Marteau, that the Cabaret prospered magnificently. He knew that in the outside world, and with the police, they had cunningly maintained its reputation, the reputation he had built up and insisted upon. The Cabaret Vibart was beyond suspicion. That he was a prisoner within its walls none knew but his gaolers. Cheques, letters, orders bore his signature as before. The three directors conducted legal and business negotiations in his name. People who had known him, who had seen how he shrank from his fellows, were not surprised that Emile Vibart had become a recluse. 

These things Vibart knew, and he knew also that the Cabaret's reputation was false-that blackmail and other secret crimes were practised there, for his three gaolers were not afraid to talk in his presence. The gates of the Cabaret were open to the world, and the world and his wife passed through them. To the ordinary pleasure-seeker the Cabaret 

Vibart was nothing more than a super-luxurious "dancing.” 

No harm befell the innocent seeker after revelry. But those with secrets to hide and with money to pay for concealment -the guilty husband, the foolish woman, the absconder, the great lady who, in an hour of folly, allowed herself to become entangled with Marteau . . . they paid, and paid

CABARET 

and nobody knew save themselves and those to whom the money passed.

Of all these things Vibart thought. His hand reached sideways and picked up the violin which lay among the pitiful papers on his desk. He cuddled it to him . . . close . . . as a lover might hold the one woman, and taking up the bow, he began to play Schubert's "Serenade" . . . the tune they had always demanded of him in the old days that were dead.

The sweet music crept on dainty feet through the secret rooms, and as it crept Emile Vibart smiled. The smile was charged with the tears of heartbreak.

Chapter VIII 

AS FISH and Marteau went downstairs, Fish said: “What is it?" 

Marteau shrugged his shoulders. "I've just arrived. Durant was waiting for me. He said he wanted you-and me. That's all." "Maybe trouble, eh?” grunted Fish. "Maybe," assented Marteau. 

Fish whistled softly through his teeth, and neither of them spoke another word as they made their descent to the ground-floor level and the office where Durant awaited them.

He was standing with his back to the fireplace, smoking a cigarette when they entered, and he said: "Shut the door, Marteau, and turn the key. I don't want any interruptions." 

Marteau obeyed, and Fish asked: "What's the big idea, Durant?" His tone was insolent and challenging.

Durant looked him over-for quite a long time. He did not answer the question. Under his scrutiny Fish shuffled at last, and looked away. Durant smiled. "Sit down, gentlemen. Sit down." His hand gestured easily. "Now then. Where's Constantin Delorme?"

He asked the question in the same tone of voice as he had employed when inviting them to be seated, and, somehow, his easy manner emphasized the sudden shock of his words. "Delorme... why..." ..." began Marteau. "How the hell should we know, anyhow?" 73

CABARET 

"That's it," smiled Durant. "How the hell should you? 

Or, rather-why the hell should you. You pair of spying bastards." 

Fish got to his feet, mouthing an oath, his hand sliding toward his left armpit.

Durant stepped away from the fireplace and hit him. The movement was swift and terrible, as though a beautiful sleek snake suddenly struck with hideous deadliness. Fish went down in a heap and Durant, bending over him, straightened himself with a forty-four gun in his hand. "Now we can talk. politely . . ." he said. 

Fish dragged himself up, shaken and pale. He spat oaths. "I'll get you, Durant," he ended. "You won't, Fish. And say no more. I'll have you killed tonight if you utter another threat.” "For God's sake, Jake . . .” began Marteau. 

Durant whirled on him. “Keep your damned mouth shut until I ask you to talk, you swine. What's your game? Spying. bringing that girl here. And where's that fool, Delorme?" 

Marteau sat silent. Fish went down into his chair, stroking his jaw. His lips were shaking and his eyes held that bleak impress of high fear which comes to men in the shadow of death.

The room was silent for a long time. During that long time the trio calmed. Durant lost all traces of his sudden, icecold and awful rage. Fish became more himself, assertive, vulgar, dogged. Marteau was once more as near being easy and smiling as he might be in the presence of the threatening Durant.

We find you playing a game on the side. What is it?"

Durant looked at them. He showed no signs of having surrendered his position. His whole attitude indicated that he was considering whether, under the circumstances, it

Chapter IX 

ON THAT night, in a luxurious flat near the Place de l'Etoile, a woman waited with impatience for a man she had expected many days earlier; waited with fret and impatience amid a silence which, more and more, was affecting her nerves. 

Mrs. James Rollerton had always been described as a beautiful woman. There was, however, a certain hint of selfishness in her expression which detracted from her beauty of face and form. A blonde, her lips were just a little too thin, her hair just too beautifully coiffured, her eyes just too coldly grey. Yet, withal, there was a latent weakness in the face which, somehow, heightened the impression of selfishnessthe weakness of the person who can never stand aside for others, yet who, faced by a crisis, surrenders absolutely. There was a ring at the bell. She answered it herself, such was her haste to see if this was the man for whom she waited, and opening the door, cried out with relief. "Peter! Why have you been so long? Ever since I had your telephone message yesterday afternoon I've been simply frantic with anxiety. It's most unkind of you—” 

Peter Madden interrupted her. "Half a minute, Alice. I presume this is to be a private talk." He kissed her on the cheek she turned toward him and, taking her arm, led her into the drawing-room and shut the door. There she perceived that he looked a little strained and hunted. 86

CABARET 

87 "What's happened?” she asked, in a quick, darting voice. "Have you got it?" "No." "Peter! And I depended on you. I told that awful man, Marteau, that my brother was coming over, and would deal with him. I really did think—” "For heaven's sake, Alice, leave the reproaches out of this for a moment or two. You've made a big enough mess without beginning to blame me." "But Jim might be sailing at any time. And the voyage only takes six weeks! He might be on his way now. Sometimes he changes his plans so quickly that he arrives when I think he's a thousand miles away. What can I say to him?" "You should have thought of that before you started cutting loose," said Peter Madden. "You know, Alice, you've got a first-class husband in Jim, but you never seem to have appreciated it. I'm telling you this without any reservations. 

Sometimes, watching the way you've behaved, I've been ashamed that you're my sister. Jim away-on the other side of the world—and you going about . . . I'm damned if I understand you." "You're trying to upset me," said Alice Rollerton, with a hint of tears in her voice. "You're the only one I could turn to in my trouble, and instead of being kind, you're just . blackguarding me. You always were a beast, Peter, even as a little boy. I don't know what I'm going to do when Jim comes back. He believes in me." "Really? Then give him cause to do so-from now on.

Chapter X 

For a day or two Marie had felt that while nothing appeared to be happening, things were moving secretly, silently, round her. She sensed it in the atmosphere of the 

Cabaret Vibart. 

Durant had talked to her again, still maintaining his pose of the chief director who, naturally, wished to know as much as possible of a new employee. He had been very nice and kind, and she wondered how he had achieved so terrible a reputation with Julie Lebrun and the other girls. 

Fish and Marteau she saw quite often. Fish never spoke to her, but now and again she caught his eyes fixed on her, and read a threat in them which made her shiver. Marteau was ordinarily businesslike, but he talked to her sometimes, and she found a hint of probing behind his talk, as though he tried to discover something she could not guess.

And all the time she thought of Madden, who was associated in some dreadful way with these men, and of the strange and secret room far away in the top of the building where the mysterious Vibart controlled the fortunes of all of them. 

Perhaps Madden could explain that room. Perhaps Madden could explain everything. If only she could find 

Madden. That he was a foe of Marteau was now established. 

That probably meant he was the foe of Durant and Fish and of Vibart. He was, in effect, the third point of the tri- 96

CABARET 

97 angle, and what his connection was with the affair she could not surmise.

And then, one night, just after midnight, he walked into the Cabaret. It was roaring at top speed, band crashing, floor crowded, lights blazing, champagne spilling into glasses at all the tables. Marie saw him through the slight smoke haze which wreathed across the lights-saw him standing at the head of the steps leading to the outer hall, straight and athletic in an evening suit of impeccable cut, looking round him slowly and deliberately.

That he should have ventured there was audacious, she thought, after what she had seen pass between him and 

Marteau; and yet on second thought, not so very audacious, since while he stayed in the dance-room no harm could possibly befall him. She happened to be alone at that moment and, acting on impulse, she threaded round the fringe of the dancers and came to where he stood.

Thus she met him face to face-on the top of the steps, and looked into his eyes. "At last, Mr. Madden.” She spoke the words quietly, but there was a throbbing undernote in them which reflected the rapid pulsing of her heart.

She expected him to show dismay, perhaps to make a dash for safety. She had swiftly theorized, as she crossed the floor, that he would not have visited the Cabaret Vibart if he had expected to find there the daughter of the man he had killed. She thought her confrontation would stagger him. 

Instead, he smiled at her. "So you are here," he said. "I wanted to see you." "You · • • wanted me? After 99 · · 

She checked her

CABARET 

words. Crowds of people were round them. She had either to make a scene-and her every instinct inclined toward wild accusation and a demand for his arrest as a murdereror she had to wait just a little while, listen to what he might have to say, and thus, perhaps, get nearer the heart of the mystery she was endeavoring to solve. 

It cost her a great effort of will to decide swiftly on this latter course, but she did so, as he added: "I want to talk to you. There's a table down there. Will you come to it with me?"

She looked at him uncertainly. He read the menace, the still remaining indecision in her eyes, and he went on: “It would be better for both of us. You think of sending for the police. Well, they can still be fetched . . . when we've talked."

It was as they seated themselves at the table that Marteau came to where Fish and Durant were sitting in the office. He burst in on them excitedly. "Madden's here,” he exclaimed. As Fish reached the door Durant spoke. "What are you going to do?" he asked.

CABARET 

99 starts ferreting round the Vibart secret, God knows what might come of it. We've all got our own opinions on that matter, but I guess in an emergency like this we've got to pull together for our own sakes. I'm going to knock him out when the lights go down and drag him in here.”

As he spoke he produced his gun, reversing it, so that he held it by its short muzzle, its heavy milled butt swinging from his hand. "I guess this'll do the business," he added. 

Durant reflected for a moment. “All right. Though I don't favor these direct methods. You'll attend to the lights, Marteau. Don't be too long, Fish. We don't want to scare anybody. I'm going to walk out to the dais when the lights go down and shout to everybody to be calm, that it's only a temporary fuse failure. We don't want a panic." "You don't cotton to the idea?" asked Fish, a little sarcastically. "It's crude," said Durant. "But get along. Time's being wasted, and they're talking." 

Fish slid out. Marteau followed him, making his way to where the main switch was installed. Durant went to a private door opening into the cabaret. He could see across the dance-floor, and considered it fortunate for their plan that a dance was in progress, that the floor was crowded with fox-trotting couples. If the people had been seated at their tables, they would be likely to remain quite still and quiet in the event of a light failure. But now there was sure to be outcry and probably a rush of some of them from the floor, and the confusion would assist Fish in his design.

CABARET 

the band. They had obviously only just seated themselves, for the waiter was standing by Madden while he glanced at the champagne list which was instantly and inexorably presented to everybody who placed himself at a table. 

Therefore, Durant judged, talk between Madden and Marie had not yet commenced.

Durant felt no thrill from noting all this-only a rather indolent anticipation. He could see Fish threading his way as quickly as courtesy permitted round the dais in the direction of his intended victim. Marteau would also be able to see Fish, for the main light switch was situated in a small box-like room wherein was installed a peephole permitting full view of the dance-floor. This was specially contrived so that, in the event of trouble, a man might watch there for the signal to plunge the place in darkness. Marteau was watching now.

Fish had reached the top of the short steps to the left of the main entrance. He went silently onto the floor, and approached Madden's table from behind. Durant drew slowly at his cigarette. The moment was near. Madden had turned in his seat to give an order to the waiter. Perhaps, in thus turning, he saw Fish; perhaps he did not notice him. Durant was not quite sure. He could not judge from where he was standing. Anyhow, he thought, Madden would not be surprised to see Fish or Marteau or himself if he came to the

CABARET 

ΙΟΙ sooner was it consumed and another ordered. The band was crashing noisily, and the packed dancers were shuffling round, front to front, back to back, shoulder to shoulder amid the heat and the perfume and the tobacco smoke. 

Fish was now very close behind Madden. The champagne was served. The waiter spun the bottle, opened it, and filled the glasses. He bent over Madden. He was asking if monsieur wanted anything more. Madden waved him away and he went in the self-effacing way of his kind. 

Fish put up his hand and stroked the top of his head.

The lights went out as though a thunderbolt had stricken the Cabaret Vibart with darkness.

For a moment the music ceased-then sounded again, blaring more loudly than ever, and Durant mentally complimented Jules, his band leader, for his presence of mind. 

For that moment the dancers came to a standstill. Then, as often happens when the lights fail at a dance, there was much shouting, some giggling and screaming; and somebody started to sing; so that the whole place was filled with the clamor of shouting and of music, of screams and song, while the drummer thunderingly increased the power and tempo of the rhythm.

Durant realized that there was no need for him to intervene. Jules and his band were saving the situation. For the moment the dancers were regarding the sudden darkness as no more than a pleasant thrill. They could not see each other, but it was delightful to dance and to steal kisses from invisible lips. 

Two or three minutes fled—a long time it seemed. . .

The band was still playing, but now it was possible to sense some uneasiness in the dancing throng. Durant was re-

CABARET

The music changed to a quick-step-faster-faster. Jules was whipping the crowd back to the normal. They were dancing with abandon. The Cabaret Vibart was all light and laughter again.

The table occupied by Madden and Marie was vacant. 

Durant stepped quickly back from the dais and slipped through the private doorway behind him. As he reached the long and silent corridor, he saw another door open farther along. A man reeled into view, holding his jaw, his legs unsteady, his hand clutching at the wall. He managed to kick the door closed with his foot. He was Fish.

Chapter XI 

THERE was a roar like thunder in the ears of Marie, the sustained roar of the almost open exhaust of the great car. She saw lights streaking away to right and left, a high radiance which was flung behind-the suddenly illuminated façade of the Cabaret Vibart.

There was a hold on her wrist like steel. Consciousness of speed came to her. A great bulk lifted, swung to her left, the car heeling. . . . They were making the circle of the Arc de Triomphe, crashing down the Avenue de la Grande 

Armee... more lights . . . more thunder speed sustained and terrible. ... She awoke to physical and mental life, to a full appreciation of what had happened in the few hectic minutes that had elapsed between the extinction of lights in the Cabaret Vibart and this swift surging flight. Fish appearing suddenly behind Madden . . . darkness like a dropping cloak a thud and a cry which she heard distinctly beneath the clamor which suddenly filled the place... Madden's arms closing round her, carrying her, despite her struggles, to the street his encounter with the outside porter . . and now the great car, leaping with tigerish swiftness toward the gates of Paris, Madden's hand on her wrist, holding her, hurting her. • · She screamed; but nobody save Madden and herself heard the scream. The interior of the car was pitch dark 104

CABARET 

105 except when the street-lamps flashed a transient ray of wan light through the closed windows. She had a dreadful sense of hideous imprisonment, as though the roof swung down upon her head, crushing her. 

With her free hand she groped wildly for the doorhandle beside her. She could not move it. Madden had snapped the locking lever into place and she did not know where to find it; and she could not see it in the darkness. 

Again she screamed and, turning, beat at him with her fist, beat wildly, futilely, striking him across the head and face. He did not turn toward her. He did not flinch under her blows. The car held on-fast . . . faster. . . . She might as well have struck at a machine set to run a certain distance inevitably.

She glimpsed columns, trees beyond. They were roaring out through the Porte Maillot, plunging into the heart of the Bois, going on and on. . . . Her heart almost stood still. 

Southward . . . through limitless distance under the night, the engine yelling triumphantly. 

She had ceased to strike at Madden. The blows had hurt her fist and had, apparently, not hurt him. She had ceased to writhe in her seat, to seek for the locking catch; movement gave pain to the wrist he gripped. ...

He lifted his other hand across the wheel. There was a click. A great triangle of white light leaped ahead of them, widening, splaying . . . until it lost itself. It showed the trees like fairy trees, touching them with silver as the car fled between them. It emphasized the fantastic blackness of the surrounding night as the car bored into it. 

They swung off the wide road. Their speed had slackened now. They were twisting in tortuous fashion through one

CABARET 

of those byways, tree-bordered and close, which are a feature of the Bois. They crossed another wide road, and dived further into silent, tree-locked darkness. 

Suddenly the engine's song died to a whisper. Its speed checked. . . . The car was at a standstill. 

Madden turned: "Now," he said grimly. "We'll talk." 

Marie found words leaping to her lips, mad words. "You'll pay for this. I'll see that you pay. Take me back at once. Murderer. . . 99 Madden leaned toward her. Cabaret Vibart. Quite a pretty arrangement—Mr. Fish coming round . . . the lights going out. . . . But I happened to see him first." "Do you think I did that?" gasped Marie. "Who else?" he said. “At any rate, it's ended now. Here you are, and here you stay, my girl-while I talk to you."

Marie crouched in a corner, surveying him as clearly as she could in the darkness. She remained like this for some time, and Madden waited for her to speak. 

At last she said: "You killed my father. I don't know what further deviltry you contemplate. I don't think I care very much. But I know this. I shall not listen to anything you say. And if I get an opportunity I shall have you arrested and charged with murder." 

Sudden emotion racked her. Her voice lifted. "My God!

CABARET 

107 go of my wrist. I'll walk back. I'll walk till I find somebody who's a bit above the level of the beasts." 

Madden's grip on her wrist tightened. "I was going to suggest to you," he said quietly, "that you give me your word you wouldn't attempt to escape, on condition that I loosed my hold of you. I suppose it would be futile to make such a suggestion?"

Marie said nothing. She looked out of the window, turning her back upon him, so far as his grip of her wrist would permit.

He pulled her round. His voice was hard. "Sit right—and look at me," he ordered.

With an effort she fought back tears. She still made no reply. "I see. Passive resistance, eh? Well, that, at any rate, lets me talk in comfort." "I don't want to hear you," said Marie in a low voice. "Take me back."

She fully appreciated her own helplessness. She could not hope to attract attention in this remote part of the Bois.. 

Madden's unusual strength had already been demonstrated to her. She was his prisoner, and she must remain his prisoner until he chose to release her, or unless some entirely unexpected assistance arrived. Therefore, it seemed that all she could do was to sit and say nothing. She would not listen to what he might try to say to her. Her conviction of his guilt was deep-founded and impregnable. Let him talk.. ... It might be the easiest way out of a dangerous situation. 

Her hatred of him was at blazing point, and was increased by her knowledge of her present impotence to harm

CABARET 

him. She dared not permit herself to contemplate the end of this adventure; the man was a brute and capable of anything. But she was determined not to afford him the pleasure of seeing that she was afraid. She forced herself, therefore, into an appearance of calmness. She sat quite still and cold in her corner of the car. 

Madden began. "I believe you're quite sincere when you say I killed your father. But surely, under the circumstances, you can hardly claim that I should be punished for that killing. You can't, in fact, be sure that it was I who did kill him."

Marie was tempted to ask him what the circumstances were. He had spoken as though she should know them. 

But she kept silent. She did not move. "Are you listening?" demanded Madden.

She made no reply. He shook her a little. She moved only when he shook her. After a pause, he went on. "Anyhow, I'm going to talk to you. Vibart. You know that, of course."

Again he stared at Marie for one sign of knowledge.

She was still looking out through the windscreen, and she might have been entirely unaware of his presence.
