As you know, he crashed through the balustrade and fell down the stairwell. The others dragged me back into the room, but I managed, after all, to beat them off and escape through a window to a penthouse roof below." 

Madden paused. "That, rather briefly, tells you the whole of my connection with the Cabaret Vibart, and exactly how your father met his death. Are you ready to believe me? I've told you the truth. I came to the Cabaret tonight to say what I've said just now-and something more. You see, if you're connected with the Marteau gang, I know

CABARET 

III you daren't go to the police. But on the off-chance that you're not, I've told you the story, in order to avoid the possibility of your having me arrested if we should chance to meet in a public place." 

Marie spoke at last. "You mean that your clever lies would not be believed by the police as you hope they will be believed by me?"

Madden was about to make a hot reply, but restrained himself. "You know they're not lies. You know all about 

Marteau. I'm willing to wager that you knew the whole story before I told it to you. I'm appealing to what sense of fair play you might possess. Whose, of the six or eight bullets fired in that room that night, killed your father? 

Was I the unlucky one? I ask you that, and . . . doing so ...I add this. If I'm arrested, so shall be all of the people in the Cabaret Vibart; Marteau, Fish . . . all of you. 

You were there-waiting below. I'm not without some standing in my own country, and though it costs the reputation of... the lady in whose interests I'm acting, I'll see that the lot of you get your deserts." ... 

Marie was shaken from her forced calm. “You would implicate me in the murder of my own father?" "I'd implicate you," said Madden, "in the whole damned business. You told me a fantastic story on the train, won my confidence, tried to pump me. You were there-waiting below, for the outcome of my talk with Marteau, for the springing of the trap laid for me; while your precious parent was among the gang."

Marie's hand lifted viciously and struck across his face. "Leave such talk of my father out of this," she panted. 

A bleak grin twisted Madden's lips. "All right. He's out.

CABARET 

He's right out." Madden was leaning toward her. His next words were spoken more slowly and struck across her brain like footfalls. "He's so far out, my dear young lady, that no news of his death has been permitted to leak into the pages of the Parisian newspapers. Think that over. Think over what it means to you to all those skunks at the Cabaret Vibart . . . when I tell my story to the police you summon for my arrest." While Marie tried to grasp the full import of these words, he added: "Cost what it may, if you try to arrest me I'll tell the truth. I shall just have to tell the truth. And you know it. So cut out the bluff of having me arrested for murder. You won't frighten me off that way. Who employs you in this game? Marteau?” ...

Marie was shaken to the soul. There was something about Madden . . . even when he was angry and violent in tone. His story-that story to which she had at first turned deaf ears-began to assume an aspect of verisimilitude, backed as it was by his threats to tell all to the police if he were arrested. Those threats had not been bluff. They had rung true. 

She said faintly: "Drive me back." It was all she could think of to say just then. 

Madden looked her over. "Well, you've heard what I had to say. How do we stand?" A curious, menacing calm had descended upon him. 

She laughed shortly. "How? Will you drive me back?" "You know the consequences of trying to get me arrested?" he asked. 

Her hands flashed quickly, despairingly. “Oh, drive back, for God's sake. If you tell the truth about the shooting,

CABARET 

113 how can I name you as the guilty one? I didn't know. 

Somebody killed him. That's all. And now drive back and don't talk to me any more." 

There was a subtle change in Madden's voice. He found himself wanting to believe in her. "Then this is the first time the facts have been told to you?" "Are you going to take me back?" she asked. "Yes." He was reaching toward the starter. "I was going to suggest to you, this evening, that you help me in the recovery of the letters and necklace I mentioned to you. I would pay well for such assistance. You see, you are inside; 

I'm out. Is it impossible to suggest it now?" "First you threaten, then you try to buy," said Marie, bitterly. "Drive me back at once, please. I can't stand any more." "Will you think my offer over? I'd pay five thousand francs for the pleasure of defeating Marteau." As she said nothing, he added: "I was a bit rough-tongued just now, I'm afraid. I thought you knew everything."

The car slid forward. Marie stayed in her corner, a perfumed, crushed and fragile thing, looking ahead almost sightlessly; for now her eyes swam with the tears she could not shed when Madden was brow-beating her. Madden did not see those tears. He was driving fast, and concentrated on his driving. They ran at last to a standstill before the lighted front of the Cabaret Vibart. "Here we are," said Madden, and leaned across to open. her door. Then he saw her face. "Why-you're crying . . ."

His voice was filled with incredulity. It was impossible that this woman of the Cabaret, of Marteau's confidences, should cry!

CABARET 

The door swung open as he said this, and Marie was gone, hastily drying her eyes as she ran across the pavement, forcing herself to some calmness, a fixed smile, as she came to the brilliant lights of the entrance hall. 

Madden drove away up the Champs Elysees, swung round the Place de la Concorde and stepped out of the car onto the terrace of Maxim's, where he took a drink. 

The girl was an enigma. He had not reckoned on tears.

He had thought her the usual hard-faced sort. . . . Rather curious, that. But he felt safer. She had believed him. He was sure of it. Against her will, perhaps, but-she had believed.

Anyhow, it was all a hell of a mess, and she was a pretty girl. He was sick of the whole damned thing. He thought he'd slip up to Montmartre for a while and forget it. He found a slim brunette in Zelli's, and drank a lot of champagne. 

Marie found herself, on her return, summoned instantly to the presence of Durant, Marteau and Fish. Fish, she observed, had got a nasty bruise on his jaw, and his lower lip was swollen. Madden had certainly picked him out in the dark. "What have you been doing?" Durant demanded, viciously. "We want an explanation, Ma'm'selle Delorme." "What explanation can I give?" she replied. "The man came into the Cabaret and invited me to drink with him The next thing I knew was that the lights went out and he carried me off. Am I responsible for that?”

Durant knew he could fasten no responsibility on her,

CABARET 

115 for the evidence of his outside commissionaire had made it plain that Marie had been taken away by force. "What did he say to you?" he asked. 

Marie looked straight at him. "He told me he was an

English lawyer named Madden, and that he is trying to help a lady who is being blackmailed by ... Monsieur 

Marteau. And he asked me to help him. I told him I couldn't."

This plain statement of fact was of greater service to her than any cunning lie. Belief was visible in their faces. "Why did he select you?" asked Durant. "Because I happened to travel with him in the train from London, and he thought I would be friendly with him." "And why didn't you accept his offer?" "To begin with, how do I know he spoke the truth? Secondly, if it were the truth, then a dangerous matter is being dealt with, and I don't wish to be mixed up with it." She looked at Marteau. "Is it the truth, monsieur?" "Why, no," said Marteau. "He is a dangerous fellow, that 

Madden. An enemy of ours." 

There was a short silence. Durant said: "All right. You can go."

When Marie had left them he turned to the others. "It was no use talking further to her. Further questioning would have shown her we were uneasy. It might have helped her to believe Madden's story. She may have lied when she said she didn't believe it. She may not have lied.

The facts, however, are these. He's in touch with her. Perhaps he's established friendly relations with her-perhaps not. But he mustn't see her again. He mustn't trouble us again."

CABARET 

He looked at Fish, with a glint of queer amusement in his eyes. "Fish, I expect you dislike Madden intensely, eh?

Well, I give you a free hand. Locate him-somehow, and as quickly as you like. When you come to me and tell me he's dead it might be a great day for you."

Marie was back in the dance-room. She felt uncertain of the result of her talk with Durant. His dismissal of her had been so abrupt as though he had made a decision and found further examination of her useless in view of it. 

This worried her, and yet the worry was subordinated to thoughts of Madden. She believed him. Try as she might to do otherwise, she believed him. She believed him, and she believed in him. 

And it hurt her that he had tried to buy her, that he thought she lied, while he fought for . . . a woman who was very dear to him.

An elderly man with the face of a hawk invited her to dance. She accepted the invitation listlessly. The business of 

Cabaret Vibart was going on.

Chapter XII 

AN HOUR had passed. Fish had gone out. Marie had happened to see him go. She did not know that the hunt for 

Madden was already in full cry, that Politot and the other bullies attached secretly to Durant's organization were being stirred to murderous activity at Fish's behest.

She had parted with her hawk-faced and elderly admirer on the most unfriendly terms, after resisting an offer from him which had sickened her; and she was walking slowly round the dais above the dance-floor unwilling, for the rest of that night, to dance with any others of the Cabaret's visitors.

As she walked she came abreast of one of the doors that led into the managerial offices of the Cabaret. She knew that employees were forbidden to pass any of the private doors unless they had been summoned. But this door was slightly ajar. Somebody had slammed it and the tongue of the lock had not quite clicked home. Had the door been closed temptation would not have presented itself to her; but the slightly open door seemed at once an invitation and an opportunity.

She was here to investigate the Cabaret Vibart, the dark secrets behind it-to wrest from among those dark secrets the secret of Constantin Delorme's death. And here was an unlatched door. . . .

She looked round. Everybody was busy, talking, dancing, 117

CABARET 

drinking. Nobody was observing her, though there was a crowd about her. She had slipped through the doorway and had closed the door behind her, almost before she was aware that she had obeyed her impulse.

Now all was deathly quiet, as it had been quiet on that night of death when first she visited the place. The soundproofing was cunning and uncanny in its efficiency. The 

Cabaret Vibart, as known to the outside world, might not have existed in that dim-lit and silent corridor with the stairway lifting at its far end. It was toward this stairway that she now fled on quick and padding feet, her heart thudding noisily and chokingly, her imagination fashioning nameless fears. 

She might be discovered. And discovery might mean death. There yet was time to turn back, before she was seen and asked to explain. But now she was at the bottom of the stairway, and now she was climbing it-up and up toward the dimness above her, away toward the top of the building alone . . . unimaginably afraid. • • The silence had closed round her like an invisible shroud. 

It clung to her, as a shroud might cling. And still she climbed.

She passed a door and shuddered. It was from this doorway that Constantin Delorme had lurched in death ... that night.

And so up-and still up. On another floor she paused. 

There were passageways right and left-dark and silent, with closed doors showing dimly in their walls. At any moment one of those doors might open. She checked an impulse to scream and flee, her face wan in the faint blue light of the dimmed night lamps. The stairway stretched

CABARET 

119 on-beckoning her. She had a mad desire to reach the top. 

She began to wish she had not come. 

She was past the third floor, nearly at the fourth when she heard a cry. It was a strange cry, ringing down the stairwell gustily, charged with the stuff of horror, so that she came to a standstill, clutching the balustrade, all life momentarily suspended within her. What-who-cried in this place of silence and of horror? 

Her feet were mounting once more, going to the fifth story, toward the place whence the cry had leaped through the stillness, to die in the second of its utterance; and so she came to the top of the stairs and onto the last landing. 

Here the passageways, as below, ran to left and right of her, their beginnings lit dimly by the blue low-efficiency lights; and here she paused, looking round her, inclined toward precipitant flight, tempted to remain and explore now that she had ventured so far. son of a pig!" 

She heard a voice. “Sign there 

It was the voice of Marteau. It came from her right. 

Marteau . . . ordering somebody to sign something.

Perhaps another victim of his blackmailing. She hurried in that direction. As she ran she was able to see that, some distance along the corridor, bright yellow slits of light outlined a partly closed door in the dead blackness of the wall. Vibart's 

Room? That secret room of which Julie and the others spoke with bated breath. Suppose it were!

She had reached the door, and stood before it, breathless, more than ever afraid. She heard a swift scuffle, a rustle of quick movement. Marteau's voice shouting an oath and a threat.

CABARET 

Then the door was wrenched open and a man, running, came to a sudden standstill as the light swept out and showed her to him, shrinking back toward the farther wall of the corridor.

She saw the man quickly, but not very plainly, for the light was behind him and striking into her eyes and temporarily dimming her sight. She saw his eyes open wide . as she saw Marteau coming on behind him. She saw his hands lift, and she heard a name pass his lips-in wonder and in thankfulness that was wrung from his heart. "Louise!"

Then the hand of Marteau was on his shoulder, while the man, struggling against Marteau's grip, was trying to reach her. Marie, seeing his striving, stepped toward him.. 

Marteau snapped: "What are you doing here? Stand just where you are."

As Marie obeyed this order, he pulled the man backward, across the threshold, and added to Marie: "Stay there. I'll talk to you in a moment."

With his foot, he kicked at the door and it shut with a slow and sullen slam that emphasized its ponderous weight and strength; so that now she stood alone in the dark corridor, facing the door, quite still, awaiting with apprehension Marteau's pleasure.

Inside the room, with the door closed and, therefore, shutting in all sound, Marteau spoke to Vibart. "Do you want to taste the irons again?" he snarled, shaking the prisoner savagely. 

Vibart struggled. "She's like my Louise! Just like her! 

Mon Dieu! Let me go. Let me go!" He made a futile move toward the door, which Marteau checked.

CABARET 

121 "Listen to me," said Marteau in a steel-cold voice. "You're going to hear a truth, Vibart, that's going to hurt, and hurt badly. That girl's not your wife. She's dead. You know 

Louise is dead." 

Vibart still struggled. "All these years I've dreamed of her... 99 

Marteau stopped his wild speech. “I told you to listen to me. That girl is not your wife, you fool. Has your memory so far failed that you can't recall your wife died giving birth to a daughter in England?”

Vibart relaxed slightly. Smouldering memory showed in his suddenly dimmed and pitiful eyes. ... “Why . . . yes. . . . Oh, God, yes. I remember."" He drooped, as though he would fall, and only Marteau's powerful hand on his shoulder kept him on his feet. "So. You remember that, eh? That's got home, has it? 

Well, get this home, too. That baby girl, whose birth meant death to your wife, is the girl outside. That's why you thought she was Louise. Because she's so like her. She's your daughter, Vibart, your child! Is it clear?"

Vibart turned slowly. He was much firmer, calmer, and what shreds of reason he had managed to preserve during his long imprisonment now showed quite clearly in his eyes. "You're lying, Marteau. I left my girl in England. I didn't want her. I never knew she would be so like-her mother."

Marteau dropped his hand from his shoulder. There was now no longer any need to hold him. "So you know I'm not lying?" He spoke steadily, evenly. "You know I'm telling you the truth. She's her mother over

CABARET 

again, isn't she? Well, I want you to understand this completely, Vibart. You started this place. It's going well. Some people have called it a little hell on earth. They may be right. They may be wrong. Well... your daughter's in that hell. Your daughter who is so like her mother, sells her services nightly to the clients of the place you created. The trap you set for the foolish and the unwary has ensnared your own flesh and blood." 

Vibart hurled himself forward. "You lie! You lie!" he shouted, and beat at Marteau with feeble fists. 

Marteau caught one of his wrists, twisting his arm with sudden ferocious strength, so that he bent sideways, sobbing with quick agony. "Keep your hands down," said Marteau. "And get hold of the truth. For it's the truth I'm telling you. Quieter now?

That's right." He gave Vibart a little push, releasing his hold as he did so; and the man went staggering a yard or two from him, clutching at his bruised wrist. Marteau went on speaking. "So this is the position, Vibart. We hold you and we hold your daughter. Think of her as Louise if you like. We hold her. Our grip of you is doubly strengthened." Marteau spoke more slowly, weightily, as though he wanted every word to sink home. "Before . . . if you did not obey us, you got hurt and you stood to lose your life. Now, if you don't obey, it's that girl's life as well that you trifle with. Is it clear?"

Vibart's tongue touched his lips nervously. He cast a furtive look over Marteau's face. "You're lying," he repeated. "She is not my daughter."

But his voice was not quite so assertive. He was doubting

CABARET 

123 half believing. The vividness of the likeness to his dead wife was a powerful argument. She was Louise come to life again-Louise whom he had loved so dearly. He yearned to have her in his arms. "All right." Marteau smiled. "Take the chance, Vibart. 

Back up your opinion. Now I'll tell you what you're to do. 

That girl, like everybody else in the Cabaret except Fish and Durant and myself, believes you, the great Vibart, to be the master of the establishment. The girl believes it. And she doesn't know you're her father. Like all the rest, she's afraid of you. Go outside. Reprimand her. Tell her she is to go downstairs and that you will consider if she should be punished." Marteau leaned forward slightly. "Fail to do this and I promise you that before morning I will show you the girl again—all that is left of her . . .' 

The room was heavy with silence. Vibart's hands were working at his trouser legs. Marteau meant what he said.

That was plain. He, Vibart, had had too long an experience of the threats of the triumvirate not to know that those threats were swiftly executed. The mockery and agonizing wickedness of this threat was very apparent to him. He had to threaten his own daughter. He had to pose as the brutal tyrant. If he failed to do so she would be killed. 

Marteau said: "Later, I will adduce proof, here in this room, that that girl is Marie Vibart. And now I want you to send her away. I'm tired of waiting."

There was a snap in his voice which forced Vibart to movement. He went to the door and opened it, his hand. shaking so badly that he could hardly clasp the handle. 

Marie was still outside. He looked her over, and she returned his gaze. At last he spoke. To Emile Vibart it seemed

CABARET 

that somebody else spoke-from a vast distance, in a toneless voice which he could not recognize as his own. "You have broken a rule in coming up here. Go down at once to the dance-room and stay there. In case you don't know me, I would have you understand that I am Monsieur

Vibart, and that it is an inviolable rule in this establishment that none of the employees are allowed up here. Now go. 

You will be dealt with later." 

Marie tried to speak. Vibart stopped her. "I wish to hear nothing further."

He could not, in fact, have stood and talked to her without losing self-control. He knew Marteau stood at his shoulder. The shadow of Marteau was across him. It was as though he looked into the eyes of Louise, across the shadowed valley of death. The pain of it was unendurable.

He wanted the girl to go out of his sight as quickly as possible. His emotion made his voice harsher than it might have been. What seemed like vicious anger in his tone was bitter outcry at the fate that held them both.

Marie turned away and went down the stairs. Vibart drew back into the room, and sinking into a chair, covered his face with his hands. Marteau stood over him for a second. "In a little while," he said, "I will bring the proofs, and you will realize how wisely you have acted. Vibart, you did well just now. You were splendid." 

Vibart looked up. He cried wildly: "Is there anything splendid left in all the world?" 

Marteau laughed, and went out, bolting the door behind him.

Chapter XIII 

AFTER the stealthy silence of the secret places of the great building the dance-room's clamor seemed deafening.

The band was going full blast. The place was packed. 

People were singing, dancing, talking, flirting, laughing.

Marie, making her way round the dais, not quite awake to her surroundings, her thoughts still dwelling on the ordeal she had just endured, and wondering what punishment the strange and extraordinary Vibart might mete out to her, found her arm seized and her progress checked. 

She looked down. A large man had taken her arm. He wore an evening suit which, somehow, made him look. grotesque. He had blond hair cut en brosse, and the flattened back of his head, the broad, slightly Mongolian features, proclaimed that he came either from eastern Germany or from Russia. 

He said: "Don't pass by a lonely man, darling. Stop and comfort me." Saying this he pulled her down into a chair at his side.

Marie had an impulse to get up, but repressed it. After all, she was here to dance with the Cabaret's clients. If she repulsed this man another would approach her within a few minutes. She smiled at him and, as she did so, she wondered if the smile appeared as forced and joyless as those she had seen on the lips of Julie and the other girls. 

The man talked. He had, Marie perceived, already drunk 125

CABARET 

sufficient champagne to loosen the strings of his tongue and his self-control, though he was not completely intoxicated. 

She should, she reflected, have been pleased. The girls liked the men to be a bit tight. It made them careless and generous. The traffic was so much easier when masculine brains were dulled by alcohol. She shivered and tried to listen. 

He wanted her to dance. She had already learned that he was Russian and was in Paris on some kind of business about which he was very vague. The band was roaring through a number in hot rhythm, and he insisted on taking the floor. Marie, within the circle of his enormous right arm, was crushed with sickening closeness against his gross body.

They were jostling round the floor with the other dancers. 

They went shoulder to shoulder, back to back, moving slowly, shuffling, heated, perspiring. There was no room for the proper steps of the dance, no room for anything save close embraces. Marie could hear snatched talk all about her, suggestions, replies, acceptances, persuasions, refusals. 

Her man was talking. She did not listen to him at all. The grip of his right arm hurt her—it was so brutally close. 

She wondered how long she could maintain her position at the Cabaret-how long they would be content to retain a girl who was unwilling to do what the others were only too eager to do. Her debts were paid-clients had been generous. There was no reason why she should not be dismissed if her employers were not satisfied with her. She had a feeling of intense tiredness as she shuffled round with the big Russian. But she must please him, or perhaps he would complain, and that might mean that an end would be put to her hopes of probing the mystery of her father's

CABARET 

127 death and the mystery surrounding herself. She must please him-please each man who chose her to dance and drink with him-until her task was finished. Then she could go -get away from all this vice, false mirth, rottenness. And thinking this, she wondered if, even when her task was finished and she was ready to go, she would be tied by her circumstances to the Cabaret Vibart. She had no friend in the world upon whom she might rely, now that her father was dead—no means of making a living, other than by working in the Cabaret.

In a city which still was strange to her she might easily starve, or slide over the edge of that long slope from the brink of which she still withheld herself. 

Her Russian was talking away. He said that he envied her luck-living always in Paris. It seemed he came from a village near Nijninovgorod. Marie didn't care if he came from Tombouctou. She wished he wouldn't hold her so tightly, wouldn't keep bending down so that his partly unshaven jowl brushed the softness of her cheek as his lips fumbled for her ear. 

Lucky to live always in Paris! She laughed as he said this, and he wondered why she did so. The observation took her thoughts away to London, to the extraordinary and unbelievable change that had come over her life in the minute when she booked that single ticket to Paris-as though she had known she would not return, as though she definitely severed connection with all that had marked her life with Madame Latiere. The folk she knew in London would, by this time, be in bed. Perhaps, that evening, they had all played tennis at the club to which she had belonged. Perhaps they had been to a cinema, a theatre. Perhaps they

CABARET 

had gathered in the club-house and talked quietly, lolling through the warm and twilit hours of the summer evening, with work on the morrow; very ordinary people living deliciously ordinary and secure lives, while she trod vaguely through a fantastic world where death lurked and men like this Russian fellow laid heavy hands on a girl who dared not let him see that she loathed him.

The band was still playing. She awoke to it again, and to her Russian's talk. He spoke English fluently. "My room is right on top of the hotel," he was saying. "It's a fine room. You'd like it."

She did not look at him. She was watching a tall and beautiful woman with blond hair and blue eyes who was being shown to a reserved table by an attentive waiter. She knew the woman, and wondered where she had seen her before. 

The Russian was still talking. "And there's a big, wide bed. My God, sweetness, you'd look grand in it.” 

She shut her ears and let her thoughts wander again. He was leering over her, his thick and fleshy lips gaping against his twisted, wetted teeth. 

That woman . . . who had just come in. Of course, she was Henriette, Marteau's mistress. Marie knew her now.

They all talked about Henriette and smiled sideways when they did so. She was, it was whispered, Marteau's mistress in truth. He was afraid of her it was said. She had fastened onto him and he dared not try and shake her off. Wellshe was superbly beautiful in a hard, fair fashion, with a tall and well-developed body. 

The Russian said: "Aren't you listening to me, darling?” "Let's keep dancing," she said tonelessly.

CABARET 

129 He laughed and hugged her very close. "For a moment, yes. You dance divinely." 

There was Ganier. Why had she not heard from him lately? Had the police abandoned their efforts to solve the mystery? Had the Sûreté, too, deserted her? She thought the police never gave up; yet Ganier had not been to see her for a long time. She decided that if he did not come within a day or two she would seek him. 

And Madden. . . . Somehow, thought of the Englishman uplifted her a little, and realizing this, she realized that she had, without any consideration, accepted his story as true. 

She knew that she could not disbelieve him, despite his cavalier treatment of her, and the knowledge disturbed her. 

There was no logical reason why she should believe. He had adduced no proof. Yet she believed . . . blindly, trustfully. . . . And he had said he fought on behalf of another woman.

Desperately, she resolved to think of something else. 

What would Marteau do? What was he doing? She wondered if he were still with the strange and rather terrible Vibart, who had glared at her so curiously when she came face to face with him, and who had dismissed her so curtly. 

As a matter of fact, she was right; for at that moment 

Marteau was leaving Vibart.

Vibart was crouched in his chair, his hands laid flat on the desk-top before him, his staring eyes fixed straight ahead. 

Marteau was putting some papers into his pocket. "So you see, Vibart, it is true. The girl is your daughter, beyond a shadow of doubt. Always remember it. Always remember that we hold you and we hold her in · • •

CABARET 

this splendid Cabaret you founded and into which she has been lured. Reflect on ironic justice, Vibart, while I am downstairs." 

As he went out he heard a little groan of anguish escape the lips of Vibart, and he smiled and sought Marie. 

Another dance had ended, and Marie and her Russian walked to their table. The heat and the gyrating movement of the waltz had quickened the influence of alcohol over the Russian; he stumbled as he trod the steps and put his arm round Marie. "Darling," he said solemnly, "it's time we went home.

I'm very tired, and I want to be cuddled and looked after. 

Come on." 

Somebody near by laughed. Marie flushed hotly. The man's arm tightened on her, lifted her, and in doing so, turned her so that she was crushed close to him, face to face. 

He kissed her on the lips, a devouring, slavering kiss which revolted her. Her arms were free and before she realized what she was about she struck his face fiercely twice. 

There was more laughter. The Russian loosed her and looked at her stupidly for a moment; and for that moment it was uncertain whether he would laugh or be angry. His tipsy brain inclined toward wrath. ... "That is an insult . . . from the gutter girl," he said thickly. "I buy your sort. I pay for your smiles. I can get blows from nice women."

Marie drew back. She wanted to run away. Now there was no laughter about her, and one or two of the men were murmuring against the Russian. A young American wanted to fight him. Drawing back from the scene, Marie can-

CABARET 

131' noned against somebody standing behind and, on turning round, she faced Marteau. "I want to see you," he said curtly. "Come with me.” 

The Russian pushed himself forward. "Who the hell are you?" he demanded. "Taking my girl!" 

Marteau smiled at him. “Pardon, monsieur. I am one of the directors of this establishment. This girl has broken a rule and I have to deal with her. A matter of discipline, which I am sure you will understand."

The Russian swayed slightly on his feet and regarded 

Marteau with stupid determination. "My girl . . ." he said slowly. "She won't be kind to me, but she's my girl." "Ah... that is the point." Marteau, behind his smiling easiness, was quick to seize the opportunity. "We are reprimanding her just because of that. Now, there are a lot of pretty and charming ladies here. . . . A moment, monsieur." He lifted his hand, and a waiter hurried to him. 

To the waiter he added: "Find this gentleman a very nice companion." As the waiter turned away, Marteau said: "Monsieur, if you will sit here the waiter will attend to you, and if you are not satisfied, and mention the name of Marteau to him, he will carry out your every wish. And now perhaps you will pardon me, monsieur. . . ." He inclined his head and turned away. 

His expression changed as he faced Marie. The smile left his lips. He said: "This way." There was immeasurable threat in the way he spoke the two words. 

Marie went with him through a doorway into the quiet and private corridor encircling the dance-hall. She was

CABARET 

thankful to be away from the Russian, but as she stumbled at Marteau's side, as his hand closed tightly on her arm when they reached the corridor, she began to wonder if she had escaped a comparatively trivial danger only to face one more terrible.

He brought her to the top of some steps, leading downward. Dimly she could see, at the foot of these steps, a short stone underground passageway, in which were the wooden doors of the cellars of the establishment. Marteau urged her to descend.

It was then that fear flared high within her. She stood still, straining against the urge of his hand. "What are you going to do?" she panted. "Deal with you according to the orders of Monsieur 

Vibart," he said quietly.

She fought frantically, but her struggles were of no avail. 

Suddenly her strength gave to Marteau's increasing pressure, and she stumbled down the steps, while he still gripped her arm, forcing her down and along the passageway until she reached the open door of one of the cellars. 

The cellar was lighted by an electric lamp, and round its walls were many wine bins. Hanging from the stone ceiling were two lengths of cord. Near them, beside an empty bin, stood a woman. 

She was a big woman, dark, with grey in her hair, and her face was the most repellent Marie had ever gazed on.

There was something foul in it which she could not define, which made her think of vague horrors lurking in darkness. Nobody else was in the cellar. 

Marteau, still retaining his hold of Marie, came to a standstill and said: "Here she is."

CABARET 

133 The woman's eyes swept over Marie, and then lit up slightly.

Marie was gradually realizing further details of the cellar; its quietness, the atmosphere of remoteness that dwelt in it, its sinister secretiveness. There was a spirit of evil in it which seemed typified by the woman who confronted her.

The woman stepped toward her and, doing so, revealed her right hand, which had hung concealed at her side. It gripped a whip. 

When she saw this Marie screamed. Her scream fled through the silence, was echoed and magnified a hundred times until it drifted to a whimper far away. She was struggling insanely, fighting desperately. . . . They held her, forced her toward the hanging cords; and as they did so the hand of Marteau tore at her frock, ripping its flimsy shoulder pieces asunder . . . tore at the single frail garment beneath the frock, and drew both down to her waist. 

When they got her wrists into the running nooses of the hanging cords she was half-faint with terror and with shame.

Marteau ran to the wall and tugged at something, and the cords tightened and ran over pulleys, until Marie's arms were stretched above her head, and she hung from her wrists, her back white and silken in the dim light of the lamp. 

Marteau spoke with a strange tremor in his voice. "Ten lashes, Marthe."

The woman nodded. During the hideous struggle she had not spoken a word. There was a lambent gleam in her eyes, a curious little flare that danced to the measure of the flitting evil in the black shadows of her twisted brain.

CABARET 

She lifted the whip-and Durant stepped into the cellar. "What's this?" he asked. His voice was not lifted. He drew slowly at a cigarette. Perhaps his eyes flicked appreciatively over Marie. But he showed no sign of any emotion.

The woman's movement checked, and the whip dropped to her side, the blow undelivered. Marteau spun round and began to explain. Marie had tried to penetrate the secrets of Vibart's Room. Vibart had ordered her to be whipped. 

Durant nodded. "I see. Well-you will cut her down immediately." 

Marteau did not move. The woman stood looking first at him and then at Durant. Durant spoke to her. His voice had lost its laziness. "Did you hear me give you an order? Cut that girl down at once."

She hesitated, again looking at Marteau. Durant took a step forward. There was decision in his movement. She dropped the whip and hastened to obey his command. When she had done so, when Marie was drooped in a little crushed heap at the foot of the wine bin, trying to huddle her tattered frock about her, Durant spoke again to the woman. "I'm not sure," he said, "whether these whippings serve any good purpose. In future, you must take your instructions from me. You can go." "Look here, Durant . . ." began Marteau. 

Durant ignored him. "I told you to go," he snarled at the woman. She hurried out, and Durant turned to Marteau, looking him straight in the eyes. "Marteau," he said evenly, "you too will go, and to you

CABARET 

135 too I will talk later." His voice became smooth with irony. "I will account to Monsieur Vibart for countermanding his order. That will be enough for you." 

Marteau hesitated. He was mad with rage, but had just sufficient grip of himself to realize that an angry scene before Marie might reveal things they wished to withhold from her. 

He said thickly: "I'll talk . . . as well." "Why, certainly," smiled Durant; and Marteau flung out of the cellar. 

Durant helped Marie to her feet. The girl was shaking and pitiful and Durant saw his opportunity. He was kind and gentle with her. "It must be a mistake," he said. "Vibart is a formidable master, but I can't think he meant to have you whipped.

At any rate, petite, it shall not happen while I am in charge under him. I will see him. And now look at your frock. 

All torn at the shoulders." "Perhaps I can mend it," she managed to say, and tried to smile at him through the tears which swam in her eyes. 

He patted her cheek. "We don't allow mended frocks in the Cabaret Vibart. No. Now let me see. You could get a really pretty frock with a mille note, couldn't you?" "Mille . . ." echoed Marie. "I haven't so much money as all that. And this frock didn't cost so much. Why, it's eleven pounds in English money." "Ah, this is Paris." Durant's right hand had appeared from his pocket and was holding up a twisted piece of crisp paper. "One mille . . . not from me, but from the

Cabaret Vibart, for a new frock in the service of the 

Cabaret Vibart. No, you must take it, maʼmʼselle. Really. I

CABARET 

insist. It shall be charged to the Cabaret's account. And now you will go home, and you will forget all this, and not worry; because before morning I shall see Vibart and all will be well.” 

Marie said unsteadily: "You're being terribly kind to me." 

Durant laughed. “And will a mille note ruin us? Perhaps

I am so ashamed of what has happened that I'm trying to put things right. Let me do so. It will really please me.”

Marie had to take the note. She felt that refusal would have indicated an ingratitude that would have amounted to baseness. Durant had her cloak fetched and a taxi ordered for her. He saw her away from the door and before the taxi started he took her hand and patted it. "No memories," he smiled. "And tomorrow night you can come here without fear. I give you my word. Au 'voir.... 99 

She went off, taking with her thoughts of his kindness.

He stood and watched the taxi out of sight, leisurely lighting another cigarette as he did so. There was a little smile in his eyes. The incident had served him, he thought. Perhaps he had won her trust. She must be grateful to him. It would be strange if the matter of a whipping and the gift of a few kind words and a mille note brought him Emile Vibart's daughter, the prospective owner of the place behind him, to wife, and gave him victory over Marteau and Fish.

Marie had not recovered from the shock of it all by the time she reached her room. Indeed, there she had a revulsion of feeling, a sweeping sense of shame as she thought of all that had happened. But through this, shining steadily, was a memory of Durant's incredible kindness, and with

CABARET 

137 the memory a warm gratitude toward him, a faint wonder as to whether she had been mistaken in him and could now call him her friend.

Durant had certainly made great progress.

Chapter XIV 

FISH and Marteau were waiting in the office for Durant when he returned from seeing Marie off the premises. He knew they would be waiting there and he went deliberately in to see them. There was a short silence following his entry; then Marteau exploded. “Can I assist in running this place if you countermand my orders?" he asked heatedly. "That girl needed to be taught a lesson. Did you overlook the fact that she was spying, trying to discover our secrets? Are you aware that she met Vibart face to face and that he knows who she is?"

Durant was about to reply when Fish broke in. "I'm telling you this, Durant. I'm sick of it. I'm with Marteau every time. Why, the whole damned game might have been blown if Marteau hadn't happened to be with Vibart just now. 

You know that door of his has only a bolt on the outside. 

Suppose Marteau hadn't been there. Suppose she'd pulled the bolt and had a talk with Vibart . . . !" Fish checked, apparently appalled by the magnitude of the disaster his suppositions painted. "Instead of supposing," said Durant, "we'll look at facts. 

Marteau, of course, was very angry. I realize that. I sympathize with his wrath. But whipping was going too far.

The threat of it was enough. Neither of you has any delicacy. Neither of you appreciates the subtle difference be- 138

CABARET 

139 tween being daring and being coarse and crude. That girl will never go upstairs again. Never. And because I intervened she will continue to stay here, and not give us a lot of trouble by trying to run away. She won't even as she might have done . . . go and tell the police Marteau tried to have her whipped." ... "She wouldn't have gone anyhow," said Marteau. “I'd have seen to that. Look here, Durant, I'm tired of your interference. I'm not going to stand for it—” 

Durant said softly: "Well, get out, then. I'm master here." "By God, you're not," snarled Fish. "Not over me. And 

I want to know, what's your game? Why did you interfere?" 

Durant laughed. "I merely took an opportunity. You see, you two are such blundering fools that you provide me with every possible chance. I have just played the knighterrant. I have just rescued the distressed damsel from agony and shame. I've been kind to her. I am buying her a new frock. She must begin to think me marvelous. And for all that I am indebted to you. I thank you both most exceedingly."

It angered them that the contempt in which he held them allowed him to display so openly the cards he played. Marteau's face was pale with rage. Fish's breath went swiftly and hissingly through his teeth. He made a movement as though he would jump. Durant watched them lazily. He was conscious that the air of the room was electric with threat, that his colleagues trembled on the verge of physical violence. And he smiled at them. "Let me see," he said, "that little girl, Julie, and her lover, the Russian fellow-Boris Kolotsky, isn't it?—will be

CABARET 

dancing just now. I think I'll go and watch them and leave you two to commiserate with each other."

He turned toward the door. With a swift movement 

Marteau confronted him, barring the way. "A minute, Durant," he panted. "We're going to have this out, here and now."

Durant slowly shook his head. "There is nothing to have out, as you put it, Marteau. Nothing at all. I've only one thing to say. I'm the master here. Not by virtue of Vibart's word, but just because neither you nor the animal at the desk behind me has the wit or the courage to displace me. 

I laugh at you both. So far as I'm concerned, you can both get to hell and stay there. And . . . with this . . . I warn you. I'm getting tired of you. Soon . . . I shall be too tired. . . . That's all." ... ... 

He brushed past Marteau and went from the room.

The Cabaret Vibart was still going on. He stood on the dais and watched the end of the dance which was in progress when he entered the great dance-room: watched the lights go down, the spot slash across the dimness, heard Jules announce Julie and Boris. Durant smiled as he thought of 

Marie. He had taken a great step forward that night. 

True, Marteau and Fish were doubtless talking treason in the office, but he cared not a snap of the fingers for either of them. He was a more deadly thing than they. While they skulked like the jackal, he stalked like the lion at whose heels the jackals creep.

Boris and Julie were "on." They were good. Durant surveyed them with a critical eye. Boris was tall and graceful, with a pale face and a shock of sleek black hair, a nervous, temperamental young fellow of the type who went to

CABARET 

141 Siberia in the name of liberty during the Czarist régime. 

Julie loved him. It was patent as they danced, as he held her in his arms, as they pirouetted round the gleaming floor. 

Durant saw it and grinned like a bleak devil standing back in the shadows. Love always amused him. It was so foolish. 

He hoped Marie would love him.

The dance was ended. The place thundered with applause. The lights had come up and Boris and Julie were bowing low, rather flushed and excited, with Julie very close to Boris, her fingers just touching his.

Jules glanced across toward the director, and Durant nodded. In response to the nod the lights went down. The dancers were to give an encore. Durant felt expansive. 

He was letting them have their chance. The little girl,

Julie, whom he had hitherto looked on as nothing more than a cocotte and the friend of Marie Vibart, was revealed as something of a danseuse. The boy was certainly very good indeed. He had a little of the true fire in him. Dancing like this was good for the Cabaret Vibart. 

They were moving again to the music of Jules' band, and the place was quiet save for the music, while all the people watched. Durant stood and smoked and thought of Marie... 

In the office Marteau and Fish sat and talked. Fish, as usual, was crudely accusatory. "You stood and let him hand you that, and you didn't try anything. I tell you, Marteau, that guy's going to get something from me that'll stop him grinning.” "Did you do anything splendid?" demanded Marteau. "You sat and said nothing while he walked out. But that's not the point. We've got a problem with three angles to it.

CABARET 

There's this girl. And there's Durant. And there's that Englishman, Madden. He also has his eye on the girl. And we do nothing. We—”

CABARET 

143 ries Marie. What happens then? Where do we stand?" "He hasn't got to," replied Fish, looking straight at him. "He's got to be stopped." Chapter XV 

DURANT's unexpected kindness made a deep impression on

Marie-the deeper because since he was the only man in Paris who had shown her kindness of any real sort. All the other men she had met were beasts, lustful or cruel in their different ways. Durant had intervened to save her from injury, had been considerate, gentle with her.

It was not until she got back to her hotel that night, in the taxi Durant had procured for her, that she realized the full extent of the shock her nerves had suffered. For an hour or two she was near to prostration, and almost made up her mind to abandon her quest. The hideousness of what she had escaped recurred constantly to her thoughts, in high and dreadful clarity, so that she shuddered and closed her eyes as though to shut out a baleful sight. In retrospect the ordeal seemed more terrible than it had been in actuality. 

And Durant... helping her, saving her, giving her money; being gentle and kind. She kept thinking of it, and as the horror of her torture was naturally lessened by the passing hours, her appreciation of Durant's goodness increased.

She began to wonder if she might not have made a mistake about Durant. After all, the terrible reputation he suffered from at the Cabaret Vibart was entirely unsupported by deeds, so far as she was concerned. Perhaps the 144

CABARET 

145 other girls feared him because he was the master-under Vibart; and the master is often hated, particularly if he is a disciplinarian. 

This passing thought about Vibart brought vividly to her mind a picture of him as he had faced her across the threshold of his secret and forbidden room. A strange man and, if Marteau told the truth, a cruel and terrible man.

Yet ... now she had time to reflect on it . . . it had not seemed to be cruelty that clouded his eyes so much as a dazed and pitiful wonder. And when a burst of light had pierced the clouds it was not so much a light of fury as of eagerness-an eagerness which was upleaping and unrestrained . . . until Marteau dragged him back.

She went to bed thinking of Vibart, and his drawn and eager face followed her through her dreams.

With morning she felt refreshed and better, and supported by her knowledge of Durant's kindness to her, she swept aside all her doubts regarding the possibility of continuing her work at the Cabaret Vibart. She must continue till the mystery was solved. She was still thinking of the difficulties before her when, at about eleven o'clock that morning, Ganier arrived. 

His appearance was so surprising that for a moment or two Marie could not greet him. He had absented himself so long that, as she told him, she imagined the police had abandoned the investigation. "The police," said Ganier with a gesture, “never abandon an investigation. They keep on until the end." "Then why have you been so long without reporting to me?" asked Marie.

Ganier continued to be slightly theatrical. "It is the

CABARET 

Sûreté which employs me, ma'm'selle, not you. When the Sûreté advises me to report to you, I report. When the

Sûreté says there is nothing to tell you, I tell you nothing.” 

Marie felt fretted and impatient. "Is my anxiety nothing, then?" "Certainly, ma'm'selle. But your anxiety cannot force us to work miracles. We've tried hard, I assure you. Not only I, but others. Monsieur l'Inspecteur himself . . . All trying. And with no great measure of success, and yet with some success." ... 

Marie cried out gladly. "You've discovered something?" "Yes . . . and . . . no." Ganier looked important. Perhaps he looked sly; but Marie did not perceive the slyness. "What is it?" she asked. “That there is nothing to discover,” replied Ganier, with a little bow. 

For a moment or two Marie was silent. The ironic and cruelly worded statement hurt her terribly. At last she managed to ask. "What exactly do you mean by that? And please try and talk sensibly."

Ganier spread his hands, and his eyes narrowed slightly. "Perhaps it is you, ma'm'selle, who might be asked to talk sensibly. We will observe the circumstances. You call in the police one night to the most famous night-resort in Paris. 

I arrive. To me you make charges that murder has been committed in that establishment, and you name one of the principals of the place as being concerned in the murder. 

This gentleman, I may say—I refer to Monsieur Marteaubears the highest of reputations, as does the Cabaret Vibart."

CABARET 

147 Ganier paused, and Marie, rather stunned by the line he was taking, had nothing to say. He went on. "Now I think you will admit that I gave you a free rein in connection with those accusations. We visited a famous and reputable doctor. Never once did I question the accuracy of your statements. I acted as though they must be true. And, with my colleagues, I worked . . . and worked. 

And the result? Nothing." 

Marie found her voice at last. "What you are trying to tell me is that my father was not shot. I saw it. I saw it, I tell you. And an exhumation would prove my words. But" -bitterly-"perhaps in the midst of your amazing activity you had no time to think of exhumation." 

Ganier spoke quietly, very seriously. "Exhumation was impossible. Constantin Delorme was cremated. I investigated that first of all." 

Marie looked him straight in the eyes. The answer had been so pat. "Is this true?" she asked. "You are at liberty to ask at the Cemetery, if you wish,” replied Ganier offhandedly. 

Again the room was silent. "So," said Marie at last, "so far as you are concerned it is ended.” "Oh, no. It is you who jump to that conclusion. But I will say that we are now beginning to wonder where fact ends and hallucination commences. That is all, ma'm'selle. You will hear from me again; and, if not from me, from Monsieur l'Inspecteur; though, for the time being, I am remaining on the case. And now I must bid you good-day."

Mechanically, Marie saw him off, and then went back to the room to think.

CABARET 

The police had virtually given up the hunt. They would keep the case before them, but they would no longer concentrate on it. That was the gist of what she had learned from her talk with Ganier. The people at the Cabaret 

Vibart had fooled them from beginning to end, and the police, not admitting defeat, were believing her accusations to be the product of hallucination. The bluff put up by 

Marteau and his people had been successful. Well, for a long time she had known that it would be successful. Marteau's creatures were so faithful to him. Soon after she joined the Cabaret she had spoken to Henri, the waiter, who was with Marteau on that fatal night—had appealed to him to tell the truth. She had tackled Legrand. Both had stuck to their stories that no shooting such as Marie described had ever happened at the Cabaret Vibart. 

And now the police had given up; but she was not going to give up. She felt more determined than ever, and as it was now lunch time, she prepared to go out.

She was lunching with Julie and with Boris, Julie's dancing partner and friend. It was really the first occasion on which Marie had had a chance of meeting Boris. She had seen him at the Cabaret Vibart once or twice, but then only fleetingly.

They were to lunch at the Fountain Café in the rue Gaillon. It was a splendidly warm and sunny day, and they sat outside on the little terrace of the café and scanned its modest carte with the great care essential to the spending of money not easily earned.

Julie was very excited, and Marie's heart warmed toward her as, having given her own order, she leaned back and watched her companions. Boris and Julie sat opposite Marie.

CABARET 

149 They sat very close together, and while they selected their food Julie's hand kept touching the hand of Boris. Then they interrupted their examination of the carte and looked at each other; and they laughed. Julie, thought Marie, was very much in love.

She rather admired Julie's taste. Boris was certainly goodlooking. He was tall and his profession of dancing had increased a certain pantherine beauty in his body. He had a shock of straight thick black hair brushed back from his forehead without a part. His face was a little pale, thin near the mouth, and his black brows were a straight line across his dark eyes. It was the face, perhaps, of one who might be rather fanatical in certain phases, but who would, at least, always be honest.

The food was ordered and served. A bottle of wine was opened. Boris declared that this was an occasion. He had something of importance to discuss with Marie. Both Marie and Julie were enjoying themselves, and Marie, in the warm shadow of the terrace awning, with the sun striking down blindingly beyond its edge, with two good friends to chat and laugh with, felt relaxed and happy. "It's your old landlady in London," said Julie to Marie, when they were sipping coffee and smoking Boris's yellow cigarettes. "Boris thinks—” 

Boris broke in. "Did you say her name was Latiere?" "Yes. Yvette Latiere. She taught dancing, you know. She taught me, as a matter of fact, both ballet and ballroom styles. I imagine she was quite a good dancer herself when she was younger-she often assured me that she was." 

Boris was looking excited. "You mean to tell me you don't associate her name with greatness?" he asked.

CABARET 

Julie laughed. "It was before Marie's time. It's only because he's mad about the theatre that he knows the name," she explained to Marie. "He lives almost only for the theatre. I'm only a secondary interest." She said this with the ease of the woman who knows she comes first. 

Boris was watching Marie. “Yvette Latiere,” he said, “was, in her day, the most wonderful danseuse in Paris. Like many others she just dropped into oblivion. Perhaps your Yvette 

Latiere is the same woman. If she is, then you've studied under a great mistress; which brings me to the point." 

Julie said: "Take no notice of him, Marie. He always talks like this. At great length. Let's have the whole argument, dear.” "It's this. Julie and I have thought up a triple act-myself and two girls. It's rather good, I think, but it calls for a girl who can dance really well. If we could get it going properly the Cabaret Vibart would give us a show and I think I could fix up a week or two at the Theatre 

Fantastique in Montmartre. It isn't the Casino or the Follies, but it's a starting point. Are you game to try, Marie?" .... "You mean you want me to go on the stage with you?" Marie was staggered. "Exactly." Boris smiled at her. Perhaps there was the slightest hint, in his smile, of a growing admiration for a loveliness pretty little Julie did not possess. “You see, we've not suddenly decided upon it. We've thought about it a lot. 

And we've watched you. We both want to get on, and we think you can help us, and get on with us. Give us an audition. I can get a room about four o'clock this afternoon, with a piano in it."

CABARET 

151 It was exciting. It thrilled Marie. She was going to be one of a little trio battling its way onward and possibly upward. There would be consultations, plans, practiceperhaps success. . . . She consented eagerly, and that afternoon, in a bleak and bare room behind the Boulevard des 

Italiens she had her audition.

Madame Latiere had taught truly, whether or not she was the Yvette Latiere whose fame had long since waned. 

Boris was enthusiastic. He kissed Marie's hands. He talked at length and largely, with much gesture. They were all going to be tremendously successful. They took another meal together. It was violent extravagance-lunch and dinner on the same day-but what would you? Their futures were assured.

Days of hectic and excited practice and rehearsal followed. Boris was teacher, critic, helper, as well as partner.

As a critic he was merciless. The time fled. Marie was amazed at the swiftness with which the days piled on top of one another. The Cabaret at night, sleep until near midday, a light lunch and then the bleak room behind the Boulevard des Italiens and rehearsal and rehearsal. 

Until at last Boris announced that he was satisfied, and went to see Durant. Julie and Marie were with him, and 

Marie was reminded of three excited and anxious children come before an old person for permission to indulge some cherished frolic. Boris was nervous in a delightful boyish fashion; Durant, as usual, bitingly calm and calculating. 

Boris requested his audition. He assured Durant he would not be wasting his time. They had an act most unique. It was sure to be a success-if only Durant would see them

CABARET 

some time tomorrow, any time suitable to himself.... 

Durant smiled and looked at them, looking last at Marie -and longest. “Well, well. So I might lose you, Ma'm'selle Delorme. 

You will be a great star, eh, and Kolotsky, here, will raise you to the firmament. All right, Kolotsky. Eleven tomorrow morning sharp. Jules and the band will be here. And don't prove to have wasted my time." "Why, no, monsieur." Boris babbled his thanks, then hesitated. “There is one more point, monsieur." "Yes.. ?" "I have already spoken to Monsieur Weber, of the Fantastique. I have appeared there once or twice when I was playing alone. He says he is willing to look at us, and give us a chance if he thinks we're good enough. It wouldn't interfere with any engagement you gave us, because we should be finished there before you needed us here. I thought I ought to mention it..." 

Durant nodded. "That's all right, Kolotsky. If I engage you, I expect you to be here on time. So long as you are, I don't care where you've been showing beforehand if it's not in a rival establishment. Tomorrow morning at eleven..." He dismissed them. 

One step on the ladder would soon be ascended. Boris was confident Durant would engage them, and, as he pointed out, that would be a recommendation to the management at the Fantastique. An act which was booked by the famous Cabaret Vibart must have something to it. 

Durant saw their turn the following morning. Jules and his band had been rehearsing numbers for that evening.

CABARET 

153 The interior of the dance-room of the Cabaret Vibart was dim and depressing, and no spots were used at the audition. 

Durant was in a morning mood, cross-grained and snappish, and he sat and cleaned his nails most of the time during their little show; so Marie decided that all their work was for nothing. 

At the end of it Durant said: “All right. Come with me, Kolotsky. You girls can wait in the vestibule. I don't want the place crowded out." He spoke shortly and yawned. 

Julie whispered: "Bad luck for us. He must have made a night of it last night."

In a little while Boris burst in on them, his face radiant. 

Durant had given him a contract. True, it was but a weekly one, but it mentioned possible renewal over a period of three months. The salary was good. They were, declared 

Boris, made for life. He dashed off to see the manager of the Fantastique.

The upshot of it all was that they had another audition at the little theatre up near the Place Pigalle, where the manager, already rendered favorable by their engagement at the Cabaret Vibart, gave them a month's engagement. 

All this represented a change in Marie's life which she found almost incredible; as though after that talk with 

Ganier, in which he announced that the Sûreté had abandoned the investigation into her father's death, the dark page was turned over and a brighter one encountered. 

They appeared first at the Fantastique. The theatre was old and small, yet Marie, on this first night, was thrilled -thrilled by its narrow and cheerless corridors, by its stuffy and dirty dressing-rooms, one of which she shared

CABARET 

with Julie and a girl who did a song and dance act; thrilled by the fact that an audience waited to see them across the footlights.

They came onto the stage. The theatre was of the intimate type which few foreigners attended. The audience was known to be difficult. Sometimes a storm of jeers and hisses arose, and on such occasions a long hook was protruded from the wings and the luckless performer dragged off with a comic indignity which changed the rage of the audience to laughter. Marie waited in the wings with Boris and Julie and shivered as the turn preceding theirs was concluded. Boris reassured her. Everything would be all right. 

She had not to worry.

The man who had just been playing came off amid indifferent applause. He was angry and rattled, and he cursed the audience. The band was playing the music to which 

Marie and her partners made their entry; the spots were flaying downward. They were on, and Marie's heart, like her body, was turned to ice.

Behind the flare of the footlights was a dimness dotted with white-a threatening yawning pit from which might leap shouts that would wreck all their plans, and subject one of them to the humiliation of the hook. For a moment -and a moment only-Marie was not sure of herself. Then the music took her and held her and she almost forgot the theatre and seemed again to be in the bare room rehearsing with Boris and Julie. They danced, with the spots following them; and no sound came from the yawning pit before her. They danced until they drooped to stillness; and then from the pit came thunder, the thunder of handclaps, cries of encore . . . a thunder that followed them as they bowed

CABARET 

155 ... themselves off, a thunder that called them back . . . once ... twice.. a thunder that told them they were successful beyond their dreams.

They drove down to the Cabaret Vibart, and there, confident now, they were as successful before its smart and worldly clientele as they had been before the audience at the Fantastique.

They had, in their small way, arrived, and the next week or two confirmed the achievements of this first night; for whenever they appeared applause greeted them.

Marie revelled in it. She had already liked Julie, and she began to like Boris immensely. He was kind and attentive and his eyes were ardent. He evidently liked her. He paid her a lot of attention.

Once, when he was being very kind to her, she happened to look up and saw Julie's eyes fixed on her and on him. 

The moment gave Marie a different viewpoint of her increasing friendship with Boris. She was a little afraid and sad. But she still liked Boris and it was plain that he liked her more and more as the days went by and their association became more intimate.

There might be danger in it. She was not sure. She found Boris attractive, and the discovery was obviously mutual. So they drifted on, extremely successful at both the 

Cabaret Vibart and the Theatre Fantastique, drifted, with Julie's eyes watching them, growing a little more strained and anxious, with Julie's laugh ringing just a little more loudly, her hand seeking the arm of Boris a little more eagerly drifted on 

Ganier sat with Marteau in the director's office.

CABARET 

"So I think that ends the affair," he concluded. "She's satisfied now, I feel sure, that the investigation cannot usefully be continued any further, and there's no reason why I should devote more time to the business of pretending that I'm a policeman." "What an excellent actor you'd have made, my dear 

Ganier," said Marteau, "if you hadn't found it more profitable to be a crook."

Chapter XVI 

IT WAS during these days of excitement and small achievement that Madden called on Marie.

Madden had been lying low for a while. His encounter with Politot and the roughs had scared him a little. They had come upon him suddenly as he strolled, thinking things over, along the Avenue Kleber, after a visit to his sister. 

Politot had come up swiftly behind him, in rubber-soled shoes, had seized him by the shoulder, and struck at his left ribs with a knife whose long blade gleamed in the lamplight. Only Madden's quickness of apprehension and action had prevented the blade from plunging into his heart. He dropped his left hand on Politot's wrist, swung himself round on his toes, and hooked a vicious right to Politot's jaw that sent that worthy reeling against the wall of the building that edged the sidewalk. Politot's head hit the wall, and he sank to the pavement, moaning horribly. 

The two ruffians who followed Politot were lacking in pluck, and their disposal was easy. The first received a short-arm blow to the stomach that doubled him up and sent him off in a crouching run across the Avenue, where he leaned against a tree, lamenting his hurt in a high, blasphemous voice. The other, seeing the fate that had befallen his companions, turned on his heel and slid off into the shadows. 157

CABARET 

Madden smiled grimly as he continued his walk, but the attempt on his life had disturbed him, and no less disturbing was the reflection that this first attempt might be followed by others. So he had decided to lay low in his sister's apartment until he could think out his next move. From 

Alice Rollerton's home he came to see Marie.

He called at her hotel at lunch time, and found her in. 

His manner was a little diffident, she thought, and reflecting on what he had said and done the last time they met, she was not sure whether the change pleased her, even though it ministered to her vanity. "I thought I'd come and see you," said Madden a little lamely. “I mean . . . the last time we met we had no real chance to discuss my proposal to you." 

Marie smiled. In the glorious days she then was living she could afford to smile at the night Madden had carried her off. "The atmosphere was hardly propitious, was it?" she suggested. "Not quite," he confessed. "But are you willing to listen. to me now?" "For a moment or two." She knew that really she was glad to see him, and she hoped that his visit to her was not, on his side, prompted solely by a desire to ask for her assistance. Though, of course, that hope was absurd. Are you willing to help me?"

Marie had become serious. Madden's talk had suddenly

CABARET 

159 turned back that page in her life which the hand of Boris Kolotsky had turned over. Once more she saw the dark and dreadful stairwell, heard the echoing gunshot, and saw Constantin Delorme come crashing floorwards through the shattered balustrade above which showed the face of Madden. 

Yet . . . Madden had told her his story and she knew in her heart that she believed it. She decided that she would enlighten him as to her exact position at the Cabaret Vibart. "I can't help you," she said. “At least not to any great extent. You're under the impression that I'm free of the whole place. Actually I'm not. You think I was on the train that day because I knew you were coming to Paris. Actually my meeting with you was pure coincidence. I'll tell you how I got to the Cabaret Vibart and all about myself."

She related the various events leading to her arrival in Paris, and what had happened to her since. 

Madden believed her. At their first meeting he would not have done so; but now he had had time to think—and to think about Marie-and he was rapidly nearing that condition when he would be quite willing to believe anything she said, however incredible it might be. "This Ganier," he said. "He seems to me to be a bit of a fool. Why not go to the Sûreté and put a jerk into them? 

I'll come with you if you like." 

Marie smiled up at him. "That's ever so kind of you. I appreciate it because I realize what you must have known when you spoke—that if the police do succeed in finding out anything there will be a great scandal in which you will be involved." 

Madden looked down at her earnestly. "Nevertheless, I

CABARET 

think you should go and try to put some activity into them."

And I now find that, beyond all doubt, he was a scoundrel. I have to admit thatthough sorrowfully. You see, I believe you." 

Madden smiled at her and his eyes were warm on her. 

She went on. "That being so, and while I am determined, if possible, to solve the mystery surrounding him and his death, I think I'm permitted to allow one or two other things to be more important for the time being. A quarrel with the Cabaret Vibart and a police hunt there might not only ruin my own chances but-and this weighs more deeply with me-the chances of two very good friends. We call ourselves Boris et 

Deux. That's Boris Kolotsky. He's simply splendid. And there's also Julie, my friend at the Cabaret. We give a show at the Fantastique as well." "Fantastique. . ." reflected Madden. "It's the little theatre behind the rue Blanche. Perhaps you don't know it."

CABARET 

161 “Oh, yes. I know. And you're there every night? I'll have to come up one evening and see you. What time are you on?" Chapter XVII 

MARTEAU pondered the situation. He had devoted a great deal of thought to it ever since his arrangement with Fish for the shipping of Marie to Buenos Aires, an arrangement 

Marteau had, so far, not been able to implement. 

For Marie had become a problem to Marteau. She had suddenly been metamorphosed in his sight. She had developed from a more than ordinarily pretty, but quite commonplace, dance-girl, among a score of other dance-girls at the Cabaret Vibart, into a clever and somewhat scintillating theatrical artist; one who might go quite a long way and become moderately famous and, therefore, doubly attractive. 

Gradually Marteau began to wonder whether it was altogether desirable to ship such a girl away to Buenos Aires.

There was about that arrangement something so terribly wasteful. Marteau would stand and watch Marie and Boris and Julie do their act, and his eyes would follow Marie as she moved across the cleared dance-floor; and the more he did this the less inclined he was toward the trade of Stanoptulos, the Greek procurer who was to put Marie's feet on the road to South America.

In connection with these considerations Marteau began to regard Henriette, his mistress. Henriette, like Marie, was a blonde. She was beautiful; but she had that suggestion of hardness about her which few blondes escape. Those who do escape it are nearly the most beautiful women in the 163

CABARET 

world; but there are not many of them. Marie, thought 

Marteau, was the only one he knew.

Marteau was a little tired of Henriette. He had suspected it for some time, and now that he was seeing so clearly this delightful Marie he was sure of it. On the other hand, it was debatable whether Henriette was tired of him. Women were curious in that way. Hideously loyal. Embarrassingly so, in fact. 

Marteau's thoughts now ran of their own volition. He was hardly conscious of observing their movement. He only knew the goal they reached. 

Suppose he took this beautiful girl, Marie Delorme, and made her his mistress? What then? She would be his, and what was rightfully hers would be rightfully his. He might, if necessary, marry her, though he had an aversion to marriage as involving definite commitments which a good business man would avoid, if possible.

But to possess her wholly would also be to have power over her. He would have beaten Durant, and he would have beaten Fish. There was something in it, apart from the greatest consideration of all, his now acknowledged and uncontrollable desire for her. 

Marteau did not arrive at these conclusions immediately. 

Day followed day, while the edifice of intention was slowly erected. It took him a long while, for instance, to decide to dispense with Henriette.

Henriette was not brilliant. She was only pretty. A man soon tired of her. Though without intellect she possessed a certain cunning which might best be described as cuteness. 

Henriette was cute. Her life had taught her to be cute. 

She was as cute as a business man. So that to contemplate,

CABARET 

165 for one moment, handing her over to Stanoptulos and suggesting her shipment to Buenos Aires, like so much merchandise, was to admit a sudden softening of the brain. 

Henriette knew all about the so-called white slave business, and she would never be trapped into it. Even if she were, the people on the other side would have a hell of a time when she was landed. And perhaps some of the trouble would get back at Stanoptulos and ruin him and his business. In fact, Marteau realized that Stanoptulos would not take a risk with a woman like Henriette, when he had such easy merchandise as entrapped and foolish girls to handle.

Therefore, Henriette had to be told. Marteau told her one evening, before dinner, in her somewhat garish flat near the rue de Faubourg St. Honoré.

Henriette had been about to dress for dinner and she wore a silken wrap over her scanty but expensive underclothes. 

She did not look particularly attractive to him. She was tall and rather well-built, so that there was a suggestion of grossness about her. Marteau was observing these things for the first time. He contrasted her with Marie. Also she used too much perfume. It was sickening. He lit a cigarette. "What's the matter, Charles?" she asked, eyeing him keenly. "Got something on your mind?” "Well . . . yes, Henriette." He examined the tip of his cigarette, and then looked up at her. "I've always been very good to you, haven't I?" "Oh!" She cocked her head to one side and stared at him until he dropped his eyes. "I see..." She spoke very softly, and the room went quiet.

CABARET 

Marteau fidgeted with the cigarette. This was awkward. 

Of course, she was guessing what he was trying to tell her.

His use of a cliché opening had betrayed his intent. 

She spoke again. Her voice sounded a long way off, and was a little unnatural and awfully steady. "Charles, if I told you I love you, that you are all my life to me... would it matter?" 

He took a deep breath. This was hell. Why couldn't women be sensible in these matters? After all, he had given her a fine time, and she must guess he was willing to hand her a good fat cheque when they parted. She had only to accept the situation with a little philosophy and everything was right. Instead ... "Charles . . ." There was that in her voice which was not so much insistence as pain. 

He looked up. "Are you sure you love me, Henriette?" he asked. 

She laughed. "You're saying that for the sake of saying something. Have you to ask it? Look at me. Oh, Charles. 

Don't say all that you were going to say. Don't. Stay with me. I'll love you-and love you, and belong to you... always, altogether . . . only you . . 99 Marteau wished he could point out to her that this was exactly what he wanted to avoid; but he could not do so. 

He fiddled with the cigarette. "It's no good." She spoke breathlessly. "It's all ended. 

You don't want me any more." Her sentences were flung from her lips between panting pauses. "It's not like that, Henriette," protested Marteau. "You know it's not. Only . . . well..." He looked vague. "I thought... that is ... ." What the devil did one say to a

CABARET 

167 woman one wished to discard and who did not wish to be discarded? Marteau could not think of anything. "You are quite determined?" Henriette's voice once more had changed.

He stepped across to her. "Look here, Henriette, this is as painful for me 99 

She flashed, in a sudden high scream: "If you say 'as it is for you' I'll laugh like hell. Keep your damned stereotyped phrases to yourself. I don't want to listen to them. I don't want to listen to your lying excuses, your assertions that you've looked after me well. I don't want to listen to anything at all." 

Marteau's jaw set. "I was going to suggest that we have a little financial discussion, but now... 

She broke in again, panting her syllables. “I see. Pay up and smile and nothing to worry about. That's it, of course.

She walked to the table and after standing at it for a moment lit a cigarette. She stood with her back to him while she did this, and she stayed in that attitude for a little while, smoking. When she once again faced him she was calm in a hard and glittering fashion. "Who's the new one?" she asked. "Come on. Out with it." "What do you mean?" Marteau contrived to look blank.
