never do without a woman, and I, of all women, should know it. So who is she? Somebody's taken my place. I want to know who that somebody is." Henriette leaned forward slightly. "Shall I tell you why? Because that somebody isn't going to find it easy to out me. That's all. Come on, Charles. Cough it up. Her name.” "There's nobody," said Marteau. “Really, Henriette, if you'd only listen to me, we could settle this little matter amicably."

As he spoke she stared at him, obviously without listening to what he was saying, obviously deep in thought. 

And it was as she stood like this, as the last words trailed rather haltingly across his lips, and a great silence came down on the room, that Marteau began to appreciate a Henriette he had never before known; a Henriette who was icily cold and detached; who was scorned, tossed aside, and, knowing it, burned like white fire with hate and poised evil wrath; who, in her cunning, might be dangerous incalculably, terribly dangerous, not only to him, but to all associated with him, to the great plot at the Cabaret Vibart, to everything which went to make up his life. 

As he thought these things Henriette's distant eyes lifted slowly and looked straight into his across the wreath of her cigarette smoke. She spoke slowly. "The matter, as you put it, will never be settled amicably, Charles. I tell you that from the first. So it's no use arguing. 

I've given you all I had to give." 

He contrived to sneer. "Are you now mounting a pedestal?

Don't try to lie to me. Just now you taunted me with trying to say that it hurts me as much as it hurts you. Don't give me an opportunity of taunting you. Don't plead the old

CABARET 

169 story of having sacrificed your virtue because of love for me. One cannot sacrifice something which is non-existent.

Her cheeks had gone a little paler. "Virtue is a word that need not pass your lips. And cruelty won't help you. I asked you just now who the new one is. I demand to know." 

Marteau laughed contemptuously. "There is no new one. 

God! You ought to appreciate that. After this I'm finished with women. They're not worth it." "Not worth your dirty money?" She looked him up and down. "No, the destruction of my peace of mind." 

Henriette laughed shrilly. “And yet you're taking on another. Do you think I don't know? D'you think I haven't watched you—and her? Henriette's hands strayed sideways and closed over the heavy cigarette box. She hurled it straight at Marteau's head. It hit his cheek, gashed it, and flying off, crashed against the wall. She was in a heap against the wall, crumpled and bruised,

CABARET

Marteau, panting for breath, adjusted his tie and collar, and stood looking down at her. "That's that," he said. "I came to settle things with you properly and, by God, I've done it. I would have paid you well in money, if you'd been sensible. Now-I'm through.

Marteau was shaken. He drew back and stood and looked at her for a moment or two. 99 

At last he said: "I'd still treat you well, Henriette, if you'd be sensible.” 

She glowered at him, and he went to the door. There he halted and looked back and seemed about to speak. 

She said in a breathless whisper: “Get out!”

He went, and in the quietness of the room she slowly settled further down against the wall, a crushed and shapeless huddled thing which stared ahead with blank and sightless eyes toward a road that must now inevitably lead to the outer boulevards and damnation.

Chapter XVIII BORIS ET DEUX, as represented by Boris, Julie and Marie, continued to be successful. Boris invented two new dances, and they got across amazingly; with the result that the trio looked forward to further engagements, not so much at the little Fantastique but at some of the bigger Parisian halls. Boris also had plans for provincial engagements during the quieter season. He had an idea of securing a cheap holiday by touring some of the north-coast resorts, from Fecamp eastward, during August and September. It was, he explained, done by a great many notable artists, and there might be a chance for them.

All this was splendid, but its splendor was gradually dimmed by something which Marie could not control and which slowly showed itself to be inevitable. 

Boris began to fall in love with Marie. He said nothing of it, but he showed it plainly, much to her distress, and much more to the distress of Julie.

There were a score of little ways in which he revealed what was happening to him. His attentions to Marie were too earnest, too eager for those of mere politeness and friendship. When the three were together he gave Marie precedence, attended first on Marie. Julie was cold-shouldered a little, left to care for herself. 

Now and again Marie caught him regarding her steadily, anxiously and, in doing so, was aware that Julie was watch- 171

CABARET 

ing them both, and that Julie's eyes were a little tired and drawn.

Through it all, the three of them maintained a pretense of perfect friendship and naturalness which, as the days went by, fretted Marie's nerves more and more.

She constantly took herself to task about the whole affair, and tried impartially to examine her position in it. Boris was growing to love her and she was not unkindly disposed toward him, even though the memory of Madden dwelt always in her heart. But Madden had somebody else, and Madden was rich in his own country, socially above her. She had long since resolved that while Madden, in 

Paris, might show unmistakable friendship for a dancing girl in a night joint, in London he would have considerable qualms about admitting that girl into his own circle. One had, she thought, to be sensible, even if one were nearly in love.

But Boris was of her own world, the world which had become hers. Boris was a good fellow, perhaps highly emotional, viewed from the remote Anglo Saxon standpoint; with an artist's temperament, but straight and honest and clever. Julie belonged to him, but they were not married.

If he ceased to love Julie Marie could not help it. The arguments were insidious, strong, tempting. 

In fact, Marie did not know her own mind. That oftrepeated lie that all is fair in love and war emphasized itself in various ways to her.

So the thing went on, and Julie's eyes were dimmed and 

Julie's laughter rang more and more false, and Julie's watching intensified to something tragic and despairing. But

Boris was happy, because Boris was making Marie smile

CABARET 

173 on him. So Julie did not seem to matter very much. 

One night Madden picked up Marie outside the Cabaret

Vibart—at least it was in the early hours of the morning. 

Now that she was established as a danseuse in a trio which was achieving some success, she had considerable latitude in the disposal of her time. She could leave when she had finished her turn. She no longer had to dance with anybody who asked her. Boris was strong enough to insist that his partners had to keep themselves fresh for their work each night; and Marie always left the Vibart long before it closed. Their act was an undeniable attraction, and Durant, pursuing his policy of ingratiating himself with Marie, was obliging and lenient. He often chatted with Marie, taking care to display the more genial side of his nature, and as he was crafty enough not to hurry matters, she began to think him rather charming.

On this night, as Marie walked out of the radius of light shed by the façade of the Cabaret, Madden's long and powerful car slid alongside her and Madden threw open its door. "I've been waiting exactly one hour for you," he said, leaning sideways so that Marie could see who spoke. “So you won't refuse to allow me to take you home, will you?" "I shall be glad," she smiled. “I'm awfully tired."

She got in and the car moved slowly and indolently along the Champs Elysees. "Well?" asked Marie, after a short silence. "And what progress have you made?" "None." There was a little note in Madden's voice which drew a quick look from her. "Is anything wrong?" she asked.

CABARET 

"Yes... and no. It's really impossible for you to help me, isn't it? I mean-inside there." "Absolutely impossible. If I could, I would." "I see." Again he was silent. "Won't you tell me?" she asked. 175 "That's fine," said Madden, and leaning sideways, he kissed her.

The action was so sudden and so unexpected that Marie had no time to resist or surrender. She just had to accept the kiss.

For a moment or two there was a queer silence for both of them. Then Madden said: "Have I offended you terribly?" "Why · • · 

I don't know." There was the woman for whom he fought. Marie was thinking of that other woman while she rejoiced in his kiss and wanted him to kiss her again. "Oh, you shouldn't, you know. Please let me get out. 

Please. . . .' 99 

He opened the door for her. "I'm sorry." 

She was on the pavement, facing him. "I'm not." Why she said that she never knew. "I'm not a bit sorry. Onlyyou mustn't again." "Then I can come to the Fantastique?" His voice held a high and eager note which followed her as she turned toward the door of the hotel. She turned, as he spoke, and he saw her smile through the shadows. The car went off with a roar which echoed the high gladness in his heart, while Marie fumbled her way in and thought of Boris and Julie, of Madden and the woman for whom he fought; and of herself. . . .

Madden drove round and round for some time, past the Madeleine, up the Champs Elysees, down the Avenue George V, across the Seine by the Pont de l'Alma, back past Notre Dame-drove aimlessly until his emotions had simmered down and he was able to think clearly and normally. When this desirable result had been achieved he

CABARET 

was able once more to face his own position. 

When he told Marie that he was confronted with an ultimatum, he was, in a way, right. Incidentally, he now realized that he had never yet explained why he risked so much to get the necklace back, had never told Marie the whole truth. At first, of course, he had been unwilling to do so because he did not wish his sister to be advertised more widely than was necessary; but now Marie must know everything, and he resolved to tell her everything at the very first opportunity. 

Meanwhile he thought he would go and see his sister. 

She was always up late. Her adventure with Marteau had not completely cured her, and it was quite possible that she had not been long in bed. He drove to her apartment. The time was then half-past two.

As it happened, Alice Rollerton was already in bed, but she put a négligée over her nightgown and sat up in bed, while Madden leaned over a chair and regarded her. 

She was inclined to be peevish. She had, in fact, been asleep when he arrived. "You're a perfectly impossible person, Peter," she said. "Has it occurred to you that one needs sleep occasionally?” "Occasionally," grinned Peter. "But I just wanted to talk to somebody, Alice. I've had a wonderful night.” 

Her eyes lighted up. “You've succeeded?” "Oh-that. No. Not at all. But just had a wonderful night." 

She eyed him critically. "If I didn't know you so well I should suspect you of being lit. You're not, by any chance, are you?" "Not on drink, my dear. Can I see Jim's letter?"

CABARET 

177 "It's in that little left hand drawer in the dressing table." 

Madden secured the letter and read it. It was written from the other side of the world, and it began prosaically enough "My dear Wife." James Rollerton never indulged in superlatives in connection with anything on earth. It went on to advise the said dear wife that the writer was sailing a week or two after the date of the letter-which meant that he was due in Paris in about a fortnight. It was signed "Your affectionate husband, Jim.” That, and its opening, were the only references to any throbbing passion which it contained. It was, in effect, a typical letter from a typical middle-aged British husband.

Madden folded the letter, put it back and closed the drawer. "Well-that's that," he said. "Jim's damned nearly on the doorstep and we've done nothing." "You've done nothing, you mean," said Alice sharply. 

Then her tone changed: "Peter, something has to be done. 

Really. You don't know how desperate I feel. Jim's a dear, you know, really. He doesn't spout much, and all that; but he's always right there. I've been mad. I know it. But if

I'd have thought I was going to hurt him I'd have cut my right hand off rather than have done it. I'll never do such a thing again."

Madden looked at her. He had heard these outbursts before. He knew they contained an ephemeral truth. When she spoke she meant what she said. But she had a bad memory. Nevertheless, in her feather-headed way she loved 

Jim Rollerton. What was more important, in his big wholehearted way, Jim loved her. 

Madden spoke slowly. "There's one thing to be done

CABARET 

now," he said. "It's pretty desperate, and it's taking a risk; but I'm going to do it." 

She eyed him straightly. “Not a risk, Peter. I don't want that. I'd rather go through with it." "I know you would." He spoke more gently. "And I appreciate it. But there's my viewpoint to be considered, and the viewpoint of somebody else. First of all, I'm not going to be done by a swine like Marteau. I'm not going to pay meekly, and grin and bear it. Then there's Jim. He's firstclass. If he thought, or suspected, all this, it would be hell for him; and I'm going to do my best to see that he doesn't suffer hell. Shall I tell you the idea?” "I'll listen. But if it involves risk I beg of you to abandon it. What is it?" 

He leaned a little farther toward her. "I'm going to break into the Cabaret Vibart early one morning, after it's closed down; and I'm going right through it like a fine-toothed comb. Perhaps I'll find the necklace and letters. Perhaps I shan't. Perhaps I'll find something to offset Marteau's threats.

There's mysterious talk of a strange and secret and always locked room, high up in the building there. I'm going into that room. That's the idea, Alice." "Peter..." She breathed the name. 

Madden straightened himself. “And now I'll let you get that beauty sleep which, if I were very gallant, I should assure you you do not need. Cheerio, Alice." 99 

She cried: "Peter! You mustn't! For God's sake..

But he had reached the door. He waved his hand to her, smiling, and went out. She sank back on her silken pillow and stared across the splendid room, now heavy with silence and, seemingly, pregnant with threat.

CABARET 

179 Peter-because of her-would break into the Cabaret Vibart, would walk with death, would wrest from its fastening the secret of the locked room. . . Because of her folly.. 

The silence of the room closed round her like a close impregnable prison, and held her fast.

Chapter XIX 

FISH received a telephone call. It was from an unexpected quarter, from a lady who had, hitherto, bestowed on him only those smiles which courtesy demanded. It was from 

Henriette. Normally Henriette regarded Fish as a lowdown tough who was hardly worthy of being noticed by a lady so high in her ancient profession as herself. Now Henriette, completely unfriended, aware that there was some strange mystery attached to Marie Delorme, aware also that Fish was on the inside of that mystery, addressed herself to him. 

Fish received the call at home. He lived in an apartment in the rue St. Florentin. His wife lived with him, and her spinster sister. They were a formidable pair. 

Henriette said: "Jake, I've something to talk to you about. 

It's important." "Spill it," said Fish graphically. "That girl, Delorme." Henriette was inclined toward incoherence. "Charles is after her. He is throwing me over. 

He is taking her instead." 

Fish yelped: "What's that?” 

Henriette repeated her information, and went on: "I want you to help me, Jake. I can't lose him. I just can't.

Isn't it possible to get that girl out of the way before it's too late? You know what Charles means to me." "A hell of a lot of money," said Fish agreeably. "I've got you." "I love him," she said fiercely. 180

CABARET 

181 

Fish said: "Oh, yeah?" And thought for a moment or two. 

Then he added: "This is straight? Marteau's going to hitch up with the Delorme girl? You're sure?” "He told me so himself," said Henriette, and felt that her slight exaggeration of Marteau's statement to her was justified. "He told you, eh?" Fish's tone had changed. "Listen, Henriette. You lay back and keep quiet. Leave it to me, and keep all that's just been said under your hat-if you can.” “Oh, Jake . . .” Her voice was filled with surging gratitude. "You said it," agreed Fish, and hung up on her.

He went for a walk, and thought hard. 

All of his thoughts cannot be set down, as some of them are unprintable. First and foremost he considered Charles 

Marteau-Marteau who had promised to get Marie shipped away to South America and had, all the time-the dirty, lowdown, four-flushing swine-been playing his own game, laying for the girl, coveting her, deciding to make her his own; thereby double-crossing his friend and ally Fish, and putting him in the dirt. 

Fish no longer thought of Marteau as Marteau, but referred to him in his thoughts from then on as that bastard. 

So the thoughts progressed.

In this progression they flitted transiently to his own position in the affair. Married and, so to speak, done for. He had married a Frenchwoman. Her father had owned an estaminet in an area wherein Fish was quartered during the war. 

She had then been plump, desirable in a fleshy and abundant fashion, and, into the bargain, marriage with her had thrown open, free, the pleasures of the estaminet to Fish. Now she

CABARET 

was older, had grown hard, as a great many Frenchwomen do grow hard, she had reinforced her position in his unhappy household by importing her sister, a replica of herself, but slightly more formidable. 

Fish had often toyed gently with the idea of murdering his wife, but the presence of the sister negatived any such design. Though Marthe Fish knew her husband's conduct with women was coarsely loose, she could not, for religious reasons, divorce him, even if she had wished to give him his freedom, which she didn't, since she valued his income. He was tied to her. 

Durant could plan to marry Marie, Charles Marteau could also decide to marry her, or, at the least, to make her his mistress; but Fish could do neither of these things. His women-folk wouldn't let him. Fish was out of it. 

Having outlined his own position to himself, Fish reverted to Marteau, and experienced a sense of rage that was all the more bitter because he recognized its futility. What could he do to Marteau?

There was nothing too bad for the skunk. Shooting him dead was a mild punishment compared with what really should befall him. But shooting him was dangerous, and it did not put Henriette right. Not that Fish worried about putting Henriette right. He worried about Henriette's reactions to Marteau's murder. 

Henriette had told him Marteau was cheating her. It was plain, also, that Henriette had a good idea of the plot at the Cabaret Vibart. Henriette was shrewd enough to put two and two together, and Fish did not want her denouncing him to the police for depriving her of her meal-ticket, in the shape of Marteau.

CABARET 

183 

No. The idea of killing Marteau was no use. 

But was there no other way? Fish's coarse and evil brain groped into further darknesses as he strolled through the sunshine of the Tuilleries gardens. 

Kill the Delorme girl . . . 

That was the way. Smash the whole thing with one great stroke. Show Durant and Marteau where they got off the tram, and how to put things right for all three of them. Flick away, on the wings of a bullet, the peril to their grip on the lucrative Cabaret Vibart.

Fish sat down and watched, with pondering eyes, a vendor of questionable picture postcards who was secretively showing his wares to an American tourist, and thought . . and thought... ...

The outcome of his thoughts was that round about lunch time he sought a friend. Perhaps friend is the wrong word. 

The gentleman he looked for had never been a friend to anybody; but he could be, in this emergency, useful to Fish.

In one of the narrow streets on the right hand side of the rue de la Paix as you walk toward the Place Vendome, there is an American Bar. It is a bright place and a chattering crowd gathers there at the hour of the apéritif. Fish walked into it.

A tall man was leaning against the bar. He wore a flashy suit and had a wide-brimmed, soft felt hat cocked on the back of his head, disclosing that his hair was the color of ginger. He had a clean-shaven face, reddened and blued, with tiny veins all over it, grey eyes like glass, and a mobile, wide and ugly mouth. 

He greeted Fish like a brother and Fish stood him a drink. "I wanna talk to you, Reddy," said Fish. “And not here.

CABARET 

It's important. A big job. I'll stand you in two thousand francs if you pull it off."

Now Reddy Backs, though he looked prosperous in his own flashy and undesirable fashion, was, as Fish knew, always on the financial danger line, and two thousand francs was real money to him. Reddy spoke a strange argot which he imagined to be distinctly Transatlantic. Actually, he was an English race-course thug who had made Britain too hot to hold him. "I'm in for a couple of thousand whatever it is," he said. "Have another, Jake." 

Fish shook his head. "Not here. Let's eat somewhere where we can talk. Come on." 

They went out, Reddy exchanging farewells cheerily with various habitués of the establishment, and Fish led him to the deserted terrace of a small café just round the corner, where they ordered lunch, and where they selected a table at which they could talk quite freely and without being overheard. "What's the big idea, Jake?” asked Reddy, after the food was served. "Somebody's got to be bumped off," said Fish quietly. 

Reddy hesitated. ""Zat so? Who ... ?" “A girl." 

Reddy's eyes brightened. "I got you." "It'll be easy," said Fish. "I'll be with you. But I wanna make sure. Two guns are better than one, specially in the dark; and in the dark it's gotta be done." "Where?" asked Reddy. "Montmartre. In a street behind the rue Blanche."

CABARET

And Fish guffawed and ordered the third pair of brandies.

They walked. Reddy rolled the slightest bit, but Fish did not heed it. Now that the hour had come upon them Fish was strangely and awfully tense, strung up to the pitch of murder. While Reddy talked, Fish said nothing. Death strode on through the dark streets.

They came at last to the dim little cul-de-sac behind the 

Theatre Fantastique. All was quiet. The street was utterly deserted, palled with darkness.

They waited against the corner and watched the stagedoor. It was only a few yards away from them.

CABARET 

The wait seemed long to Fish and Reddy. There appeared to them to be an uncanny silence on the whole neighborhood, as though the tall and flat-faced, shuttered houses drew back slightly, breathless, watchful and afraid. To right and left of them the street that crossed the entry of the culde-sac drifted away into deepening obscurity wherein nothing moved.

Reddy vaguely began to wish he had carried the steadying process a bit further. He ought to have had another brandy. 

Fish was ice-cold, poised, wicked. "Can't be long now," he muttered. "Keep steady, Red. 

Got your gat?" "Sure," said Reddy, and showed how he had his automatic slipped up his coat sleeve, ready to slide down into his grip at the right moment. Fish grunted approval. 

They heard voices, a girl's laugh, a man's deeper tones.

The sounds came from within the theatre, out through the stage-door. 

Fish hissed: "Here they are!"

Reddy tried to take a big grip of himself, but the measures he had adopted to ensure steadiness were now taking their toll of his nerves and head. His head felt dazed and his nerves jangled. He ought to have had at least one more brandy. 

They heard a girl say: “Boris, let's walk part of the way. 

We've plenty of time tonight. Several minutes in hand. And it's so warm." 

They were out-the three of them.

Reddy saw them as through a strange red mist across the intervening space of darkness-two girls with cloaks about them, and a man in a long coat. Their stage make-up was still on their faces. One of the girls drew slightly away from

CABARET 

191 her companions, and Fish said in a hoarse whisper: “That's her." 

As he spoke Reddy fired.

It was too soon, too quick for careful aim; a nervous blazing at a moving target. The bullet went across Marie's shoulder and whanged the brickwork behind her. 

With a cry, Boris stepped in front of her, putting his arms about her, holding her close, himself between her and the dim figures of the two assassins.

Fish, who had lifted his hand, dropped it, for now he must hit Boris before he could hit Marie.

All this took place in the space of time during which one might snap a finger and thumb-the shot, Boris's quick movement, Marie's inarticulate scream, Julie's shout for help. 

Fish yelled: "God damn you! You've bloody-well wrecked it."

Reddy threw his gun forward again, and doing so, began to move toward the stage-door. He was mad now, his nerve and brain temporarily out of action, an automaton moving to a killing. 

Boris, holding Marie, was crying: "Dear-my dear. You're all right. You're not hurt. Oh, my dear . . ."

And now Julie did not scream, but was flat against the wall staring at Boris through bleak and agonized eyes, while 

Boris still held Marie.

Only seconds had fled; seconds charged with the stuff of all eternity. Love was wrecked, death had swooped and missed, gunshot had brought high clamor to the little street; and only seconds had passed.

The stage-door keeper was outside now. Marie was running past him through the doorway. The man rushed at

CABARET 

Reddy, and Reddy, seeing that failure had become certain, the last dregs of his brandy-sustained courage completely evaporated, turned and bolted. Fish, yelling with mad and unleashed fury, was taking a step forward into the roadway, with the idea of firing a last shot, when a great car roared round the corner, its brakes screaming as they were slammed on.

Its lights, sweeping along the walls in a wide arc, flicked across the face of Fish and passed him. The game was up. 

Fish dropped his gun and fled for his life. He had a reason for dropping his gun. If, a street or two ahead, he were caught, he could swear he had not been implicated in the affair. If a gun were found on him he might be disbelieved.

The car was at a standstill and Madden tumbled out. He had come to keep his long-promised appointment with Marie. He found Marie in the passageway just beyond the stage-door. She was very pale, but absolutely unhurt, and 

Boris was carefully tending her. 

She gave Madden a smile, and Boris said: "She was not hit, monsieur. She is unhurt, but very frightened." He turned to Marie. "Darling-they are waiting at the Cabaret Vibart." 

In a louder tone he called to the stage-door keeper to telephone for a taxi. 

Madden began: "I'll . . ." Then he checked and added: "You can get down in a taxi all right?" "Yes, certainly," replied Boris.

Madden was about to speak to Marie when he remembered how Boris had just spoken to her. He was conscious of awkwardness and dismay. 

He said: "Well, I'll be off. I want to catch somebody up. 

You are all right, Marie?"

CABARET 

193 "Yes-now. Thanks . . ." She spoke uncertainly.

Madden ran out to his car. He reversed it with a roar into the street and sped down the hill toward the Opera. 

He had not noticed a deeper shadow among the shadows of the cul-de-sac-Julie still standing against the wall, her face uplifted toward the stars, seared with an agony which would leave its mark on her soul.

Chapter XX 

MARIE knew she was dancing. Beyond that she knew little, for her thoughts still dwelt in the sinister, shot-rent darkness of the narrow street behind the Theatre Fantastique in Montmartre.

She knew she danced in the Cabaret Vibart, that colored lights played down on her, that in the dimness beyond their glowing beams were faces, the white shirt fronts of well-dressed men, the gems of elegant women. She felt in every fibre of her body the throb of the music to which she danced. She knew there was, save for that music, silence on the place. And she knew that with her danced Julie, her face very white and set and wooden, her eyes like deep pools across which the moon has stooped sadly. 

All these things she knew-and yet she still seemed to be in the dark street, still seemed to hear the crashing echoes of the gunshot, still seemed to see the lurching figures in the shadows and to hear the sudden high roar of the powerful car engine.

They had come down to the Cabaret Vibart in a taxi, for, whatever happened, the show must go on. Boris had comforted her on the way down. She had cuddled within the shelter of his encircling arm, her head on his shoulder, her body quivering under his hand, quivering with terror and nervous reaction; while Julie had sat on one of the 194

CABARET 

195 little tip-up seats, vague in the darkness of the cab's interior, terribly mute and set. 

And now they danced-and danced-with the slow rustle of the wings of the Angel of Death still in their hearing, and the clientele of the Cabaret Vibart watching them. 

It ended at last, ended with a thunder of applause, a thunder that racked her head and jangled her nerves. They were recalled. More was demanded of them-when she only wanted to run away and crouch somewhere quiet and safe and secret.

They danced again. As they danced she met the eyes of 

Boris. He smiled anxiously, reassuringly. It was as though he whispered that the ordeal must soon be over, that she must be brave and carry on.

There was more applause. They had gone across well. 

They had achieved another success, and they were bowing themselves from the floor. They hurried through the corridors to their dressing-rooms and as they went Boris said: "I've got to go straight away and see Durant. He's furious,

I believe. Wants to know why we were so nearly late. I'll explain. I'll come and see both of you after I've talked to him."

He left them, and Julie and Marie entered their dressingroom. There Marie sat down by the table and put her head on her hands.

Julie made no effort to change from her stage clothing. 

She stood back and looked at Marie, and continued to look, though Marie's face was buried in her hands. So the room was very quiet. 

Julie broke its silence. Her voice sounded very unnatural, far away, and was steady in an awful, suffering fashion.

CABARET 

"I heard what Boris said to you outside the Fantastique."

The words seemed to hang on the silence of the room as did the little checked sob on which the speech ended. 

Marie slowly lifted her head from her hands and looked at Julie. There appeared to be no need for more words between them. Julie's statement had merely brought into the open what both of them had secretly known for a long time. "I heard..." repeated Julie, and then cried, suddenly, pitifully: "Oh, God, I don't know what I shall do. I don't know what I shall do."

She drooped to the floor beside a chair, her arms on the seat of the chair and her face buried in her arms. Gusty and terrible sobbing shook her, unrestrained and racking.

Marie came across to her. She was forgetting now the dark street and the bullet's high whisper as it passed her, and was remembering Boris's words to her. "Julie," she said. “Julie.” "Go away," sobbed Julie. "Don't speak to me. Don't touch me. Go away. You've taken him from me. You've broken my heart, and you know it. I wish I'd never lived to this night. I wish I'd died. I wish that man had shot me." "Julie," repeated Marie. "You mustn't, you know. Really. 

Boris lost his head. He meant nothing." 

Julie turned, clinging to the chair. "Nothing!" she echoed. "You dare say that? When his words meant that I was just thrown away without a thought! 

I don't want you to stay in this room with me. I can't have you near me. You've ruined everything, and I just can't bear to look at you."

There was a wildness about her which frightened Marie.

CABARET 

197 At the same time it showed that Julie, for the moment, was not to be comforted. Marie drew back from her, and without saying another word, began to change into her outdoor clothing.

Julie, after a little while, did the same. The room remained quiet. Now and again, and because it was impossible to avoid doing so in the small room, Marie looked at Julie. 

Julie's face was deathly white and set. It was like stone. 

She was putting on her clothing mechanically.

Marie observed that she did all the little things in dressing which she normally did. Habit asserted itself, even in this hour of her Calvary, and would not be denied. She carefully patted her hair into place after as carefully adjusting her little hat. She used her lipstick with careful skill, though her eyes did not appear to look at the mirror, but through it. 

She was dressed at last. Boris had not returned from his interview with Durant. Julie picked her handbag up. "I'm going," she said. "Home. . . ." 

Marie, also dressed, tried to appeal to her. "Won't you wait?" she asked. Julie knew she meant wait for Boris. 

Julie shook her head. "Do you wait at a grave for the dead to rise?" she asked, and walked out, leaving Marie alone.

Chapter XXI 

FISH had fled toward the Cabaret Vibart. He was rather shaken. He had taken the precaution of wearing his dress clothes under a dark overcoat during his interlude with Reddy, and so, after calling at a bar in the rue Blanche and taking a couple of drinks to steady his nerves, he was properly attired to return straight to the Cabaret. He would take his accustomed place there, and there would be no reason why anybody should associate him with the happenings at the Theatre Fantastique.

He was in a morose and evil temper, cursing himself for having employed Reddy, and cursing Reddy for being a bungling drunken fool. For now he realized that the brandy 

Reddy had drunk prior to meeting him had robbed Reddy's nerves of just that last ounce of poise essential to the successful prosecution of such an enterprise as they had undertaken.

He did not hire a taxicab. He did not want anybody to be able to say they had driven Jake Fish to or from Montmartre that night. The bar where he had stopped was one in which he was not known. He had pulled his hat well down over his eyes, and had stepped unobtrusively to the dark end of the counter before ordering his drinks. Now, as he walked, he kept in the shadows. Nobody would know where Jake Fish had been.

True, the headlamps of the big car that had interrupted 198

CABARET 

199 the shooting had swept his face, but only momentarily, and he reflected that the driver probably would not be able to recognize him, for the happenings had all been so swift. 

He had reached the Champs Elysees and was passing the 

Marigny gardens, feeling very hot and tired and savage, when a car ran alongside him in a dark patch between two lamps. Madden leaned out and spoke to him. "I've been waiting for you, Fish.”

Fish checked in his hurried walking and regarded him morosely. "Why?" "Get in and I'll tell you." 

Fish pursed his lips. "I don't want to. I'm in a hurry. The show's on."

Madden slipped out of the car and confronted him. “Get in," he said, and looked straight into Fish's eyes. 

Fish stared at him, glanced round swiftly to see that nobody was near, and struck like lightning. His blow missed, his wrist was seized, twisted mercilessly, and he was propelled, head foremost, toward the car. "You're breaking my arm," he panted. 

Madden gave an extra push, Fish gasped with pain, and shot headlong through the open doorway of the car. Madden jumped in after him, and the machine went off toward the outskirts of Paris, slipping out through the Porte Maillot and so to the Bois.

All this happened very quickly. Once, in the Avenue de la Grande Armee, Fish tried to get up. Madden hit him under the chin with his clenched fist, and Fish crouched quietly. 

In truth, Fish was uneasy. Madden's strength, when

CABARET 

bundling him into the car, had amazed him. Fish was essentially a creature who respected brute force. He had no use for the higher attributes of humanity. Brutality was his creed, and by it he lived and ruled such of life's creatures as came under his control. Now he was vaguely apprehensive that he had met somebody who could, if he chose, deal more largely in that commodity than he himself. 

He would never have respected Madden in the same way that he respected and feared Durant. Madden was a poor mutt-an honest man who spoke well, behaved himself normally, and lived quietly. That kind were regarded by 

Fish with derision. They were put on the earth for Fish and his sort to shoulder rudely aside, or loot as occasion offered.

Now. ... The car was at a standstill in one of those dark and narrow ways which thread all over the Bois. Stars were glinting through the tangle of branches above them. 

Fish felt a hand on his coat collar. He was dragged unceremoniously out of the car, not helped to his feet, but left to sprawl on his back in the road, the lower part of his legs still on the sill of the car's doorway. 

In this undignified fashion he found himself resuming his talk with Madden. His language was lurid. He got up and went for Madden. He was wild with rage and wished a thousand times that he had not dropped his gun in the little side-street behind the Fantastique. 

As he bored in he heard Madden laugh. That laugh did more to hurt Fish than anything that had happened that night. A man he despised was laughing at him, and the laugh held a quality of contempt which stung him like a whiplash.

CABARET 

201 He took a left in the face which jolted his head backward until he thought his neck would break. A right came across viciously, half-armed, hooked. It did not take him on the jaw. Madden had no wish to knock him out. It connected with his left eye and Fish hit the dust with a crash.

His head was ringing. He felt a strong hand on his coat collar, felt himself dragged upright. Again the fist crashed home, and again Fish went down; again he was dragged up. "Now 99 said Madden. "We'll talk." 

Fish groaned and fingered his eye. All thoughts of aggression had left him. He knew a good boxer when he met one, and he knew that this particular one packed unconsciousness in both fists. 

Madden went on: "I believe I saw you just now in Montmartre." 

Fish managed to speak. "That's a lie," he said. "I ain't been near the place for weeks.” "Are you calling me a liar?" began Madden menacingly. 

Fish, again wishing he had his gun, hurriedly said: “No. 

You're making a mistake. See? That's what I meant. You're making a mistake." Reddy, could he have observed Fish then, would have imagined himself in a dream. Fish was crawling. . . . "I'm not making a mistake," said Madden. "My only difficulty is to prove you were there. It would be my word against yours, and that would not be enough. You tried to commit murder tonight, Fish." 99 "Listen. began Fish in an earnest voice. 

Madden said, very dangerously: "If you interrupt me

CABARET 

while I'm talking I'll put you in a hospital for a fortnight. 

I feel like it."

Fish relapsed into silence. He had ceased to caress his injured eye and stood abjectly, drooping a little at the shoulders, furtive and afraid. "I repeat that you tried to commit murder," went on Madden. "I picked you up for this reason to warn you that if any harm comes to Miss Delorme in any way I shall hold you responsible for it and make it my business to punish you as I think fit." "I'm not the only one," began Fish; and checked himself as he realized that he might be revealing secrets of which Madden was unaware. "I know,” replied Madden in the same dangerously quiet voice, "but you're the one I'm going to hold responsible. 

And don't think that when I threaten you I mean a thrashing only. Behave yourself and you'll be all right. Misbehave and . . . you won't live very long. All right. That's what 

I wanted to say."

He reached out and opened the door of his car. "You going to leave me here?" gasped Fish. "Do you think I'm anxious to ride with you? The car seems cleaner when you're not in it." 

Madden got inside. Fish stood stupidly, staring at him. "Madden . . ." he said. “You might give a guy a break.” 

Madden leaned sideways. "Fish, the less you say to me and the less you see of me, the better for you. That's how

I feel tonight, and how I shall continue to feel. I'm after you, Fish, from now on. So walk pretty. Good-night."

The car snorted down the road and vanished from sight. 

Fish turned and walked back along the winding narrow

CABARET 

203 road, weary and hurt in body and soul; and as he walked he cursed Madden and Marie and Reddy, cursed Marteau and Henriette, cursed Durant and Vibart and all with whom he was associated. For if he had not been associated with them he would not have been in this evil plight. 

Bitterness and humiliation continued with him as he blundered on his way, coming at last from the network of narrow lanes into the broad lit road that carves through the Bois from the Allee de Longchamp to the Porte Dauphine. 

On this road he picked up a taxi and told the man to drive to the Cabaret Vibart and to pull up at the rear entrance. 

He knew his eye must be turning black and blue, and his head rang nastily. His clothing was disarranged and needed attention, and he wanted a wash and brush-down. He could not walk in through the main door of the Cabaret in this condition.

As Fish sat in the taxi he was very much afraid. He could not divert his thoughts from Madden and from Madden's threats against his personal safety. Somehow, he believed 

Madden when he said he would kill him if anything happened to Marie. He, Fish, dealt in threats of that kind so often that, while some of them were carried out in desperate emergencies, the majority lost weight through constant repetition. But Madden was law-abiding. Madden was the sort to whom murder was something awful and remote.

Fish judged that when Madden made up his mind to kill, he determined on something cataclysmic. It was not an idle threat which left his lips. He was like an ordinary citizen who hates talk of war and battle, but who, once he has put his hand to a gun holds that gun till the last shot is fired.

CABARET 

Madden, in fact, had become, in Fish's imagination, a force to be reckoned with, dreaded, avoided. 

Fish wondered if he should warn Durant; but he was just then in no state of mind to assist Durant to avoid trouble. He was in a mood which made him relish the prospect of somebody else suffering as he had suffered. 

By the time he reached the Cabaret Vibart he was a little steadied but still morose. He slipped in through the back way and met Durant in one of the passages just inside. 

Durant glanced at him. "What's the matter with your eye?" he asked. "Been bathing it in rose water," snarled Fish, and brushed past him. He heard Durant chuckle as he slammed through a doorway.

He furbished himself up as best he could, tended his injured eye carefully—it was assuming a variegated hue-and went out to the dance-floor, keeping discreetly in the background, and wondering how long his eye would persist in remaining black and blue and thus prevent him from walking abroad in the Cabaret without exciting comment.

There was one curious fact about all these reflections. He did not stop to think about reprisals on Madden. It was significant, indicative of the fear Madden had managed to inspire in him. He was afraid of Madden, uneasy regarding him; but he devoted no conscious thought toward hating him sufficiently to do him injury. Fear outstripped his capacity for hatred.

He stood against the wall and watched the dancing gloomily. Madden was after him, and Madden had proved that he was an opponent to fear. The fact complicated an already complicated matter. Again and again Fish wished

CABARET 

205 the attack on Marie had been successful, and once more he reviled himself for introducing Reddy into the scheme of things.

Thus reviewing the catastrophe of the night, he suddenly remembered something which, while he had vaguely appreciated it at the time, had been swept from his mind by the swiftly rushing current of events. 

He remembered the wild words Boris had shouted to

Marie as he held her; remembered how Julie had drawn back, had ceased to cry out, and had shown agony and dismay. 

Fish whistled softly. Here was another complication. Boris falling in love with Marie; Boris actually in love with Marie, and showing it in a moment of crisis when self-control was broken.

How did that fit in with all the plots and counterplots at the Cabaret Vibart? Fish did not consider Julie in the matter. Julie did not count. But the emotional intervention of 

Boris might have a material effect on the fortunes and plans of various people, and Fish was shrewd enough to realize it. 

Fish had reached this stage in his uncheerful cogitations when a waiter approached him and said Durant would like him to step along to the office. He went there and found 

Durant alone. The door was closed on them.

Fish drew slowly at the cigar. He was finding himself near to breaking point. After all, he had endured much that night. His big plan had failed and Durant was obliquely accusing him. CABARET

Durant tore his thoughts away from these things. He looked Fish over. "Just now," he said, "I spoke to you about that black eye you're sporting. Then I merely joked. Now I'm wondering.

Fish felt badgered and fretted beyond all endurance. All sorts of wild words flooded through his brain. He wished he had kept silent regarding Boris. He wanted to shout that he hoped the whole great scheme fell in ruins about Durant's feet. He wanted to clear out of the Cabaret Vibart and never see it again—just to spite everybody. 

Instead he managed to say: "You go to hell. I'm going to have a drink." He walked out and slammed the door. 

After he had gone Durant sat and thought for some time and carefully. Before doing so he glanced at the time. This precaution was typical of his balanced consideration of things. 

He wished to ensure that Boris would be in the Cabaret for a little while and thus be on hand if he wanted him. 

Durant was sure Fish had tried to kill Marie, and he wondered who had injured Fish's eye. The attempted murder was a serious matter, and if Fish had been recognized the results of the recognition might touch the Cabaret Vibart. 

Fish was a blundering and murderous fool.

209 sky. If she married Boris everything was ruined, though Fish, in his present rage, did not seem to mind very much. 

Durant made up his mind. He would go carefully. He would discover from Boris himself if he loved Marie, and if she loved him. If it were so . . . then there was a quick way to end the affair. Marie could not marry a dead man. But it must all be done smoothly, suavely. There must be no high words, no indication of a rage which might warn Boris to take heed of his own safety. If things were as Fish had said, then, all the more, Durant must be charming to Boris until the hour came for him to strike, unsuspected and decisively.

He picked up the telephone and asked for Boris to be sent to him.

Boris came and Durant looked him over quickly. He was certainly the sort of young man most girls would like. Durant invited him to be seated and gave him a cigarette. Death was very polite to Boris Kolotsky that night.

Chapter XXII 

THE seeds of tragedy which were sown on that day when Boris Kolotsky first suggested to Marie that she should join company with Julie and himself in their dancing-act germinated swiftly and grew toward fruition. 

A few days after Boris had his interview with Durant and revealed his secret, he called on Marie in the morning. He had thought the matter over very carefully. Durant's false kindness had encouraged him, and he had begun to feel that a courageous declaration of his love, without first subtly testing Marie's feelings, might sweep her off her feet. It was an old romantic idea, but all romantics are old, though never out of fashion. 

As when talking to Durant, Boris, on this morning, had lost all his air of sophistication. The ostentatious bohemianism acquired from the folk with whom he mixed was stripped from him. He was a very ordinary lad proposing an outing to a lovely girl; and, for that, he was all the better in Marie's sight.

He suggested that they should go to the Bois and hire a boat on the lake. Marie had never been on the lake. It was a beautiful morning, warm with flooding sunshine, and all

Paris was gay and chattering. The terraces of the cafés were full; there were crowds on the pavements, people with leisure to stroll anywhere on such a morning, and foreigners making holiday. The roads were crammed with luxurious traffic, 212

CABARET 

213 great cars and smart town machines; and the shops glittered with the wealth of the earth. There were little traceries of shadow on the sidewalks, and the newspaper kiosks beneath the trees were bright with colored posters. Paris hummed and talked and laughed, and seemed to trip gaily on her way as Boris and Marie, sitting side by side in an open taxi, drove out toward the Bois.

Boris felt uplifted and exalted. He loved Marie so much that he could be content just to sit and watch her, and keep admiring her, and wonder what it would be like to kiss her eyes and her hands. She was so beautiful in his sight that there was no other beauty in the world except that which ministered to her beauty. Paris was lovely in the sunshine only because it seemed a fit setting, in its laughing gaiety, for Marie. 

Marie felt the thrill of the morning, its wide freedom, its pulsing life. She was prepared to enjoy herself, although at the back of her thoughts dwelt a picture of Julie. She wondered what Julie was doing, and whether it was quite fair that she should be out with Boris alone while Julie spent her time elsewhere. She nearly asked Boris about Julie, but refrained from doing so in case she broke the brisk happiness of the sunlit hours.

The taximan did not drive fast through the Bois, and they enjoyed watching the people under the trees. Some were playing tennis, others—a crowd of young men on holidaywere kicking a football, despite the heat. Everybody was playing some kind of game. Boris remarked that the air was exquisite, and Marie agreed with him. He had not previously spoken for some time. They reached the lakeside and Boris secured his boat.

CABARET 

It was amusing on the lake. There were quite a number of people out, and some of them could row and manage a boat and others couldn't. It is possible that those who couldn't had the more fun. Boris was an expert oarsman and he ran swiftly away from the broad and open expanse of water, and rowing strongly, reached a little sheltered backwater where there was solitude and peace. 

The branches of the trees hung down to the surface of the water and covered it with restful shadow. The voices from the wide stretch whereon experts and non-experts disported were faint and remote. Over Boris and Marie stole a pleasant feeling of aloneness. Boris hitched the bowline of the boat round a tree branch and shipped his oars. "Enjoying yourself?" he asked. "Lovely," said Marie. "It was good to think of this. What made you?" "You." “I . . . ?” Marie suddenly realized the import of his reply and flushed.

Boris moved to the stern, and dropped down beside her and took both her hands in his. "Marie," he said quietly, “I asked you to come out this morning for one reason. I wanted to tell you how much I love you, and now you're here I find I just can't. I can't tell you how much. I can only leave it to you to discover. I love you so much that there is nothing else and nobody else in all the world except you. When I'm with you the world is empty except for you and me. Just now, riding through the streets, I felt the happiest man alive. You were at my side and we had all the morning before us. It seemed like an eternity stretching ahead of us. It is passing like a moment.”

CABARET 

215 Marie looked at him and said nothing. She thought of 

Julie, and of Madden, while Boris's eyes pleaded with her and were warm and gentle with love as they looked at her. "Marie... You love me?" His voice held an urgent, frightened ring. "I was thinking of Julie,” said Marie softly.

Boris's shoulders moved very slightly. "That is the tragedy, Marie. But can one help it? Love is like an army. It just marches on. I wouldn't lightly hurt Julie; but I love you so much that even Julie does not count, and can never count." "You mustn't say that, Boris." "But I do say it." 

He leaned forward and kissed her on the lips. She made no effort to prevent him from doing so, but her lips did not respond to his. She was unmoved as he drew back, and his eyes clouded. "You don't love me," he said. "Oh, God . . ." It was a little pitiful cry, wrenched from his heart. His hands fell away from hers and he sat against the side of the boat, watching her, suffering. 

Marie tried to speak very calmly. "Boris, I don't love you-like that. I like you ever and ever so much, and . . .” 99 

He exclaimed, a little wildly: "That hurts more than anything. I don't know what I shall do. You don't love me; and I've built on it, dreamed of it, counted on it . . . loving you all the time, thinking of you, needing you." "Boris . . ." Marie's voice shook a little. "Let me talk to you, please. The other night-when that man shot at me 

Julie knew. She knew that night. She broke down in our dressing-room. It was terrible. You could never imagine how

CABARET 

much she cares for you unless you had seen her then. She just belongs to you, Boris, heart and soul; and if she loses you there will be nothing at all left for her. Doesn't that make a difference to you?" "Can I help loving you?" he asked harshly. "Perhaps. You can try and stop thinking about me-in that way. Keep trying and trying." "Oh, God, it isn't easy!" he cried. "It's going to be hard, frightfully hard; but if you win through-and I know you will-you'll be glad. You see, Boris, I don't think I'm quite so loyal and true as Julie. She's wonderful, you know. Though she knew the truth the other night, if you went to her now and told her you loved her, I'm sure she would ask no questions and speak no reproaches, but would just come to you and love you and cling to you. 

And that's worth a great deal, isn't it? It's something to prize and keep close and hold to." “I know she loves me,” said Boris. "But I can't help it. 

Marie, can't you say-?" 

Marie broke in. "No. I can't." "There's somebody else," he said with darting swiftness. "You love somebody else."

Marie shook her head. She had not the courage to speak the lie. She felt she could not. She would have denied something infinitely true, and the denial would have sounded almost sacrilegious. "Will you think over what I've said?" she asked.

He considered her, his eyes uncertain. Both of them were silent, and during this silence Boris buried his face in his hands and stayed like that for some time. Marie felt infinitely

CABARET 

217 sorry for him. She knew that through his closed eyes he was seeing the ruins of the castles of his dreams. 

He looked up at last. He was pale and a little set, but his eyes were steady. "Perhaps you're right, Marie. I don't know. I don't know a bit why I should be allowed to fall in love with you, when you don't love me. I don't know why life should be so cruel. 

But there it is. And, as I say, perhaps you're right. I'll see 

Julie . . . in a day or two; when I've had time to get steadied." 

He took her hands again. "Look at me. Just once. Look right into my eyes.” His eyes held hers, glowing, tender. He whispered: "Darling And his voice broke a little as he spoke. 99 Then he breathed very deeply and stood up in the boat. "We'll have to get back," he said, “or we shall be late for lunch and rehearsal this afternoon." He stepped across to his seat and outshipped his oars, poling away from the bank into open water, and pulling strongly for the landing stage.

On the way they saw a man "catch a crab." Both of them laughed. It was rather ridiculous to laugh after what had just passed between them. But they laughed and looked at each other and laughed again. The man's companion in the boat was helping him to get up, and the boat rocked as they clung to each other. It seemed rather a joke.

Chapter XXIII 

AMONG Durant's correspondence, one morning, was a letter that surprised him. He read it several times before finally tearing it up. He showed it to nobody, though its contents affected Marteau and Fish. The letter was from Peter Madden, and it suggested that Durant might like to call on him at a flat Madden had rented furnished near the Place des Ternes. The time appointed was in the evening, before dinner.

Durant was treading very carefully these days; hence his destruction of the letter. He knew the affair in which they were all engaged was fast approaching crisis, and he knew he walked absolutely alone and that all about him were foes. 

He did not mind this. He felt capable of meeting and beating them all; but he also felt that he must be cunning in all he did.

The revelation of Boris's love for Marie had disturbed him as much as he might, in the normal way, be disturbed. It was the unexpected, and yet, as he now realized, it was what might have been expected. A girl as beautiful as Marie was sure to have a lover. It was a point the triumvirate had not taken into consideration in laying their plans, and it needed dealing with at the earliest possible moment.

Durant viewed the position quite calmly. Killing Boris would be a small matter to him. What was important was 218

CABARET 

219 the manner and place of the murder, so that it might be done without too much risk. This aspect of the affair was receiving Durant's thoughtful consideration. Meanwhile, here was the letter from Madden. He kept the appointment. 

Madden was waiting, and himself admitted him. Durant guessed Madden had sent his man out so that whatever passed in the flat could not possibly be overheard by a third party. In this he was right. Madden showed him into a comfortably furnished, mannish sort of room, in which were some drinks and cigars, and invited him to take a cocktail.

Durant, watching Madden, felt a little strange, as though this happening was unreal, and he saw that Madden was perfectly calm and self-possessed and easy in his manner. 

It was, in fact, the first time Durant had ever encountered 

Madden face to face. Previously-though he had seen Madden from a distance-he had acquired all his ideas of the man from what Marteau had told him. Now he was understanding that Marteau had made some mistakes in his estimate. This Madden was a person of resource and of strength-perhaps dangerous.

Madden shook up the cocktails and poured them out, at the same time indicating a box of cigarettes by Durant's elbow. Durant was ensconced comfortably in a deep leather chair. The light was on and the room looked comfortable and friendly. 

Durant lifted his glass. "To what do I drink?" he asked with a smile. 

Madden returned the smile. "To ourselves," he said gravely. "Whom better?" Durant sipped the cocktail. "Excellent. 

I've never tasted this before. Your own?" "Yes. I'm glad you like it. And now-I wanted to talk to

CABARET 

you seriously about... business, shall I say? I thought of calling at the Cabaret Vibart one night, but it occurred to me that this atmosphere might be more conducive to quiet discussion." “Quite," agreed Durant. "What is your trouble?" "This business of blackmailing my sister," said Madden bluntly.

Durant's hands moved in a little deprecating gesture. 

Neither of the two men was displaying emotion. They might have been talking over an honest business deal. I've never met you before, but I do know that you're the big noise at the 

Cabaret Vibart." "There is, you know," said Durant softly, “a gentleman named Emile Vibart." "I believe so. But never on view; and inaccessible. You are-accessible. You are powerful. All-powerful, I imagine.

I invited you here because I know that what you say goes." "It's very kind of you," said Durant. "Well? What do you suggest?" Durant laughed and, picking up his glass, slowly sipped at the cocktail.

CABARET 

221 "Might I suggest that that's a most one-sided proposal?

The discussion was conducted on rather different lines from those we are now following. You can't put Mr. Fish in a chair and talk quietly with him. You have to knock him down to make him listen. It's the only thing he understands."

Durant emptied his glass. "So it was you who gave him the black eye, was it? I thought so. I was most amused. Fish was not. Yes-thank you. I will have another cocktail."

CABARET

223 "It was," said Madden deliberately, "your last chance, Durant."

Durant took the cigarette from between his lips. His movement was deliberate. His eyes were fixed on Madden's eyes all the time. "You are a very bold man," he said, but his voice held no contempt whatsoever. "I don't ask for compliments," said Madden, and smiled at him.

Durant's right hand moved slightly toward the opening of his jacket. "Under my left arm," he said, "I've got a pistol. I could shoot you dead where you sit." "That would be foolish," said Madden. CABARET 

there might be trouble. There would be no alibi for you, Durant."

They sat very still for a few seconds; and Durant was conscious of a sense of failure such as he had never known before. He knew that threat of violence had been a mistake. 

He had strayed into the crude territory governed by Fish, and he knew he would have done better to remain on his own ground.

In this quiet battle of wills Madden had threatened-but had not lost his head one little bit; Madden had maintained the pose of polite friendship which had marked the opening of their discussion. He, Durant, had been, for a moment, crude. He picked up his glass. "Unfortunate," he said; but whether he referred to Madden's attitude or to the fact that he could not, just now, safely murder him, Madden never knew. Durant's teeth showed brilliantly in a smile. "Of course." 

Madden stood up. "I'm sorry. I'm afraid there's going to be a whole lot of trouble in the near future; and I wished to avoid it." · · "Naturally-for your charming sister's sake. For myself and my friends we care nothing for trouble. We can meet it, avoid it, get over it. Why not pay, Mr. Madden, and finish? One can always get out by paying, you know." 

Durant also stood up. "I only pay just debts,” said Madden.

CABARET 

225 "Then you are unprepared to buy experience? An extraordinary outlook on life, isn't it?" "Perhaps. But some experiences are not worth buying.”

Madden was moving toward the door. Durant followed him, and as Madden held the door open, passed through to the tiny hall of the flat, where he picked his hat from the chair onto which he had dropped it when Madden admitted him. "Well, good-night, Mr. Madden. Deadlock, eh? I won't be frightened by your bluff, and you won't pay. We shall see."

Madden saw him out, and Durant went down and into the street. There, he admitted himself a little shaken. It was a strange experience for him. All the emotions he had suppressed when in Madden's presence flared wickedly and dangerously high, so that he was drunk with rage. 

Marteau had precipitated all this; Marteau and Fish between them. They had bungled from the first to the last. And 

Fish particularly. . . . Bringing the methods of American thugs into a delicately adjusted situation. He strode on, walking hard, as though he would shake off, by intense physical effort, the uneasiness he felt.

And as he walked, he began to wonder what might happen. 

Madden was deadly dangerous. He had surprised Durant -more than surprised him; shocked him. That threat to shoot Madden ... that threat which had proved to be so empty. The English lawyer was the only man he had ever so threatened who had sat quietly and laughed at him! And this was the fellow Marteau's rapacity and Fish's folly had brought into the lists against them.

Durant felt that he would like to take the whole affair under his hands and tear it into shreds and cast it to the

CABARET 

winds. He strode on feverishly, boiling, raging; until once more the rage subsided slightly and left him calmer and more normal.

One fact was definitely established. Crisis was near, and, one way or another, the matter must soon break clean. When the break came he must hold the winning portion. He pondered the situation in detail, his brain once more steady and calculating.

First-Boris. Boris must be prevented from marrying the girl. It did not occur to Durant that Marie might not be willing to marry Boris. He had gathered from the lad's talk that she already favored him, and Durant also considered she might be prepared to marry him if only to find shelter and some kind of security.

So he continued to manufacture the stuff of an unnecessary tragedy. Boris should be the first. Boris must die. 

Then-Fish. Fish was a mutineer and a nuisance. Fish was now ready at any moment to fly into open rebellion, to defy him and wreck things. He knew Fish better than 

Fish knew himself. He knew the man's ignorance and brutality. He knew his inherent weakness. Fish's love of brutal things, his inclination toward scenes of violence, was a sure sign of cowardliness. Fish was not strong enough to be quiet and calm as Madden had just been. Fish would not have appreciated the deadliness in Madden which Durant appreciated. Fish, taken by the police, would tell everything. That was the sum-total of these reflections. 

Quite calmly, Durant made up his mind that Fish also must be killed, if only to shut his mouth. 

And Marteau and the necklace. In his heart, Durant knew

CABARET 

227 he should have accepted Madden's offer, even though it would have enraged Marteau. It was the sane thing to do. 

Madden had proved he could be dangerous and might wreck a bigger scheme than the blackmailing of his sister. To surrender to him meant a loss of pride, but it also meant security to go ahead with the great scheme for acquiring the ownership and sole control of the Cabaret Vibart. 

Yet... to hand the necklace over after what had just passed; to come away smilingly defying Madden, and then meekly to write and acknowledge defeat! If Madden had not so definitely established a moral superiority over him Durant might have done it. But he rebelled against surrendering again to a man to whom he had already, in a way, surrendered. Durant was displaying a weakness which had always dwelt within him. He could be mulishly obstinate. 

His thoughts turned to Marie. His proclamation to Fish and Marteau that he intended to marry Vibart's daughter had been, after all, no more than the voicing of an ambition. 

The girl's own point of view had to be considered, and she did not yet view him as a lover. He had, it is true, been very kind to her, had done his best to make her think well of him; but he had made no lover-like approaches-had, in fact, considered it wise to get her to regard him as a friend before he began to appear in a more intimate rôle. If he had really been in love with Marie he would not have imagined that a bold approach would startle and alarm her. But he was not in love; his purpose was evil, and he considered that he must be cautious in every move he made toward his intended victim.

Now, however, the hour of crisis was drawing near. No-

CABARET 

body knew what Madden might do, what further mischief Fish was about to start, what plans were coming to maturity in Marteau's sleek head. As Marie's husband, Durant's position would be greatly strengthened, whatever disaster might happen to the great conspiracy, while if disaster were averted he would be complete master of the whole situation. It was time to take a definite step forward. 

He did not, in making this resolution, bestow any further thought on Boris. Boris was doomed. He was as good as dead. He could be left out of the reckoning.

Durant knew where Marie lived, and he knew-from constant conversations with her—just about what time in the evening she left her hotel in order to catch the omnibus to Montmartre and the Theatre Fantastique. He had some little time in hand. He went for his car and drove it to the end of the little street behind the Avenue de l'Opera in which Marie's hotel was situated, and there he waited, the engine running; so that when she stepped out of the hotel, it seemed perfectly accidental that Durant-who started his car forward at once-should encounter her. 

He pulled up alongside her. "Hello, Miss Delorme. This is an unexpected pleasure. Can I drive you anywhere?” 

Marie stopped and smiled at him. She had not, since the night of her ordeal at the hands of Marteau and the woman with the whip, been able to make up her mind definitely about Durant. His kindnesses had multiplied. From time to time he had chatted with her at the Cabaret Vibart, and always courteously and attentively. He had considered her in a great many ways, and she could not help but feel grateful to him. "I was going to the Fantastique," she said.

CABARET 

229 He pushed the door of the car open. “Get in. I'll run you up. Really. I've nothing very pressing to do."

Marie seated herself beside him and the long car slid toward the traffic of the Boulevard des Italiens. "And what do you do with yourself these days?" asked 

Durant, handling the machine skilfully. "I seem to see so little of you, now that you are a great artist." He laughed quietly and jokingly, without offense. "I don't know," confessed Marie. Marie stammered some kind of thanks, and they drove for a moment or two in silence.

We could dawdle through the forest. And I'd show you roads that are lined with apple trees, and anybody can pick the apples. In the autumn, when they're falling, they roll all over the road.

Chapter XXIV 

MARTEAU, at first, acted as spokesman. That was obviously his rôle. He was speaking as much for Fish as for himself. 

He said: "Fish and I have been talking things over, and we've come to the conclusion that the whole affair of this girl 

Delorme wants finishing up without delay." "Admirable," said Durant. He was in no mood to listen to them. They were both trying to cheat him, and one another. Their talk was false. Besides, he was rattled by his interview with Madden. 

Marteau glanced at Fish. “Fish and I," he repeated, “have had our little differences, but we've settled them in a friendly fashion." "That pleases me," observed Durant suavely, "more than anything I've heard for a long time. Like children together, playing happily." He eyed Marteau blandly. 

He guessed that Marteau had been unaware of Fish's effort to kill Marie until that effort had failed. He was willing to hazard a theory that Marteau had tried to cheat Fish in the matter of Marie's destiny, just as Fish had tried to cheat him; and he also guessed that for their mutual sakes, they had had a straight talk and that this approach to him was the outcome of it. Durant was right. "Now," said Marteau, "Fish and I [Durant wished he 231

CABARET 

wouldn't keep saying 'Fish and I'] think a clean sweep is essential if we're to hold on. By a clean sweep I mean putting out everybody who's in our way, and leaving ourselves free to do as we like." In a tontine the last survivor of a partnership takes all. We can antedate it by seven years if we wish. And 

Sard, the crook lawyer in Belleville, will see that its antedating is made to appear legal. That means that following the signing of the agreement, Vibart has lived for seven years. We're let out of the suspicion of murder on the spot. As it's a tontine, we, the survivors, automatically split his share. How's that?"

It was quite sound, and mentally, Durant had to admit it, and also admit that it might have been thought of before. 

But unfortunately it was a scheme that cried check to his plans for establishing his own legal right to ownership and control as soon as Vibart was dead. "I think it's too dangerous," he said. "It lets Sard into the affair." "We put him right out when he's done his job," said Fish directly.

This, also, was sound. They eliminated the one man who knew their secret.

Chapter XXV 

DANCING at the Fantastique and the Cabaret Vibart that night was, for Marie, something of a strain, and the strain was born of Boris's lovemaking and her knowledge of Julie's position. Julie was always dispirited nowadays, though she tried to appear cheerful. In their dressing-room she had spoken little since the evening she accused Marie of taking Boris from her, and Marie, awkward and diffident, had not cared to try and make conversation. 

On this night, however, she took her courage into her hands. They would do their act at the Vibart, she thought, and then, when they were changing into their outdoor clothes, she would have a talk with Julie and try and put things right. 

She fully appreciated the perils of interfering in Julie's unhappy love-affair, but she considered that she could effect a reconciliation if only she approached the matter properly. 

Besides, Boris had promised to try and turn once more to Julie.

When they came out onto the floor for their act she saw 

Madden. He was sitting with one of the dancing partners at a table on the dais. He smiled at her as their eyes met. She felt tumultuously glad he was there, and yet wished he affected her less.

Boris et Deux performed their dances, and were, as usual, greeted with applause, after which they went to their dressing- 236

CABARET 

237 rooms. A card was awaiting Marie. It was one of Madden's visiting-cards, and written on it was a request that he might be allowed to take her home. "Is that the Englishman?” she asked, when the boy who had brought the card had gone. "Yes," said Marie.

Julie turned to her looking glass and began to remove her make-up. She said nothing more.

239 In a little while her sobs checked. Marie had held her, comforting her, reassuring her, so that when she drew back
