Marie laughed with her. Marie felt as though a gentle hand had been laid on her heart, soothing it to rest. Julie would go back to Boris and Boris would forget his infatuation for 

Marie and welcome the girl who loved him. "I'll go tomorrow afternoon," said Julie. "There's no rehearsal tomorrow, but I know he always takes long walks in the morning and rests in the afternoon before working at night. I've still got the key to his room. I'll show it to you."

She exhibited the key as though it were a precious thing, and she talked on until they were both dressed and said good-night to one another. As they parted Julie whispered: "Good luck with your Englishman, Marie," and slipped away.

Marie found Madden awaiting her, and she got into his car and was driven away down the Champs Elysees.

CABARET 

"I've wanted to see you," said Madden, “ever since that affair outside the Fantastique. Have you quite recovered from the shock?" "I think so,” replied Marie. "I was awfully upset at the time." She paused. “You bolted off so quickly that I had no time to thank you for intervening." "It was nothing heroic," laughed Madden. “I happened to have a high-powered car and I nearly knocked one of the blackguards down with it. The other I recognized, and followed." "Who was it?" asked Marie quickly. "Fish."

Marie uttered a little cry and sat and stared at him for a space. Though he was supposed to be taking her home, he had pulled the machine up alongside the curb in a dark spot in the rue de Rivoli, the better to talk. 

He said: "It was Fish all right; but the difficulty, of course, is to prove it. My word against his, you know, and he, no doubt, with a manufactured but well-supported alibi. So I could do nothing but thrash him. That's why he's sporting a black eye."

The shock of his disclosure of the identity of her assailant was now passing and Marie was able to speak. "You shouldn't have thrashed Fish. He's terribly dangerous, and he'll never forgive you. You took an awful risk." "I'd take any risk where you're concerned," said Madden quietly. "I warned Fish off, and I think he'll heed the warning." 

Marie was silent for a moment, flushing rosily in the darkness of the car's interior.

CABARET 

241 "I wish you hadn't done it. You ought to leave me and my affairs alone. It's not worth it." "I think it's worth a great deal," smiled Madden. "Will you have a cigarette, by the way?" "Yes... thanks." Her voice shook a little.

He gave her a cigarette and struck a match for her. Its flame lit both their faces, and their eyes met. Marie smiled at him unsteadily, her eyes a little afraid. She kept thinking of the woman for whose honor he fought against Durant, Marteau and Fish. The match went out and darkness came down once more on the car. "We're sitting here," said Madden, "when really I ought to have reached your hotel by now. Are you in a great hurry?" "Well... not really; but it's late, isn't it?" "A bit. What about just toddling round Paris for half an hour? We could have a drink at one of the cafés on the 

Boulevard des Italiens. Game?" "If you want to." "What else should I want?" he asked, and started the car forward.

They chatted desultorily as the machine made a wide circle through the inner boulevards. Marie felt a little ill-at-ease, not quite natural, and she sensed that Madden felt the same. 

They crossed to the south side of the river and came back along the Boulevard St. Michel and through to the Boulevard des Italiens, where they pulled up at one of the big cafés which still remained open and had a drink. "I've enjoyed this," said Madden, stretching his legs past the little table. "I wish you could find time to do it every night."

CABARET 

"I don't think it would be wise," said Marie quietly. "Why not?" His eyes met hers. "Just because that is my opinion." "Oh!"

They finished their drinks and he put her into the car once more and drove the short distance to her hotel in the little side-street. There he pulled up. "I want to tell you," he said, before opening the door, "that this has been one of the happiest hours I've spent since 

I reached Paris." 

Marie fumbled for the catch, and opening the door, stepped out. She was charged with emotions she did not care to analyze. She knew that if, before she opened the door, he had put his arms about her, she would have surrendered herself to his kisses without protest, gladly, completely. Her hand was shaking as it manipulated the catch. She spoke almost without thinking of what she said. "How is your business with Marteau going? Is ... the lady . . . still distressed?"

Madden looked at her, and she saw realization dawning slowly in his eyes, an understanding which unsteadied her the more, and with it, a little smile. He was remembering that he had not told her the identity of Mrs. James Rollerton. 

At first it was because he wished to keep his sister's secret as closely guarded as possible. Since, he had had little opportunity of revealing the truth. 

He leaned sideways. "You mean my sister? She's still as cheery as she can be, of course." ... "Your . . . sister ?" Marie echoed the words slowly. 

He nodded, still looking at her. "I ought to have told you."

She uttered a little cry and turned and ran toward the

CABARET 

243 closed door of the hotel. He began to go after her, but checked himself and laughed, and slid back into his seat. 

The car started forward, and it was as though, in its high exhaust, a note of exultation roared; an exultation reflected throbbingly in the heart of Marie as she went through the revolving door of the hotel.

Madden drove straight to his flat near the Place des Ternes, and arriving there, sat down to write a letter. It was to Marie. 

He just had to write it. It ran: "My dear Marie, "I've been a fool. I know you will appreciate my reasons for not telling you about my sister when first I met you; but there can be no excuse for my subsequent silence, save that it never occurred to me that an explanation was necessary. I want to see you again and talk to you—as I wished to talk to you tonight and as I now know I might have talked to you but for this very foolish misunderstanding. "Peter."

His man had sat up for him, and Madden gave him the letter and told him to deliver it at Marie's hotel first thing in the morning.

The letter itself was unimportant, but it gave Marie Madden's address; and that was of vast importance. 

The following morning, after his man had delivered the letter and returned to report that Marie had received it from him in person, Madden was lounging about, reading, when his telephone bell rang and he heard Alice Rollerton at the other end of the wire. "Peter," she said, “come round at once. Something terrible has happened. Please don't ask me any questions. Come."

CABARET 

Madden tossed his newspapers aside, and taking a taxi, raced round to Alice's apartment. He found her in a state of intense agitation, but not, as he had expected, in tears. She was, in fact, remarkably self-controlled considering what had occurred. "It's Marteau," she began when they were alone. "He has just been to see me. Peter, we're bust." "What do you mean?" asked Madden. "Oh—they've hit on a scheme for forcing our hands. They know Jim's due any day now at Marseilles. They're not going to wait for him, and risk his standing by me. They're going to create a first-class scandal. They've given me till tomorrow evening to pay them twenty thousand pounds. 

Otherwise Marteau says he will put the necklace up for auction on the day after that, at a public auction room, and announce it as once the property of Mrs. James Rollerton and now being sold to pay a gigolo's gambling debts.”

Alice leaned with one hand on the table and spoke slowly and quietly. "That would be the finish, Peter. The newspapers would all take it up. Marteau, in fact, told me plainly he would see that the matter was communicated to them. You know what some of the Paris papers are. You know the hints, the filthy innuendoes they'll print. I'm not being given a chance to see 

Jim. His name will be dragged in the gutter before he gets to 

Marseilles, and he'll reach Paris to find it . . . in the mud . . . where I put it." 

Madden was shaken. Marteau's scheme, as disclosed to

Alice, held all the simplicity which ensured success. A public auction preceded by a public announcement. The unleashed newspapers of Paris, delighting in a scandal, featuring the

CABARET 

245 sale. It was better than threatening a disclosure of the facts to 

Jim Rollerton, far better. In that there was always the risk that Alice would forestall them by confession. Now she had no chance. They threatened a scandal before her husband's arrival. Marteau's scheme had been an inspiration. "What shall we do, Peter?" asked Alice dully. "Oh, I'm just sick of myself. I've been the biggest fool . . . worse than a fool. "They've given you till toeh?" morrow, "Yes. Twenty-four hours, Marteau said." She took a step forward. “But you're not going to do anything mad, are you?

Pay, and be glad of the chance. Let us pay." "I'll see," said Madden slowly. "We've got till tomorrow. 

That gives us time to think." "Not to act, Peter." Her eyes implored him. "To think I said." he replied; and left her. 

His mind was made up. He would wait no longer to make the desperate move he had been contemplating. That night he would break into the Cabaret Vibart, whatever the risk, and search the place from roof to cellar.

Chapter XXVI 

AS ARRANGED, at eleven o'clock that morning, Durant called for Marie. Her encounter with Madden the previous night had wrought a great change in her. She glowed with happiness, and in her eyes was the richness of a woman who has found love. Durant, naturally, misunderstood these signs, and preened himself. He attributed Marie's excitement to the prospect of her excursion with him, and he saw victory within his grasp.

He handed her into the car, and drove in leisurely fashion from the outskirts of Paris, first to Versailles, where they did not trouble to look round the great ramshackle palace with its disappointing, though famous, Hall of Mirrors and its wonderful gardens, but went on, swinging away to Fontainebleau. The day was splendid with sunshine, and the country was beautiful. Marie drank in the air greedily, finding it exhilarating after the closer air of Paris, and made up her mind that if ever she married and settled down, she would live in the country.

They took lunch, about half-past twelve, at a wayside inn. 

It was a plain meal, but everything was fresh and redolent of the rich countryside of France. The meal was served in the garden, and when it was ended Durant took his opportunity. "I expect," he said, “you have wondered why I invited you to come for this trip." 246

CABARET 

247 "Oh, I haven't thought why," laughed Marie. "It's been awfully kind of you, and I've enjoyed it immensely." 

Durant smiled at her. "I should like to take you for a great many more such trips," he said softly. "I'd like to be your companion always." 

For a moment Marie stared at him; she flushed quickly. 

Durant leaned across the table. “Marie, I love you," he said. "I think I've loved you from the time we first met. I knew it myself when I found you in that cellar . . . on that awful night. And since, it has grown and grown until I have had to tell you."

He had rehearsed this speech carefully, and thought it sounded convincing; which, in fact, it did. 

Marie, however, heard it with dismay. She had just discovered love and there was no room for anything else in her heart or her thoughts. Madden was a magnified being in her imagination, somebody beside whom all other men were very ordinary and uninteresting-even Durant, with his incisive intelligence and his handsome face, his obvious strength of mind.

Durant went on. It was natural that, as a lover, he should not be entirely on his own ground. He had never loved in his life. He had no capacity for warm emotion. That eloquence which love gives was not his; that sincerity which, though often clumsily expressed, is utterly unmistakable, could not be achieved by him. And he had not the gift for twisting women round his finger which was possessed by 

Marteau, whose business it was. "I can claim," he said carefully, "to be a rich man, Marie.

My share in the Cabaret Vibart is very valuable, and I've saved money and made some more on the Bourse. My wife

CABARET 

would have a very good time-a nice house, cars, servants, fine holidays at the best places." All these things counted with 

Durant. He thought they would count with everybody. 

Many men as shrewd as Durant have made the same extraordinary mistake. "A moment," said Marie a little breathlessly. “Are you asking me to marry you, Monsieur Durant?” "That is what I was suggesting," he replied. 

She shook her head slowly. "I'm awfully sorry..."

He studied her for a moment. “Marie, that can't be final. 

Will you promise to think about it?" "It's impossible for me to think about it," she said. "I see. Then there's somebody else?” "Yes." "May I ask who he is?" "Does that matter?" she asked quietly; and her tone told him she thought he was intruding. “Well, no. Perhaps not.” He was keeping himself well in hand. "Marie. Suppose this-somebody else had not come along? Would you have so quickly rejected me?” "I can't tell, can I?” replied Marie. “Everything would have been different. CABARET 

249 "I've had to be kind. One is kind to the person one loves. 

Then I'm permitted to try once more?"

Marie felt she could not bluntly tell him not to do so. He had taken her rejection of him splendidly, and though she knew his case was hopeless she had that natural disinclination toward brutality which most of us possess; even when brutality may be kindness.

They left the inn soon after that, and Durant glanced at the clock on his dashboard. The time was a quarter to two. 

He traveled fast along the tree-lined roads to Paris, and by three o'clock had dropped Marie at her hotel. He did not linger to say much to her at her door.

Durant was in a hurry. A deed he had contemplated had been made inevitable that sunny lunch time. If Marie had accepted him, he could have allowed Boris Kolotsky to live.

Chapter XXVII 

THE affair was rushing toward its conclusion. Into those last fateful hours events crowded thickly. While Durant was worriedly busy, so was Marteau. 

When Marteau outlined to Durant and Fish his simple scheme for auctioning the necklace, he revealed to them only part of his plans. The other part he kept to himself for a very good reason. They had to be secret because they concerned Marie's future-and his own. He took the first step in this secret plan when, that afternoon, he visited Henriette. 

She received him without any great warmth, but also without animosity, and inviting him to take a chair, suggested a cup of tea. She always took afternoon tea in the English fashion. 

Marteau, prosecuting his designs with outward calmness, was feverish within, anxious to get things done before the inevitable crash came, the crash he foresaw; and Henriette's amiability pleased him. It was what he wanted. 

Balancing a teacup on his knee, he said: "Well, my dear,

I've come to have another talk with you-about ourselves." "Yes?" She sipped tea and looked somewhat indifferent.

Marteau was glad. She was becoming sensible, and she didn't really love him. He felt relieved about that. Having a woman in love with one was an awful nuisance. When she was definitely on a mercenary basis you both knew where you stood; but love was incalculable. "And so," he added, "what shall we do? Quarreling is 250

CABARET 

251 foolish, isn't it?" He paused. "I want to apologize for the other day. I rather lost my head, and though there was a certain excuse for it in your attitude, actually my behavior was unpardonable. Will you forgive me?" "I suppose I shall have to, Charles," she replied. “Anyhow, it's ended. What do you propose to do?" "How about twenty thousand francs?" he asked.

She took another sip at her tea. The cup trembled slightly. "You wouldn't consider for a moment resuming . . . our friendship?" "You know it's impossible, Henriette.” "Yes. . . . ?" She put the cup down on a small table and considered him. "I suppose so." She was very quiet and steady now. "Under no circumstances, eh?” "I can't, my dear. And listen. The last time I talked to you there was mention of the Delorme girl. You were utterly wrong there, you know, utterly and completely." "All right, Charles." Her smile might have been seen by da Vinci when he painted his masterpiece. "We won't say any more about that. Twenty thousand francs. . . Payment in full." She appeared to think. "It's not enough,” she announced at last. "But, my dear ... 99 "No. Not nearly enough. A hundred thousand." "What? I can't find that amount.” Henriette's shoulders lifted slightly. "That's about the price, Charles." "It's impossible." His jaw set.

She measured him with her eyes. "Well, why not take the possibility? Why not . . . come together again? We were very happy."

CABARET 

"No." He spoke definitely. 

Perhaps she drooped a little, momentarily, in her chair; but Marteau did not observe it. The next second she was as calm and smiling and easy as before. "Then we must compromise, mustn't we? I've offered twenty thousand. I'll meet you. I'll give you sixty. That's absolutely as much as I can afford. And I want your decision here and now. Sixty thousand."

He was a little irked. He wanted to get out and never see her again. These interviews were not painful to him. He was not easily pained by the suffering of others. But they fretted him, made him impatient. He was finished with Henriette and he wanted never to see her again.

She lit a cigarette. To do this she had to get up and walk away from him to another table, with her back toward him. 

It took her a comparatively long time to light the cigarette, and all this time he could not see her face. At last she turned round. 

She still smiled and looked easy. "All right, Charles. I accept. And this is the last time, eh?” "It will have to be. As I say, one can't go on indefinitely like this." "Perhaps you're right. Not indefinitely. The thing must

CABARET 

253 end. I've just learned that. It must end. Good-by, Charles."

He got up. His dismissal was rather abrupt, and it had startled him a little. He had expected some argument, for he knew his Henriette. Now he felt somewhat uncertain. She was so calm and unmoved, so quick to accept his compromise. "You have nothing else to say?" he asked hesitantly. "I think you yourself have said everything that could possibly be said." "I see."

CABARET 

While Marteau imagined himself concluding his affair with Henriette, Julie went to see Boris. She waited until four o'clock in the afternoon, for she knew his habits well. After lunch and a brief stroll, he always went to bed for an hour or more in preparation for his work at night, and usually he got up at about four o'clock and made himself some strong coffee. Julie had often shared this coffee with him. 

She would, she thought, do the same this afternoon. She had her key to his room, and she would steal in on him and give him a surprise, and if what Marie had said were true, he would be glad to see her and all the unhappiness of the past weeks would be wiped out in an hour of love and understanding and forgiveness. 

For she was willing to forgive him. She knew she only wanted him to smile and hold out his arms to her. In those things she would find all her compensation for suffering. 

She walked to his room, which was on the other side of the river in a large apartment house near the rue St. Jacques. 

The house was something of a warren and was filled with students and others of the same kidney, each occupying a room. Boris's room was at the top of it, because the top rooms were cheapest. Boris would, thought Julie, soon be able to leave his eyrie and live somewhere more convenient. Their act was becoming known, and offers were coming in. 

The sunshine on that afternoon as she went through the streets seemed brighter than she had ever known it. She had plenty of time, and she was able to linger on her way and look at the shop windows. She had not wanted to do that for many days, and now she took a particular delight in it. 

She revelled in her sense of happiness. All manner of little things pleased her. She stood in front of a jeweler's and

CABARET 

255 imagined herself decked in the costly gems she saw displayed there, but she did not crave for them. She had Boris back again. That was worth all the diamonds that ever came out of Kimberley.

The paintwork of the gay barges on the Seine was more than ordinarily bright that day. She leaned over the parapet of the Pont St. Michel and admired the scene. The Boul' 

Mich' itself was a gay grand street which seemed specially full of joyful bustle and happiness as she walked its pavements. She felt as though she wanted to tell the passersby that she was going to see Boris, that she was going to love him and keep him always to herself. She wanted to stand still and laugh a high exultant laugh.

So she came at last to the side-street near the rue St. Jacques and the tall tenement building in which was Boris's room. 

She had not been there for some time, and somehow, it seemed strange to her, perhaps a little repellent. It looked so tall and grey that she shuddered as she walked through its ever-open doorway, stepping from the flooding sunshine into the dark shadow of the entrance-hall. 

There was no need to speak to the concierge. Folk were always coming and going through the doorway, the inhabitants of the place, strangers visiting them, tradesmen, all kinds of people. It was an open highway. 

Julie ascended the stairs. Her heartbeats had quickened and she felt slightly sick with excitement. The stairs were long and narrow and steep, and she paused twice for breath before she completed the ascent and found Boris's door facing her across the dim landing. 

She had arrived. Her excitement was now almost overwhelming, and her hand shook so much that she had diffi-

CABARET 

culty in putting the key into the lock. The key jammed a bit, as though it did not fit properly. She twisted hard, wondering whether, by some extraordinary chance, Boris had had a different lock fitted since her last visit.

Suddenly, and with an unusual click, the tongue of the lock shot back and she walked into the room. Its familiar interior swept up to her gaze-the big comfortable chair by the fire-grate, the table and its two dining-chairs, Boris's bookcase, and the screened bed in the far corner. 

She called: "Boris!" Her voice shook slightly.

There was no reply. He was out, she thought... but ... just past the screen by the bed, she saw a foot. She walked across. He was asleep. She reached the screen, and, looking round it, stared, clutched it so that it crashed to the floor, while above the crash of its fall rang her high wild scream as she slipped to her knees beside all that Durant had left of Boris.

Chapter XXVIII 

LONG hours had passed. That sunny afternoon, through which Death had stolen to the side of Boris Kolotsky, merged into night, and once more the façade of the Cabaret Vibart threw its flaring light across the beauty of the Champs Elysees. Through Paris the hunt was up, the police were seeking the killer of Boris. Somewhere, crept away out of sight, Julie was learning heartbreak and the meaning of utter desolation. The later newspapers screamed the news. The 

Cabaret Vibart was gay with light and with the sound of jazz, with the movement of dancing, with the sound of song.

In the sound-proof office on the ground-floor Durant, Marteau and Fish were gathered. There was a strained unrest on all of them, even on Durant, despite his efforts to display his normal calmness. They were like men who, going their ordinary ways, with the world moving normally about them, are aware of intense and awful threat. 

Fish said: "Funny-that guy, Kolotsky, getting bumped off. I didn't think anybody hated him all that." "Most unfortunate," agreed Durant. "They had a good act, those three, and their places won't be easy to fill.

Marteau was watching tensely, expectant, ready. He knew that here was the end of it all, the ultimate, unhealing breach, the quarrel which would wreck their alliance. CABARET 

record so ripe for investigation, it would probably mean the guillotine." "This is what comes of blackmailing women," said Fish, turning with a snarl on Marteau. "We've done it before," retorted Marteau; "and you've been a party to it. It's only because there's been a hitch in this affair that-" "I tell you it's wrong," said Fish, raising his voice. 

Durant strolled toward the door. "The quarrel, my dear

Marteau, is now carried into your territory and I am preserving an armed neutrality. Get on with it; and if he tries to shoot you, ring the bell for the waiter. See you later, Fish.” 

He walked out.

His departure checked the flow of hot and angry words between the two of them and they stayed looking at one another after the door had closed on him. 

Marteau spoke first. "You're not meaning all that, about what's going to happen after the place closes down tonight, are you?" "Why not?" asked Fish. "It's him or me. It's just gotta be, Marteau. Things can't go on like this." "Not like this. But they can go on. Look here, we want to have a talk about the whole affair, together, friendliwise, and round a table. I'm willing to make a sacrifice. I don't want another row. One of you'll get killed tonight if you start again." "I know," said Fish. "Durant." "No, you. My God, Fish. He's terrible when he's smooth and smiling like he is now. He's piling it up for you, and you ought to have the sense to see it. Keep clear. Get out tonight before the show ends and let me talk to him.”

CABARET 

263 "And let that grinning dude put the skids under me? You have another think. I'm sticking.” 

Marteau sighed heavily. "Then you won't listen to reason?" "All rhyme and reason's been gone out of this joint for weeks-since that damned girl blew in. I'm staying. I tell you that flat. And if Durant wants it-he can have it. That's me tonight. I'm going to watch 'em dancing."

Fish slammed out, and Marteau sat down at the desk and toyed with a pen for some time. That part of his own private plan which he had not revealed to either Fish or Durant revolved slowly in his thoughts. 

It was now or never. That night would see hell let loose in the Cabaret Vibart, and perhaps the whole conspiracy would be irreparably wrecked. So it was every man for himself, and he, Marteau, must see that his end of the business was secure.

His mind was made up in those few seconds. All the hesitance he had known in regard to his secret scheme was now dispersed. The scheme should go forward-on the minute. Ere morning it should be an accomplished fact, and if death stepped into the Cabaret Vibart, it would not matter to him.

There was one person to whom he gave passing thought during these moments of immense decision-Emile Vibart, shut up in his prison in the top of the building. Vibart would have to be dealt with. Suppose Durant murdered Fish, or 

Fish murdered Durant . . . that night . . . and fled? Could it not be made to seem that the murderer had also murdered old Vibart?

It was an idea, and Marteau kept it with him as he walked

CABARET 

out of the office and locked the door after him. Most certainly an idea worth treasuring.

There was a person to whom Marteau did not devote one moment's thought: Madden ... who had made up his mind to raid the Cabaret Vibart that night after it had closed down.

Chapter XXIX 

MARIE reached the Cabaret Vibart in a taxicab, whose route she did not follow, of whose traveling she was unaware; for on her head and her heart rested a weight which seemed as though it would never lift.

She carried with her a confused memory of Franord, the manager of the Theatre Fantastique, talking of death, of murder in a room lit by sunshine, of love that was wrecked beyond repair, of dreams that were shattered, never to be dreamed again. She had not seen a newspaper before she arrived at the theatre, and Weber had told her of Boris's death, of Julie's despair.

Her first impulse had been to go to Madden, whose letter she carried, and whose address she treasured in her memory; but Weber had told her to go down to the Cabaret Vibart and see if Julie might now be there. Now, if ever, Julie needed a friend, Weber had suggested, and Marie could be that friend.

The incredibility of death stayed with her as she rode in the taxi. Boris was dead; Boris who had loved her and made love to her, whose smile had always pleased her, who had helped her, cheered her; he was dead, with all his great hopes and his faith in himself, his ambitions; dead-and Julie's stricken love was like a flower that is trodden underfoot.

The taxi had pulled up. A colored glare struck through its windows. Marie sat huddled in her corner until the driver 265

CABARET 

pulled open the door and announced that she had arrived. 

She stumbled out of the vehicle and thrust a ten-franc note into the driver's hand. He began to fumble in his pocket, but she did not wait. She had walked away from him and was looking up at the Cabaret Vibart. 

Lights... and more lights . . . writhing neon, flaring clusters of bulbs, a sweeping, challenging radiance that mocked the distant stars with an evil leer; clamor within, heat and perfume, and a man had died as a man had died on the night when she first stepped within its luxurious walls. 

She shuddered. The taxi was sliding off. She took no notice of it. She did not even remember having paid the driver. She stepped forward, ignoring the friendly greeting of the doorkeeper, her face pale and set, her eyes fixed ahead of her. She shuddered as she trod the steps. She knew now that she hated the Cabaret Vibart as she might have hated some living creature which was inexpressibly vile. It held a black soul, she thought. It was not a thing of stone and steel and wood, but a pulsing living entity that jeered at the dark fates of those who passed often within its portals. 

She sought Julie in the dressing-room, but she was not there, and Marie went onto the dance-floor. The time was now after midnight-nearly one o'clock-for she had been at the theatre for a long time, while Weber told her the ghastly story and comforted her. In fact, he would not permit her to leave until he was assured that she was recovering from the shock his news had given her. She would always be grateful to Weber.

Julie was not in the dance-room. At least, Marie could not see her. Marie went and sat at a table. A man came up and spoke to her. She did not hear him. He started to talk again,

CABARET 

267 and then stooped and looked at her face closely. He walked on. 

A waiter came across to her. He said: "This is a terrible thing, Ma'm'selle." "Eh?" She started and looked up. "Hello, Jacques." 

The waiter's eyes were kind. "Why don't you go home? 

Better than sitting here." 

Marie shook her head. "I'm waiting for Julie. She's sure to come, and she'll need me." Marie paused. “But perhaps she's at home-waiting for me." 

Jacques shrugged his shoulders. “No. She's not; and you must not go to that dark lonely room of hers by yourself. 

Jules sent round to see if she were there, and to see if any of us could do anything. But she hasn't been back since . . . since... 99 "I see.” Marie sat very still. There were kindnesses to be found, even in the Cabaret Vibart. It had been good of the chef d'orchestre to send round to see if anything could be done for Julie. She wondered where Julie was. The girl would certainly come to the Cabaret Vibart. Marie felt sure of it. "And you'll stay?" She heard the question as from a distance, and she nodded. 

The waiter walked away. A moment or two later he returned and placed a glass of champagne on the table. "Drink this," he said quietly. "It's from one of the bottles of that party over there. I poured it out quickly. They won't miss it, and it'll do you good." 

Marie thanked him and sipped the wine. It braced her a little. She sat on, amid the crash of the band, the ceaseless movement, the wreathing cigarette and cigar smoke, the

CABARET 

talk, the dancing, the clamor and the lights; she did not move and she did not see Julie. And the time slid by.

Later, Durant walked past the table. She looked up and met his eyes, and she realized, as she did so, that he wished to avoid stopping to speak to her. Now, facing her, he had no other alternative and, after a moment of hesitation, he dropped into a chair opposite her. "I've been busy," he said. "I didn't know you were here."

There was something strained about him, and she was quick to perceive it. It was emphasized by his entirely unnecessary explanation. 

She said: "I don't know why I should be here . . . now; except that I want to see Julie." She added: "Who could have killed him?" There was no need to mention Boris's name. 

Durant's shoulders moved ever so slightly. “Your heart is breaking, eh?" His voice was ungentle. ... "Breaking ?” She stared at him. "I am terribly upset, if that is what you mean." 

It was his turn to stare. "But at lunch time you told me you loved him." "I told you that?" Something seemed to be turning over inside Marie. "I didn't. I told you I loved somebody. It wasn't Boris. I didn't mean Boris." “No. ?" Durant leaned forward, and there was something awful in his face. "It was a mistake, then?" His voice was terribly breathless. “Dieu! I misunderstood you!”

Marie was not speaking now. She was trying hard not to think. There was that about Durant . . . and his words ... his strained talk of a mistake. ... 

Durant-making love to her that lunch time which seemed so many years ago; Durant suggesting that she loved

CABARET 

269 somebody else, and hearing that this was true; Durant, who rushed her back to Paris immediately, and left her; Durant, who knew, from long association, Boris's address and his habits; Durant into whose eyes her own were now looking with uncanny understanding and awful realization.

She got up. Her hands moved as if to ward off something evil and terrible. 99 

She breathed: "No.. no But she did not say it as though she denied all her thoughts but as though she protested against his presence, his smile, his nearness to her. 

And she turned and began to run. She jostled people, she reeled in her running as she came into contact with men and women. She scrambled through to the outer hall, and she stumbled down the steps, turning away from the lights, running blindly as though death followed her; running. ...

And Marteau, who had sat outside in his car in the shadow of the trees for the last hour . . . waiting . . . started the machine forward on the last movement in his own great secret plan.

Nobody followed Marie from the Cabaret Vibart. Even 

Durant had been stricken to temporary immobility by the horror in her face and the understanding in her eyes.

She went on down the Champs Elysees, and she did not heed the car that purred at her heels. She turned off into one of the quiet avenues, still running, breathless, exhausted, and the car slid forward alongside her. 

Marteau leaned out. "I've been waiting for you," he said, and dragged her in beside him.

She fought, but she was helpless, exhausted by her run, her brain reeling under the succession of shocks the night had brought to her, and by the time Marteau had swung several

CABARET 

corners, had pulled up in a dark little tree-lined road, she was almost fainting. "You are, I observe," he said, “going to be sensible. That's better. I should hate to hurt you, but I shall have to do so if you decide to be troublesome."

Marie moaned. She felt her senses slipping. She managed to ask him what he intended to do, why he was acting in this mad fashion.

Marteau explained. His manner was a little feverish. The sense of crisis had infected him, and he was playing on the top note, strained to breaking point. "I'm taking you to an apartment of mine," he said. “And 

I'm keeping you there all night." He paused, and added: "In the morning... you may like to consider your position in relation to me and . . . what will have happened. Such consideration might influence any decision you will make.” She tried to get out of the car, and he struck her on the side of the face so that she fell back into a corner of the seat, half senseless from the blow, inert. The car roared forward, snaked away from the little turning in which it had been halted, and, after a minute or two, stopped in a steep sidestreet, off the Avenue Niel.

Marteau opened the door of the car and picked her up. One of his hands was across her mouth. She was conscious, in a vague fashion, of Marteau's arms about her, but she could not struggle against him. She felt as though she had run her race, and that nothing on earth mattered. 

Marteau was hurrying through a narrow and deserted entrance-hall and bounding up a flight of carpeted stairs. He passed one landing, going strongly, for he was a powerful man. The second landing, like the one below it, was lighted

CABARET 

271 by a dim lamp in its ceiling, and its bare walls, the empty lift shaft, emphasized a loneliness that swept over Marie and engulfed her. 

Marteau put her on her feet. "Stand still," he ordered. “I shall be merciless if you try to escape."

She swayed slightly, and she tried to cry out, but only a hoarse little croak escaped her lips. Her head was aching, and her face was bruised where Marteau's hand had struck it.

He was fitting the key into his lock, and he turned the key and flung the door wide. Beyond, the hall of the flat was dark, so that Marteau was plainly visible from that darkness as he stood in the light of the landing. He was about to turn toward Marie when Marie saw him lean forward slightly and heard a name pass his lips. "Henriette!"

A woman laughed. She laughed from out of the darkness of the flat, and on the heels of her laugh two shots thundered. 

Marie saw Marteau's body shake horribly. He uttered no cry, but fell forward, through the doorway, on his face; and the woman who had laughed now screamed, high and long, and cried his name, and bent over him . . . who was dead. The gun dropped from her nerveless fingers.

Marie was running down the stairs to the street. She reached the door and was aware that behind her, on the staircase, was an outcry and the clamor of startled people. She pulled the door open. As she ran from the side-street into the Avenue, she saw the Arc de Triomphe, and knew she was not far from the Place des Ternes and Madden's address, Madden's home in Paris. In five minutes she was hammering on a door, crying: "Let me in! Let me in!"

Chapter XXX 

THE Cabaret Vibart was dark and dead, except for the little sound-proof office on its ground floor. There Durant and 

Fish were meeting. Everybody save those two had gone. The great façade was dingy and black. The place slept. In its silence Madden walked. He had come in through a small window at the rear. He had taken the precaution of wearing light rubber-soled shoes, and his movements in the place were soundless and secret. 

He groped his way to the big dance-room. It stretched around him and lifted above him like a cavern, the walls of which were hidden by far shadows wherein might lurk all nameless things. He thought he was alone in the building and he used an electric torch freely. This torch showed him one of the small private doors, and through this he passed to the encircling corridor, and so came to that flight of stairs down which Constantin Delorme had dropped to his death on the night of Madden's first entry.

He decided to mount to the top story and work downward. 

He knew he had a long job before him, but he was quite cool and poised and was determined to carry it out thoroughly. 

He began to climb the stairs.

He kept his torch burning, and it cast an ellipse of light before him on the stairs, an ellipse which moved slowly upward and into which his feet trod, one after the other, while all around was blackness. 272

CABARET 

273 He reached the top and looked round. There were rooms ... and rooms. . . closed mute doors that confronted him blankly as his light flicked across them and passed on. He started methodically at one end of the passage and opened door after door. The rooms were all empty. What their purpose was Madden did not know. He only knew that the opening of each door disclosed an unfurnished bare chamber wherein it was most unlikely that the necklace would be kept. So he came to the last door in the passage. 

This, to his surprise, was heavily bolted on the outside. 

He drew back the bolt carefully, but he made some noise in doing so. He pushed the door open, and walked into the prison of Emile Vibart. A big desk and some chairs were the only furnishings of the room he entered. The desk was strewn with papers; the drawers were unlocked, but there was nothing in them. Madden saw another door in a corner of the room, opened it, and looked into a small bed-chamber. 

A man was lying on the bed. 

The noise of the drawn bolt had awakened Vibart, but he was cunning, and lay still and pretended to sleep while he watched Madden. No doubt, he thought, Marteau or Durant or Fish had sent this man. Some new deviltry was afoot. Better lie still and wait. Madden came over to him and shone the light down on his face. Vibart did not move, and Madden, seeing an old man who, unless he were armed, he need not fear, did not touch him. But he kept glancing at Vibart as he searched the room, to be sure that the man really was asleep; and all the time Vibart lay very still and breathed the long deep breaths of a sleeper.

The silent search went on. Madden ransacked the dressingtable and a chest of drawers-in vain. He subjected all the

CABARET 

room to a hasty but close scrutiny, but did not find the necklace. He went back and looked down at Vibart. The old man lay in the bed and breathed quite naturally, and Madden decided he was sound asleep. He slipped out into the passage and descended the stairs.

Vibart got up. He went into the outer room and found that his visitor had left open the door that gave access to the corridor-the door whose heavy bolt had always clanged home when his tormentors had left him. He was shaking now, and his confused brain was working thunderously. He had clung to the remnants of his sanity all through his dreadful ordeal, but now that he saw that open door, now that the gates of the wide world were flung back for him, that brain toppled dangerously and whirled and crashed and blazed, as though an explosion had taken place inside it. 

He stole out into the corridor. Where the stranger had gone he neither knew nor cared. He was in his own place, the place he had created, and he could walk about it and look at it, and switch its lights on, and do as he liked. The cloak of his imprisonment had fallen from him. The exaltation that thudded in his brain had taken to itself the semblance of a mighty hammer crashing at a gong. He had an impulse to leap and shriek with laughter, to jump about and cry out, to fling his arms wide and tell the world that he was free. 

But... caution. They might be waiting for him. This might be a ruse to lure him to destruction. He had to be clever-he, whose last shreds of cleverness were being snatched from him by the mightiness of his moment of freedom, by the incredibility of that for which he had prayed all through the years of his captivity. 

Caution.... They might be near. And there was his girl

CABARET 

275 somewhere . . . here. . . . here. . . . His girl, Louise born again—his girl and hers, whom he wanted to nurture carefully, rear in sweetness .. she was here in this hell he had helped to build, the hell he had made for himself on earth. He wandered about the top story for a long time and he turned on all the lights in the passageway and stood and looked at them. He bolted the door of his prison very carefully, as if he would shut in the life he had led there, so that it could trouble him no more. And now he was rubbing his hands and chuckling and thinking how grand everything was, and talking . . . and talking. . . . 

The hammer still smashed at the gong in his brainclanged and smashed with a clamor that deafened him. He crept down the stairs. . . down . . . down . . . past the story below. On the landing below that he checked again and turned up more lights and went into more rooms. He kept turning up lights. It was lovely to turn lights on, to be surrounded by a blaze of glory that matched the flare in his head, that danced before his eyes to the rhythm of the great gong in his head. 

Lights and lights . . . and crash and throb. . . and

Vibart moving ever so quietly, for he trod in his bare feet and was careful and cunning. And here was another room, with something he knew in it. He stood and looked at it. His desk. His own old desk, the desk he had had fitted with the cunning little secret drawers in the feet of its pedestals. The desk had been ransacked. He could see that. The stranger who had come into his prison, and who had turned out the drawers as though he looked for something, had been here before him, had gone through all these rooms while he was wandering about on

CABARET 

the top story for heaven knew how long, turning up lights and admiring them, hugging himself, talking. . . .

His desk-all upset, the old desk he had used eternities ago, and round which had sat, with him, Durant, Marteau and Fish when they planned the greater Cabaret Vibart which had set its heel on him and crushed him, which had caught and enslaved his girl.

He walked to the desk and began carefully to replace the drawers Madden had pulled from it. They were all empty, whereas really, they should have held very important papers, very important papers indeed; all the papers relating to the Cabaret. 

Perhaps that fellow had taken them. Eh? There was an idea for 

Taken his papers. ... you.

Vibart sat down on the old chair which was behind the desk. He could not appreciate that the rest of the room was bare, that the desk and the chair had been stored in it for years. He sat and he looked round. He felt enthroned. He was back in his own place. 

And thus sitting he ponderously considered affairs. It seemed plain to him that the Cabaret Vibart was deserted save for the mysterious man who had come into his prison who had done him no harm, and had actually effected his release. He was grateful to that mysterious man, most grateful. He would like the opportunity of telling him so; but that must pass. 

Meanwhile... the Cabaret Vibart.

In the flame-lit, feverish darkness of his brain a thought ran and twisted and made itself ever more distinct.

This place had clutched hold of him and of his daughter.

CABARET 

277 

It had slain him, mind and soul; it would slay her. Marteau had made that horribly plain. Marie Delorme they called her. 

She was a dance-partner here. Vibart knew what that meant. 

She was any man's toy. The Cabaret Vibart had done that to her-Vibart's Cabaret. Vibart felt in his pocket and produced something at which he looked freely. It was a box of matches -nearly full.

He held one of the matches between his fingers and suddenly he saw an enormous jest. This splinter of wood was the father of the great destroyer, the great cleanser. . . .

His wavering brain flicked away from contemplation of the match. This desk of his-ransacked. . . . But there were those secret drawers . . . right down in the feet of the pedestals. Surely the prying fingers of the mysterious stranger had not found those. And in them, surely, would be kept his most important papers, as he had been wont to keep them years ago.

He opened them one after the other, and in the second one he found a great diamond necklace, coiled like a gleaming snake upon a little packet of letters in a woman's handwriting.

He picked them up and looked at them-at the letters and the necklace. Letters from a woman. Diamonds for a woman's throat. A signature danced across his vision. "Alice" ... 

The necklace for his girl; freedom for his girl; and his own last atonement. He held the necklace in his hand.

It had no value for him. Its beauty passed him by, but his girl would like it. He clutched the little box he had taken from his pocket and he ran to the bedrooms downstairs. Room

CABARET 

after room... sheets, clothing, curtains, rugs . . . he dragged inflammable stuff from beds and windows and floors, and piled it anywhere . . . everywhere. 

And now he was shouting, crying aloud, the necklace flashing from his fingers; and as he shouted, as he ran here and there, he lit fires-fires that grew and blazed and devoured, while he jumped around and shrieked with laughter, and urged the flames on, as they leaped along the corridors and the stairs, as they fastened on the Cabaret Vibart a burning grip which was relentless and never to be shaken off.

Chapter XXXI 

MADDEN, far away on the ground floor, at the end of one of the passageways, still prosecuting his search, smelled fire and smoke and began to run. His run carried him through a door into the great dance-room, and as he came into the room part of its ceiling crashed, bringing a burning avalanche on the polished floor. Flames licked the walls. The Cabaret Vibart was being cleansed to extinction. 

Madden stood for a moment or two staring round him. He had no need of his torch now. There was fire at one of the side doors, fire blazing along the floor, a roaring inferno overhead, crashing walls filled the place with sound, and above the noise of destruction was the high mad voice of the fire itself, roaring with evil joy.

A long stretch of plaster and wick-work buckled and went down in flames. Madden was about to bolt for the exit when, beyond the wall that had just fallen, a door flew open, and he saw Fish and Durant. Their sound-proof chamber had kept from them all the clamor, and they came suddenly into dust and smoke and blaze. 

Fish was reeling, his face ghastly white, his hand to his chest; and through his fingers stood the black hilt of a knife. 

Durant began to run. If he saw Madden he did not heed him. He streaked across the floor, making for the entrancehall. As he ran Fish's right hand lifted and flame stabbed from it. Durant staggered, recovered, stumbled on. Fish tried 279

CABARET 

to fire again, but his finger never pressed the trigger. He fell forward on his face and stayed there while the conflagration surged closer to him.

Durant had nearly reached the door when another long stretch of the ceiling buckled, spurted flame, and crashed. 

Madden saw him throw up his arms as though he would defend himself; and that was the last he ever saw of Durant. 

Now he must get out. His position was perilous. The rest of the ceiling might collapse at any moment as the overhead flames bit into its beams. He was cool even then, and realized that the portions of the ceiling nearest the wall must stand the longest; and so he ran in a wide circle, scrambling up onto the dais to do so.

As he ran, one of the private doors opened and Emile Vibart reeled into his path. Vibart was hurt. A falling beam had struck him; there was a wound across his head and his left shoulder hung in ugly fashion. His right hand still mechanically clutched the necklace. Alice Rollerton's letters were gone. They had been tossed onto the flames when 

Vibart started the fire. 

He saw Madden and he tried to approach him, but fell headlong.

Madden stooped over him and tried to pick him up, but as he did so Vibart's face changed. Madden had seen the faces of many men change thus in the war, and he knew he could not save Vibart.

Vibart's lips moved and Madden bent close. The fire was terribly near. The thunder of the crashing structure filled the air. Death stood across Madden as he stayed to listen to the last words Vibart ever spoke. "Take this necklace to my girl, Marie. You know her.

CABARET 

281 Everybody here knows her. Marie Delorme. My daughter. 

They've kept her here. I know. They got her from London. 

They can't deceive me. Tell her ... her father is dead. They had me up there-locked up for years. And they tortured me. Don't let her know that there ever was Emile Vibart. 

Never. See? Just this necklace for her, and no word about the Cabaret Vibart. . . . I made it. I've destroyed it. . . ." 

His head fell back.

Madden had the necklace and he ran. The main way out was now barred to him. He bolted along the narrow corridor, and he fought through smoke until he reached an outer wall and a window. He smashed the window and climbed through it to a side-street and the stars. ...

And in that little street there were no people. The great crowd was all in front . . . watching the Cabaret Vibart go down in flames that would have dimmed the flare of its façade, that dimmed them, in fact, for ever. 

Madden hurried through the streets to his home. He had the necklace in his pocket, and he knew his sister's letters must be altogether destroyed. Also he understood just as much as ever he wished to understand about Marie and the mystery of her presence at the Cabaret Vibart. 

The old man had obviously been a prisoner, the owner of the place, subject to the tyranny of Durant, Marteau and

Fish. Marie was his heir. That's why so many queer things had happened to her. She needn't know. Madden reached home and shut these things from his thoughts. 

His man was waiting for him. "There's a lady, sir . . ." he began, when Madden heard 

Marie's voice from his sitting-room.

CABARET 

"Peter. Is that you, Peter?"

He ran to where she waited for him, while his man discreetly effaced himself; and she came into his arms and clung to him, sobbing, trying to tell him all that had happened, until he calmed her and made her sit down. "I want to show you something and tell you something," he said when she was quieter. ... “First . . . this. I've got it back." He showed her the necklace and checked the words she would have spoken. "And now to tell you. Tonight the Cabaret Vibart has ceased to exist. It has been burned to the ground. And tomorrow on the mid-day train, you are coming back to England... with me. Will you come?"

She leaned toward him and kissed him, and he held her close . . . and safe.

THE END
